The International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA), with 
headquarters in Amsterdam, is an independent, international cooperative of national 
research institutions and governmental research agencies. It conducts large-scale 
comparative studies of educational achievement and other aspects of education, with the aim 
of gaining in-depth understanding of the effects of policies and practices within and across 
systems of education.

Chapter 1:
Overview of ICCS 2022
John Ainley and Wolfram Schulz
1.1	Background
The International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) has conducted cross-
national surveys of civic and citizenship education for over fifty years (Malak-Minkiewicz & Torney-Purta, 
2021). As an extension of its previous research on this topic, it established the International Civic and 
Citizenship Education Study (ICCS) with a first data collection in 2009 (Schulz et al., 2010). ICCS 2009 
was built on previous IEA research, especially the IEA Civic Education Study of 1999 known as CIVED 
(Torney-Purta et al., 2001). ICCS 2009 expanded the assessment of civic knowledge to 80 items (there had 
been 38 items in CIVED) by using a rotated block design that provided a wider coverage of the field and 
included a cluster of items that had been used in CIVED (Schulz, et al., 2011). This was followed by a second 
implementation in 2016 (Schulz et al., 2018) and now a third implementation in 2022 (Schulz et al., 2024). 
Each cycle of ICCS collects and analyses data on students’ civic knowledge and understanding, as well as 
their attitudes, perceptions, and activities related to civics and citizenship both to measure changes over 
time and to respond to new developments of relevance for civic and citizenship education. The International 
Civic and Citizenship Education Study (ICCS) 2022 focusses on the ways in which young people are 
prepared to undertake their roles as citizens in a range of countries in the third decade of the 21st century. 
ICCS also gathers and analyzes information on education systems as well as schools, teachers, and students. 
Based on nationally representative samples of students, it also examined differences among countries in 
relation to these outcomes of civic and citizenship education, changes in these outcomes over time, and 
the relationship of these outcomes to student characteristics, school, and community contexts, as well as 
national characteristics.
ICCS 2022 is a continuation, and an extension, of previous cycles ICCS. It is intended as an exploration of 
the enduring and emerging challenges of educating young people in a world where contexts of democracy 
and civic participation continue to change at the national, regional, and global levels. ICCS 2022 extends 
the scope of previous assessments but is designed to maintain continuity with ICCS 2009 and ICCS 2016. 
It is designed to enable the measurement of enduring aspects of civic and citizenship educational contexts, 
processes, and outcomes and supporting the comparison of outcomes and contexts across 2009, 2016, and 
2022. Key variables are statistically linked to enable changes to be investigated. For example, ICCS 2022, 
as was the case for ICCS 2016, uses a rotated-block assessment design that enables the inclusion of secure 
assessment material from previous cycles to provide the basis for analyses of trends as well as the inclusion 
of new assessment material to provide relevance to contemporary curricular developments. 
1.2	New developments in ICCS 2022
Although ICCS 2022 instruments included many measures from ICCS 2009 or ICCS 2016 to collect 
data about changes over time, the survey also set out to address topics that had recently been included 
or become more prominent in civic and citizenship education, or considerably broaden the measurement 
of aspects that had already been included in previous cycles. To this end, through extensive discussions 
and deliberations with experts and national study centers we identified five focus areas for ICCS 2022 for 
inclusion in the assessment framework (see Schulz et al., 2023). Each focus area was developed in response 
to recent developments that were viewed as relevant to the area of civic and citizenship education. The 
following focus areas were identified to broaden the scope of ICCS for this cycle:
•	
Sustainability: Education for sustainable development (ESD), which is frequently considered in 
conjunction with Global Citizenship Education (GCED), had received increased attention as an 
educational area of interest in view of many demographic, environmental (including the potential 
impact of climate change), economic, and social challenges, aspects of which have been increasingly 
included in national curricula (Benavot, 2014). 

OVERVIEW OF ICCS 2022
17
•	
Engagement through digital technologies: Over the last two decades, there has been an enormous increase 
amongst young people in their daily interactions via digital communication and social media. Digital 
technologies for engagement have further opened new possibilities for mobilization, organization, and 
interaction of wider audiences and of young people (Brennan, 2018; Cho, 2020). 
•	
Diversity: As result of recent economic, demographic, and technological changes, increasing levels of 
migration with growing diversity have been observed in many countries (Sandoval- Hernández et al., 
2018; United Nations, Department of Economic and Social Affairs, Population Division, 2017) with 
implications for schools and other educational institutions (Malak-Minkiewicz & Torney-Purta, 2021) 
including the building of more multicultural and inclusive schools (Banks, 2020). 
•	
Young people’s views of the political system: Recent years have witnessed growing concerns about a global 
“democratic recession” (Diamond, 2021) connected to a surge in authoritarian government practices 
as well as electoral success of extreme political movements threatening the stability of established 
democratic systems (Boogards, 2017) in connection with growing alienation also among young voters 
(Estellés & Castellví, 2020). The recent COVID-19 pandemic has also highlighted challenges to forms 
of democratic governments in view of restrictions of personal freedom and divisions in society about 
these responses (Marzocchi, 2020). 
•	
Global Citizenship was identified as an overarching area that deserved more explicit recognition 
regarding aspects already present in ICCS or in relation to the other focus areas. GCED has received 
considerable attention in recent debates about global education targets giving growing cross-national 
interconnectedness and globalization of political, social, economic, and environmental issues (Veugelers, 
2011), but there have been inconsistent definitions across the literature (Pashby et al., 2020). 
One major change in ICCS 2022 was the introduction of a computer-based delivery for student instruments, 
as an option for countries. Eighteen participants opted for this new mode while six countries continued 
delivering ICCS student test and questionnaire material in a print-based format. One important aspect of 
introducing computer-based delivery was the development of three clusters with test item material that 
made use of interactive possibilities for measurement and were only available in a digital mode. 
It was necessary to provide a basis for investigating and adjusting mode effects through print-based data 
collections in additional representative samples. These bridging studies were conducted in all 11 countries 
that had opted for computer-based delivery and had also participated in ICCS 2016. The additional samples 
provided invaluable insights in potential mode differences for student outcomes reported in this cycle as 
well as a way of evaluating whether a mode effect adjustment was needed for civic knowledge test scores 
(see Chapter 11).
1.3	Research Questions
ICCS 2022 addressed five research questions which are elaborated more fully in the assessment framework 
(see Schulz et al., 2023):
1.	
How is civic and citizenship education implemented in participating countries? This includes the aims and 
principles of civic and citizenship education, curricular approaches, changes, and developments since 
the 2009 and 2016 cycles, and perceptions the roles of civic and citizenship education.
2.	
What is the extent and variation of students’ civic knowledge within and across participating countries? This 
includes variations in civic knowledge associated with student characteristics and background variables, 
contextual factors explaining variation in students’ civic knowledge, and changes in civic knowledge 
since the previous survey cycles.
3.	
What is the extent of students’ engagement in different spheres of society and which factors within or across 
countries are related to it? This research question is related to students’ beliefs about their capacity for 
civic participation, the extent and variation of students’ civic participation in and out of school, students’ 
expectations regarding future civic and political participation, and changes in the extent and forms of 
student engagement since the previous ICCS cycles.
4.	
What beliefs do students in participating countries hold regarding important civic issues in modern society 
and what are the factors influencing their variation? This research question concerns students’ beliefs 
about the importance of principles underlying a democratic society, students’ attitudes toward civic 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
18
institutions and society, students’ perceptions of social cohesion and diversity in the societies in which 
they live, and changes in student beliefs observed since previous ICCS cycles.
5.	
How is schooling in participating countries organized with regard to civic and citizenship education and what 
is its association with students’ learning outcomes? This research question is related to the extent to which 
schools in have participatory processes that facilitate civic engagement, the extent to which schools 
and communities interact to foster students’ civic engagement and learning, and the extent to which 
schools offer programs or activities related to civic learning. 
1.4 Assessment Framework 
The assessment framework for ICCS 2022 (Schulz, et al., 2023) provided a conceptual underpinning for the 
international instrumentation. It identified and defined those aspects of cognitive and affective-behavioral 
content that were considered important learning outcomes of civic and citizenship education, as well 
as contextual factors for students’ civic learning. It should be noted that the way students develop civic 
knowledge and understanding, as well as affective-behavioral dispositions towards civic and citizenship 
issues, depends on many factors, including those beyond the learning environment at schools (see Amnå et 
al., 2009; Neundorf et al., 2016; Pancer, 2015).
The ICCS 2022 assessment framework includes a civic knowledge framework that describes the aspects of 
students’ civic knowledge in terms of the content and the associated cognitive processes that are measured 
in the student test items. It distinguishes four content domains related to knowledge and understanding 
developed through civic and citizenship education:
I.	
Civic institutions and systems (three sub-domains): (i) State institutions, (ii) economic systems, and (iii) 
civic society.
II.	
Civic principles (five sub-domains): (i) Equity, (ii) freedom, (iii) rule of law, (iv) sustainability, and (v) 
solidarity.
III.	 Civic participation (three sub-domains): (i) Decision-making, (ii) influencing, and (iii) community 
participation.
IV.	 Civic roles and identities (three sub-domains): (i) Citizens, (ii) civic self-image, and (iii) civic 
connectedness. 
The civic knowledge framework further distinguishes between processes involved in understanding complex 
sets of factors influencing civic actions and planning for and evaluating strategic solutions and outcomes. It 
differentiates two cognitive domains:
I.	
Knowing refers to the learned civic and citizenship information that students use when engaging in the 
more complex cognitive tasks that help them make sense of their civic worlds.
II.	
Reasoning and applying refers to the ways in which students use civic and citizenship information to 
reach conclusions that are broader than the contents of any single concept and to make use of these in 
real-world contexts.
The ICCS 2022 assessment framework also includes a civic attitudes and engagement framework that outlines 
affective-behavioral constructs measured through student questionnaire items included in international 
and regional instruments (see a more detailed description in Chapter 3). These are regarded as important 
learning outcomes and have a similar standing in the process of development, analysis, and reporting as 
cognitive measures of students’ civic knowledge. Student attitudes, perceptions, and behaviors relevant to 
civic and citizenship issues are organized according to the two affective-behavioral areas attitudes (e.g., 
judgements in relation to ideas, people, objects, events, or situations) and engagement (e.g., interest in, and 
expectations of, civic engagement through civic action and future political participation).
The ICCS 2022 assessment framework also includes a contextual framework which distinguishes four 
contextual levels (wider community, schools and classrooms, home and peer environments, individuals), at 
which factors influencing learning outcomes may be located (see a more detailed description in Chapter 3). 
Further, the ICCS 2022 contextual framework distinguishes between variables that are antecedents (pre-
existing variables) and processes (variables related to civic learning). 

OVERVIEW OF ICCS 2022
19
1.5 Participating Countries, Study Design, and Implementation
Twenty-four education systems, including the national systems of 22 countries and the education systems 
of two states from another country, participated in ICCS 2022.  Most participants were from Europe with 
just two Latin American countries and one Asian country being part of ICCS 2022. Various factors, including 
the consequences of the COVID-19 pandemic, impacted country participation that was less than the initial 
interest from a broader range of countries. ICCS 2022 gathered and analyzed data from approximately 
82,000 students in their eighth year of schooling, 40,000 teachers of that grade and 3,400 school principals 
in these countries. In the nested sampling design used in ICCS 2022, the surveyed principals were the 
principals of the schools sampled for the student and teacher surveys.
Fourteen countries and one benchmarking participant that participated in ICCS 2022 had also participated 
in ICCS 2016, and 16 of the countries participated in both ICCS 2022 and ICCS 2009. Twelve countries 
participated in ICCS 2022, ICCS 2016, and ICCS 2009. Four of the ICCS 2022 countries had participated 
in ICCS 2009 but not in ICCS 2016. Four countries and one benchmarking participant participated for the 
first time in ICCS 2022.
Populations and samples
The student and teacher population definitions and sampling methods for ICCS 2022 were the same as 
those used in ICCS 2009 and 2016. The student population was defined as all students in Grade 8 (students 
approximately 14 years of age), provided that the average age of students in this grade was 13.5 years or 
above at the time of the assessment.1 The population for the ICCS teacher survey was defined as all teachers 
teaching regular school subjects to students enrolled in the country’s target grade at each sampled school.2
Stratified two-stage cluster samples were selected for the study. In the first stage of sampling, PPS 
procedures (probability proportional to size as measured by the number of students enrolled in a school) 
were used to sample schools within the participating countries or entities. In the second stage, random 
procedures were used to select intact classrooms for the student survey, and individual teachers of the 
target grade were selected at random for the teacher survey.
Instruments
Data for ICCS 2022 were collected using the following survey instruments:
•	
The international student test consisted of items measuring students’ civic knowledge and ability to 
analyze and reason on topics related to civic and citizenship education. The instrument was either 
administered on paper or using the computer-based delivery platform. While computer-based tests 
included 14 item clusters (including three clusters with computer-enhanced item material), paper-
based tests administered in countries that chose the paper mode included 11 item clusters (with items 
that were common across modes). Eight of the common item clusters were used to assess students who 
participated in the bridging study.
•	
The international student questionnaire consisted of items measuring student background variables, 
school contexts, and student perceptions and beliefs. The instrument was either administered on paper 
or using the computer-based delivery platform.
•	
Regional student questionnaires were optional for national centers and administered only in countries 
participating in European and Latin American data collections. The instruments were either 
administered on paper or using the computer-based delivery platform.
•	
The teacher questionnaire gathered information about teacher background variables and teachers’ 
perceptions of factors related to the context of civic and citizenship education in their respective 
schools. It was administered to sampled teachers teaching any subject in the target grade, while an 
additional section was targeted at teachers teaching civic-related subjects (as defined by national 
centers). Participating national centers primarily delivered this instrument using online administration, 
with an option of offering paper-based delivery.
1	 If the average age of students in Grade 8 was below 13.5 years, Grade 9 became the target population.
2	 The teacher population included only those teachers who were teaching the target grade during the testing period and who 
had been employed at school since the beginning of the school year.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
20
•	
The school questionnaire captured school characteristics and school-level variables related to civic 
and citizenship participation as provided by principals (or designates) at sampled schools. As with the 
teacher questionnaire, the school questionnaire was completed primarily online by respondents, with 
an option by the national center to administer using paper-based delivery.
•	
The national contexts survey was completed online by experts coordinated by the national centers. It was 
designed to gather data about the structure of the education systems, the status of civic and citizenship 
education in the national curricula and policy documents, and recent developments. Data obtained from 
this survey supplement published information sources about countries and their education systems to 
assist with the interpretation of the results from the student, school, and teacher questionnaires, and 
in describing national contexts for civic and citizenship education.
Common items as a basis for measuring changes over time
Of the student test items used in ICCS 2022, almost half were secure items from ICCS 2016. The inclusion 
of these items meant that student achievement in ICCS 2022, 2016, and 2009 could be reported on the 
same scale and compared across cycles. The ICCS 2022 questionnaire instruments (for students, teachers, 
schools, and national centers) also included selected sets of questions from the corresponding ICCS 2016 
instruments, thus allowing for comparisons across the cycles in these selected areas. Importantly, the ICCS 
2022 international report presents only the changes for those countries where data collections met the 
technical standards associated with sampling, instrument preparation, field operations, scoring, and data 
management during both cycles. This stipulation means that the reporting of changes over time does not 
cover all 15 countries that participated in ICCS 2022 and ICCS 2016 or all 17 countries that participated 
in both ICCS 2022 and ICCS 2009. Consequently, the number of countries included in comparisons of data 
collected by the various questions and instruments vary. 
Participation rates
Data collection started in late February 2022 in the Northern Hemisphere and ended with the data collection 
in Brazil in September 2022. While most countries met IEA sample participation requirements for the 
student survey, two countries (Brazil and Denmark) and the benchmarking participant Schleswig-Holstein 
(Germany) failed to meet these requirements. For the teacher survey, there was a much lower number of 13 
countries that met IEA sample participation requirements. It needs to be acknowledged that participation 
rates for ICCS 2022 were also affected by the COVID-19 pandemic which caused disruptions across many 
education systems. In keeping with established IEA standards, results from ICCS 2022 distinguish national 
data from surveys meeting IEA sample participation requirements from those that did not. By reporting 
data from surveys not meeting participation requirements in a different section of the reporting tables, 
readers are advised to interpret the corresponding findings with due caution regarding the possible impact 
of sample participation bias. Further, comparisons across different cycles of ICCS are only presented for 
national data where sample participation requirements for all data points.
1.6 Report Structure
This report is structured to provide comprehensive technical information about the conceptual, 
methodological, and analytical aspects of ICCS 2022. Following this overview there is a series of chapters 
that provide detailed information about ICCS 2022 and its implementation in a chronological order from 
the conceptual and instrument development at the beginning of the study to data processing, analysis, and 
reporting towards the end. It needs to be acknowledged that, given the cyclical nature of a study like ICCS, 
in many chapters there are sections that are highly similar in content to descriptions of technical aspects 
included in the technical report from the previous ICCS cycles (see Schulz et al., 2011; Schulz et al., 2018). 
This is especially the case for descriptions of technical aspects that did not change from previous cycles. 
Chapters 2, 3, 4, and 5 are concerned with the development, adaptation, and translation of instruments. 
Chapter 2 provides information about the development and properties of the ICCS 2022 cognitive test, 
while Chapter 3 describes the development and properties of the international questionnaires, including 
the student questionnaire, the teacher questionnaire, the school questionnaire, the European student 
questionnaire, and the national contexts survey. In addition, ICCS 2022 also surveyed students in the two 
Latin American countries using items that had been used previously in ICCS 2016. Chapter 4 describes 

OVERVIEW OF ICCS 2022
21
the development of the computer-based assessment systems including the adaptation of print-based items 
to computer-based formats and the development of the computer-enhanced item material that was only 
delivered by computer. Chapter 5 outlines the preparation, translation, and verification of the ICCS 2022 
study instruments.
Chapters 6 and 10 are concerned with aspects of sampling as well as weighting before and after collection. 
Chapter 6 describes the sampling design, national variations, and implementation, while Chapter 10 
documents the weighting and adjudication procedures that were used to ensure the results from ICCS 
2022 represented the defined populations in each country.
Chapters 7, 8, and 9 are concerned with survey implementation. Chapter 7 describes the field operation 
procedures and the process of preparing data files at the country level. Chapter 8 documents the quality 
control protocols and observation procedures that were used during the data collection. Chapter 9 provides 
an account of data management and the iterative building of the international databases.
Chapters 11 and 12 document the psychometric and statistical analyses used in this study. Chapter 11 
reports on the scaling procedures for the cognitive test and how responses to the items were used to 
construct scores on the civic knowledge scale. This includes the procedures for equating items from ICCS 
2022 on the previously established civic knowledge scale and for adjusting mode effects. Chapter 12 
describes the methods and procedures used to derive scales from sets of questionnaire items.
Chapter 13 describes how the study results were reported, including the types of statistical analyses that 
were used to produce the reporting tables.
Finally, the technical report also contains appendices including: 
•	
Lists of the organizations and individuals involved in ICCS 2022 (Appendix A);
•	
Characteristics of national samples (Appendix B);
•	
Descriptions of civic knowledge test items and their allocations to proficiency levels, noting that many 
items will remain secure for use in future ICCS cycles (Appendix C);
•	
A mapping of questionnaire items common to ICCS 2022 and 2016 (Appendix D); 
•	
Coding information for the items in the questionnaires and the test (Appendix E); and 
•	
Information about questionnaire scale averages and score-point differences across delivery modes 
(paper- vs. computer-mode) in 11 countries (Appendix F).

Chapter 2:
ICCS civic knowledge test development
Julian Fraillon
2.1 Overview
The International Civic and Citizenship Education Study (ICCS) civic knowledge test was developed between 
November 2017 and April 2020. The ICCS 2022 civic knowledge test development was conducted by the 
International Study Center (ISC) at the Australian Council for Educational Research (ACER) in collaboration 
with the study’s national research coordinators (NRCs), and the Project Advisory Committee (PAC).
This chapter provides a detailed description of the test-development process, review procedures, and the 
test design implemented for the ICCS 2022 field trial and main survey.

2.2 Test scope and format
ICCS Assessment framework
The cognitive test items were developed with reference to the ICCS 2022 assessment framework (Schulz et al., 
2023) and designed to measure a single trait labelled civic knowledge in the international report on ICCS 2022 
(Schulz et al., 2024). The way civic knowledge was expressed through the ICCS test items required students to 
apply the cognitive processes to the civic and citizenship content as described in the assessment framework.
Each test item developed for ICCS 2022 was mapped to both the civics and citizenship content and cognitive 
process required by students to respond correctly to the item.
The ICCS 2022 assessment framework includes four content and two cognitive domains. The four content 
domains are:
•	
Civic institutions and systems;
•	
Civic principles;
•	
Civic participation;
•	
Civic roles and identities.
The two cognitive domains are:
•	
Knowing;
•	
Reasoning and applying.
In ICCS 2022, the increased focus on global citizenship education (GCED) and education for sustainable 
development (ESD) required an increase in the number of items relative to previous cycles. Some new test 
items were developed specifically to address these two areas of increased focus. The degree to which all 
test items (both newly developed and secure trend items) represented content associated with each of 
GCED and ESD was also evaluated as part of the test development process.
Test item formats
In the two previous ICCS cycles, all students completed the tests on paper. Approximately 90 percent of 
items were four option multiple-choice (MC), with students required to select one option, and the remaining 
10 percent of items were short constructed response (CR), with students expected to respond by writing 
between a few words and a few sentences.
ICCS 2022 initiated the transition in ICCS from paper-based to computer-based assessment. Countries could 
choose whether all students in the country would complete the tests on computer or on paper. The introduction 
of computer-based test delivery to ICCS afforded the opportunity to broaden the set of item formats available 
to students. However, it was also necessary to ensure that student test data collected on computer in ICCS 
2022 were comparable to those data collected on paper in ICCS 2022, and in previous cycles of ICCS. 
The ICCS 2022 test instrument included a set of items that were common to the paper-based and computer-
based delivery modes. This included the secure trend items from previous ICCS cycles and newly developed 
items. All these items were either MC or CR as used in previous ICCS cycles. Hereafter in this chapter, MC 
and CR items are collectively referred to as conventional items.
The conventional items that were common to paper-based and computer-based delivery modes included a 
small number of decontextualized questions of knowledge or understanding (Figure 2.11) although most of 
the items were presented in units. Each unit typically included some brief contextual stimulus (an image or 
some text) followed by items relating to the context established by that stimulus (Figure 2.22).
1	 The test item shown in Figure 2.1 is presented as sample item 4 in the ICCS 2022 international report.
2	 The test items shown in Figure 2.2 are presented as sample items 5 and 9 in the ICCS 2022 international report.

What is the most likely reason a person in a democracy would join a political party?

to help the police with the enforcement of laws

to decide on sentences for people found guilty of crimes

to decide on the location of polling booths for national elections

to participate in activities that influence government policy
Figure 2.2: Example unit comprising stimulus material and corresponding test items
Fake news is a term used to describe false or misleading information that is deliberately presented as 
news.
How can the presence of fake news harm a democracy?
Give two ways.
1.
2.
What can a person do to confirm whether information in a news article is real or fake?

check whether or not the same information is presented by many different sources 

check whether or not the government agrees or disagrees with the news

check whether or not their friends believe the news article

 check whether or not the news is [trending] on social media
The test instrument also included a set of items developed for use on computer only. This included a range 
of computer-enhanced items, such as drag and drop items and sliders that dynamically adjusted the relative 
proportions of allocations in a fictitious budget. 
The items presented in the computer-based delivery mode only were presented in computer-enhanced 
clusters. These were fixed sequences of items (some computer-enhanced and some MC or CR), each with a 
form of narrative structure providing a thematic link across the items within that cluster. In total there were 
20 items distributed across three computer-enhanced clusters in the ICCS 2022 main survey computer-
based test instrument.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
28
2.3 Test development process
The cognitive test-item and instrument development process consisted of a series of stages. These stages 
followed one another sequentially. However, the iterative and collaborative nature of the overall process 
meant that some materials were reviewed and revised within particular stages more than once.
Test specifications planning
The item development process began formally with the development of draft test specifications. These 
outlined the number of items planned for use in the main survey, incorporating the available secure trend 
items from previous cycles of ICCS, and with a view to increasing the emphasis on GCED and ESD in the 
ICCS test. 
Under the planned test design for ICCS 2022, and consistent with previous cycles of ICCS, each test item 
was planned to be allocated to one cluster of items. Clusters comprising conventional items only were 
planned to include 11±1 unique items and were anticipated to take students not longer than 15 minutes to 
complete. The computer-enhanced clusters were also planned to take students not longer than 15 minutes 
to complete, but the number of items was planned to be less than in the clusters comprising conventional 
items only. This is because it was anticipated that some of the computer-enhanced items may take longer to 
complete than the conventional test items.
Each test form was planned to comprise three clusters. These were planned to be allocated using a partially 
balanced incomplete block design. Details of these allocations are included in the sections relating to the 
field trial and main survey test designs later in this chapter.
The early phase commitment to developing the computer-enhanced test items was to create themes and 
initial item plans for between six and eight computer-based clusters for consideration by stakeholders, with 
a view to reducing this set to five to be instantiated in the online test environment for inclusion in the field 
trial and main survey.

By design, the ICCS test instrument planning assumed that the ICCS 2022 test would measure similar civic 
and citizenship education content and cognitive processes to those measured in previous cycles. However, the 
ICCS 2022 test was planned to include a higher proportion of GCED/ESD items than in previous cycles, and it 
was accepted that the inclusion of the computer-enhanced clusters may also affect the relative proportions of 
ICCS test content mapped to the different content domains by delivery mode (CBA versus PBA). 
The test specifications plan was presented for consideration at the first meeting of ICCS NRCs in March 2018. 
It was further reviewed by the ICCS PAC and finalized following the second meeting of NRCs in May 2019.

ICCS CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST DEVELOPMENT
29
NRC item development workshop
An item development workshop was conducted as part of the second meeting of NRCs in May 2019 in 
Rome. At this workshop, national representatives were provided with information about the framework and 
procedures for ICCS 2022 test development, as well as the test specifications. Participants drafted ideas in 
small working groups. The workshop involved the following activities:
•	
A review of the content of the assessment framework to ensure a common understanding of the civic 
knowledge construct;
•	
A review of the test specifications and the assessment framework to guide the development of new 
items;
•	
Confirmation of the necessary properties of test-item stimuli, including issues relating to ensuring 
cultural sensitivity and avoiding potential biases (such as linguistic, cultural, or gender bias);
•	
Confirmation of item formats, scoring guide formats, and test development systems, including the 
expected features of the online test authoring and delivery system;
•	
An overview of the plans for computer-enhanced test clusters, including review of an example cluster 
with a discussion of the principles underpinning the development of the clusters;
•	
An introduction to the general principles underpinning all ICCS cognitive test-item development; and
•	
Test-item development in small groups.
At the workshop, NRCs were also provided with an open invitation to submit materials to support the 
development of ICCS 2022 test items. These could include proposed stimulus materials, topics, test items, 
or test item ideas.
Stimulus selection and preliminary item development
The focus of this preliminary stage of item development was on establishing authentic, viable, and relevant 
contexts for items to assess the content specified in the assessment framework. Stimulus materials, 
contexts, and item ideas were developed internally at the ISC. NRCs and members of the PAC submitted 
a small number of contexts and ideas, some of which had been created at the item-development workshop.
These materials were submitted to the ISC where the test-development team assessed them for their 
suitability for further development. This work included evaluating the extent to which these materials were 
appropriate for civic and citizenship testing and their suitability for the target student population. The team 
also reviewed these materials with respect to the range of contexts and themes that they covered.
Materials selected for further development were subsequently refined (as required), and the related items 
were developed by a test developer responsible for a particular unit (stimulus, items, and scoring guides). 
Once the project researchers had developed their respective units to the degree that they considered them 
to be complete, they submitted their units to a quality control process called “paneling.”
Paneling
Paneling is a team-based approach to reviewing test materials. This rigorous quality-control mechanism is 
employed during the development of test materials. Paneling is a process that recognizes the importance 
of exposing material to multiple viewpoints. During this process, a small group of test developers (between 
three and six) jointly review material that one or more of them have developed. The review leads to 
acceptance, modification, or rejection.
Panel participants compare their answers to the questions and raise issues about the questions and the 
material. A robust discussion is required to ensure that the selected items perform as intended.
The following questions provide a summary of the issues that formed the focus of the evaluation of the item 
material developed for ICCS 2022. The relevance of each evaluation issue varied according to the individual 
characteristics of the material under consideration.
Content validity
•	
How did the material relate to the ICCS 2022 test specifications?
•	
Did the questions test the content and cognitive processes described in the assessment framework?

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
30
•	
Did the questions relate to the essence of the stimulus, or did they focus on trivial side-issues?
•	
How would this material stand up to public scrutiny (including staff involved in the project as well as 
members of the wider community)?
Clarity, context, and translatability
•	
Was the material coherent, unambiguous, and clear?
•	
Was the material interesting, worthwhile, and relevant?
•	
Did the material assume prior knowledge and, if so, was this assumed to be acceptable or part of what 
the test intended to measure?
•	
Was the reading load as low as possible?
•	
Were there idioms or syntactical structures likely to prove difficult to translate into other languages?
Format
•	
Was the proposed format the most suitable for the content and process being assessed by the item?
•	
Were there any characteristics of the item format that created hindrances to test-takers’ capacity to 
access the core content of the item, and if so, how could they be corrected?
•	
Did the content of the computer-enhanced clusters make use of the computer-based delivery 
environment in a way that extended beyond what could be delivered on paper?
Test-takers
•	
Did the test-item material match the expected range of ability levels, age, and maturity of the ICCS 
target population?
•	
Did the material appear to be cross-culturally relevant and sensitive?
•	
Were items likely to be easier or harder for certain subgroups in the target population for reasons 
other than differences in the ability measured by the test?
•	
Did the constructed-response items provide clear guidance as to the expected answers to the test 
question?
Scoring
•	
Was the key (the correct answer to a multiple-choice question) irrefutably correct?
•	
Were the distractors (the incorrect options to a multiple-choice question) plausible but also irrefutably 
incorrect?
•	
Was the proposed scoring consistent with the underlying ability measured by the test and would test 
respondents with higher ability levels always score better than those with lower ones?
•	
Were there different possible student responses that might receive the same score, and did these 
responses represent equivalent or different levels of proficiency?
•	
Were there other kinds of answers that had not been anticipated in the marking guide (e.g., any that did 
not fall within the “correct” answer category description but appeared to be equally correct)?
•	
Were the scoring criteria sufficiently clear for scorers to allow them to distinguish the different levels 
of performance?
The reviews and evaluations conducted during the paneling process provided the participating test 
developers with extensive notes on each stimulus piece, item, and scoring guide (for constructed-response 
items). The item material deemed appropriate for further development was subsequently refined based on 
the panel’s feedback.
Refinement of item material
During the process of refinement, all revised materials were shown to at least one test developer who had 
not previously seen them. The purpose of this additional check was to ensure that the revision of items had 
not created additional technical problems.

ICCS CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST DEVELOPMENT
31
External review
Once all ICCS 2022 draft test material (stimulus, items, and scoring guides) had been developed, it was 
made available to PAC members and NRCs for review. Reviewers were invited to make comments on the 
items and complete a brief rating (1 to 4) of the suitability of each item for inclusion in the test. The rating 
categories were:
4.: 
  Item should be included in the test without being altered.
3.: 
  Item should be included in the test with some minor changes.
2.: 
  Item should only be included in the test if specific changes are made.
1.: 
  Item should not be included in the test.
The web-based external review took place in March 2020, after which the draft test material was further 
revised in accordance with the feedback, and in preparation for the small-scale piloting of ICCS test material.
Pilot testing
The original plan for ICCS 2022 was for pilot testing to be conducted in all participating countries. The 
impact of the COVID-19 pandemic meant that most countries were not able to participate in the pilot 
testing. Small-scale pilot testing with convenience samples of students was conducted in five countries 
(Chinese Taipei, Colombia, Norway, Spain, and Sweden). Six non-overlapping test forms were created with a 
total of 155 newly developed items representing parallel content that could be delivered on computer and 
on paper. 
Because of the reduced number of countries participating in the pilot, and the challenges within those 
countries to find schools available to participate, the computer-enhanced cluster content was not included 
in the pilot instruments. The focus of the piloting was narrowed to collect feedback on the large set of items 
that potentially were to be presented to all students in ICCS, as well as to collect information on student 
experiences of the computer-based delivery in comparison to the paper-based delivery of these common 
items. The test items were translated into the target language at national centers (at this stage without 
translation verification or adaptation review) and administered to students under test conditions.
The items were scored, and the resulting pilot data were returned to the ISC for review. There were not 
sufficiently high numbers of students to support a robust scaling analysis, but response frequencies for 
multiple-choice options and proportions of correct responses for all items were checked as part of the 
review. We also conducted Rasch IRT analyses of the pooled (from all countries) multiple-choice data for 
each of the six test forms. National centers were invited to provide qualitative feedback from post-test 
interviews with students.
Information collected from the pilot was used to inform further revision of the items and the scoring guides 
in preparation for further review by the ICCS PAC and ICCS NRCs.
Final item review and revision – post pilot
A final proposed item set, that incorporated feedback and information collected in the external item review 
and the pilot was presented to ICCS NRCs for discussion at the third NRC meeting, conducted remotely, in 
May 2020. Following the meeting, the full set of proposed items and scoring guides were made available to 
NRCs to conduct a final online review in May. Feedback from this final review was used to complete a final 
revision of the items and confirmation of the test instrument for the ICCS field trial.
2.4 Field trial test design and content
Overview
In ICCS 2022, the field trial test needed to accommodate countries completing computer-based testing and 
paper-based testing.
The test instrument included a pool of 195 items that can be described as belonging to two categories. The 
majority of items (154) were conventional items, developed in order to be administered in parallel in the 
paper-based and computer-based ICCS 2022 tests. These were items in which the stimulus, content and 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
32
formats were identical in the paper-based and computer-based tests. Twenty-two secure trend items from 
ICCS 2016 were included in this set of 154 items. 
The remaining 41 items were developed to be used within five computer-enhanced clusters. Eighteen of 
these items used formats that could not be delivered on paper. Adapted (but not identical) versions of 11 of 
the remaining 23 items were also included in the paper-based test forms. These 11 items had formats that 
were suitable for delivery on paper and did not require the broader contexts provided by the computer-
enhanced clusters to be accessible to students.  The remaining 12 items had formats that might also have 
been delivered on paper but could not be included in the paper-based tests without extensive adaptation 
to compensate for the lack of context provided by the computer-enhanced clusters. Therefore, these items 
were delivered only on computer.
Table 2.3 shows a summary of the item types in the ICCS 2022 field trial instrument. The computer-
enhanced clusters included three drag and drop items and 15 dynamic interactive items. These are items 
with a range of formats that shared the common attribute of being associated with some form of dynamic 
interactive feedback to students within the test.

Coverage of the assessment framework
The target mapping of test content to the content and cognitive domains in the ICCS assessment frameworks 
for ICCS 2009 and 2016 was as follows:
Content domains
•	
Domain 1 (civic society and systems): 40%
•	
Domain 2 (civic principles): 30%
•	
Domain 3 (civic participation): 20%
•	
Domain 4 (civic identities): 10%
Cognitive domains
•	
Domain 1 (knowing): 30%
•	
Domain 2 (reasoning and applying): 70%
The ICCS 2022 assessment framework was still under review at the time the test development was 
completed. Therefore, during the ICCS test instrument development phase, it was not confirmed whether 
the changes to the assessment framework and the introduction of the newly developed computer-enhanced 
clusters would require changes in the proportions of content by domain in the ICCS test instrument. 
Our expectation was that data collected using the ICCS 2022 test would be equated to and reported on 
the single ICCS civic knowledge scale. Some variation in the proportions of content from the four content 
domains was not anticipated to compromise this expectation, given that content from the four domains 
has historically been reported on the single achievement scale. However, we left open the possibility that 
developing a second GCED/ESD scale may be feasible. As such, all test items were also classified according 
to whether they were consistent with GCED/ESD content.
The 195 items used in the ICCS 2022 field trial, and particularly the 173 new items, provided good overall 
coverage of the content and cognitive domains in the ICCS assessment framework. The intended profile of 
the main survey test instrument had not been confirmed at the time of the field trial. However, the field trial 

ICCS CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST DEVELOPMENT
33
instrument was developed to include sufficient content coverage across the framework (including items 
associated with either or both of GCED/ESD) to accommodate a range of relative proportions of content by 
domain for in the main survey instrument (Table 2.4).

Test design
Allocation of item content to clusters
The items in the field trial test item pool were allocated to clusters. These were unique sets of items, anticipated 
to be completed by students in up to 15 minutes. Each item was allocated to one cluster only. Each test form 
comprised three clusters, and students were allocated a total of 45 minutes to complete the test.
Clusters of items that could be delivered either on paper or on computer typically included 11 conventional 
items. The computer-enhanced clusters included predetermined content based on their cluster themes and 
included between five and eight items. The computer-enhanced clusters were structurally equivalent to the 
item clusters in the test design.
The ICCS 2022 field trial included the following three testing scenarios:
•	
Computer-based delivery (used in all countries selecting the computer-based delivery option);
•	
Paper-based delivery (used in all countries selecting the paper-based delivery option);
•	
Mode-effect study (paper-based delivery to an additional sample of students in selected countries 
participating in the computer-based delivery option).
The test items were allocated to clusters in a test design comprising 14 clusters across 14 test forms in 
each of the paper-based and computer-based delivery testing scenarios. Each of clusters 3, 5, 7, 9, and 
13 had alternative versions. These comprised the computer-enhanced clusters (denoted as _V(C) in Table 
2.5) and the alternative paper-based clusters (denoted as V_(P) in Table 2.5). The test item content was 
different between each of the computer-enhanced clusters and their alternative paper-based clusters. The 
alternative clusters were included to maximize the item content that could be included in the field trial and 
to create a common design across all participating countries regardless of delivery mode. 
The content of the computer-enhanced clusters was established by the item content associated with the 
narrative theme of each cluster. The conventional item clusters were established so that the clusters were 
equivalent with respect to: item format (MCQ and CR), reading load (in English), CCE content and cognitive 
processes (as specified by the ICCS 2022 assessment framework). Further checks were completed to ensure 
that there was no interference of item content across items within test forms (each form comprised three 
clusters). Where any interference was noted as possible (such as where two items addressing similar content 
were in a test form) the cluster content was adjusted accordingly. Two clusters (C1 and C11) were constructed 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
34
using secure trend items only. The remaining clusters were constructed using newly developed items only.

Allocation of clusters to test forms (computer-based and paper-based delivery)
The computer-based and paper-based delivery modes used the same test design comprising 14 test forms 
(Table 2.6). The content differed between the two modes only where the alternative clusters (shown as 
_V in Table 2.6) would be either a computer-enhanced cluster in the computer-based delivery mode or an 
alternative conventional item cluster in the paper-based mode. 
ICCS 2022, as in previous cycles of ICCS, used a partially balanced incomplete block design. Each cluster 
(including the alternative paper or computer clusters) appeared once in each of the first, second, and third 
positions across the test forms relevant to their delivery mode. While each cluster was presented once in 
each position across the test forms (relevant to their delivery mode), each cluster did not appear an equal 
number of times with each other cluster.

Allocation of clusters to test forms (mode-effect study)
The mode-effect study was conducted in selected countries using the computer-based delivery mode. The 
purpose of the mode-effect study was to collect data using paper-based test forms that could then be used 
to provide information about the relative impact of the delivery mode (paper or computer) on estimates of 
students’ civic knowledge. 
The mode-effect study test forms were administered to an additional sample of students in participating 
countries. To minimize the additional burden of this work for countries, we used a reduced sample of field 
trial test items in the mode-effect study forms, as this would keep to a minimum the number of additional 
students needed for the study. 
The mode-effect study forms were created using a subset of the conventional item clusters used in the 
paper-based delivery mode of the field trial (Table 2.7). Each cluster appeared once in each of the first, 
second, and third positions across the test forms.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
36
Scoring guides for human scoring (field trial)
All CR items were scored by humans only. The scoring guides for the items are a critical component of the 
item, as interpretation of the guides by trained scorers generate the data for each item. Preliminary scoring 
guides were created and developed and reviewed for all CR items developed for ICCS 2022. 
The scoring guides for the CR items selected for inclusion in the field trial were further reviewed and revised. 
Draft detailed scoring guides were used as the basis for training human scorers in the field trial. This was a 
train-the-trainer model in which lead scorers from each country were trained to score the items according 
to the draft scoring guides. These lead scorers were then responsible for training scorers and supervising 
scoring operations in their own countries.
The scoring guide for each CR item consisted of:
•	
descriptions of the necessary components, content, and characteristics of the score codes;
•	
descriptions of the conceptual categories that can be considered within each level of credit;
•	
sample student responses that were indicative of each conceptual category for that item; 
•	
some annotations to example responses to explain why certain responses were allocated to a specific 
category; and
•	
some additional notes to assist trainers and scorers to interpret the intention and content of aspects of 
the scoring guide.
It was essential that the scoring guide was used consistently across countries. It was emphasized that under 
no circumstances should the agreed scores be altered during the scorer training. 
All sample responses selected for scorer training were thoroughly vetted, discussed, and cross-marked by 
the lead scorers.
The selection and discussion of sample responses was a pivotal part of scorer training. This exercise helped 
scorers become more familiar with the scoring criteria being applied to the responses and helped clarify 
any areas of misunderstanding or concern. Sample responses are important in helping scorers calibrate and 
refine their judgments and gain confidence in their ability to mark effectively. 
In summary, for scorer training, sample responses were used to: 
•	
teach scorers how to apply the scoring guide correctly; 
•	
help scorers calibrate their marking in preparation for ‘live’ responses; and 
•	
give scorers confidence in their judgments so that they can mark efficiently.
Sample responses were intended to give scorers information about the sorts of responses they can expect 
to encounter during the scoring operation. Consequently, it was expected that the sets of sample responses 
selected would showcase variety and breadth in: 
•	
scores assigned; 
•	
content;
•	
styles of response; and
•	
interpretations of items (and stimulus).
The draft scoring guides were reviewed and refined following the field trial scorer training meeting. This 
was done to remove any ambiguities or unclear information in the guides that was revealed in discussions 
at the training.
Lead scorers were encouraged to contact the ISC with any questions they had during the field trial scoring. 
Relevant questions and ISC responses were published online for the information of lead scorers across all 
countries.

ICCS CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST DEVELOPMENT
37
2.5 Main survey test design and content
Selection of items for inclusion in the main survey
Selecting items for possible inclusion in the main survey test instrument
We evaluated the measurement properties of the field trial test items based on the data collected among 
students in all participating countries. The analysis procedures used to review the items’ measurement 
properties are described in Chapter 11. We reviewed those items with unsatisfactory measurement 
properties further to determine whether they could be revised or had to be deleted from the item set. We 
made minor revisions to a small number of items. Items were only modified when there was clear evidence 
that the revision would improve their measurement properties.
In total, 25 items were deemed to be unsuitable for inclusion in the main survey instrument. These included 
one entire computer-enhanced cluster.
The remaining items were considered as viable for inclusion in the main survey instrument. These findings 
were further discussed with the ICCS PAC, and subsequently at the fourth meeting of NRCs.
Following these meetings and in response to further review comments, a final pool of items was established 
from which the content for the ICCS 2022 main survey test instrument could be selected. The final pool 
included three of the five computer-enhanced clusters that had been included in the field trial, and 137 new 
conventional items.
Selection of item content and allocation of item content to clusters
The initial ICCS 2022 test development specifications (Table 2.2) indicated that the main survey test 
instrument would comprise 12–13 clusters, including 3–4 computer enhanced clusters and up to 55 trend 
items. Following the review of the field trial items, we decided to include 55 secure trend items (equivalent 
to five 11-item clusters), the three available computer-enhanced clusters (comprising 20 items in total) and 
a further 66 newly developed test items (selected from the 137 available). This corresponded to a total of 
11 clusters of conventional test items to be delivered across 11 test forms in countries completing the tests 
on paper. In countries completing the tests on computer, the three computer-enhanced clusters would be 
delivered together with the 11 clusters of conventional test items across 14 test forms. 
The decision to include 55 trend items was made to maximize the availability of trend item content to 
support equating the ICCS 2022 test data to the ICCS civic knowledge scale. The decision to include 66 
new conventional items was made to keep the overall test design as close as possible to that established in 
the test development specifications and planned for by countries. Having a pool of 137 items from which 66 
were to be selected offered considerable flexibility when selecting items to ensure that the clusters were 
well balanced while still offering a broad range of content coverage.
The contents of the 11 conventional item clusters were selected using similar principles that applied for 
the field trial. The items were selected such that the clusters were balanced with respect to: item format 
(MCQ and CR), reading load (in English), and CCE content and cognitive processes (as specified by the ICCS 
2022 assessment framework). Furthermore, the items were selected to ensure that a range of contexts was 
presented across the instrument. Unlike the field trial, the secure trend items were distributed relatively 
evenly across the 11 clusters rather being included as two discrete trend item clusters. Further checks were 
also completed to ensure that there was no interference of item content across items within test forms.
The contents of the three computer-enhanced clusters were predetermined within each cluster to ensure a 
good balance across cognitive and content dimensions.
Main survey instrument composition and framework mapping
The main survey test instrument consisted of 141 items. There were 121 conventional items (55 secure 
trend and 66 new) and 20 items from the computer-enhanced clusters.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
38
The relative proportions of item content coverage by content sub-domains (Table 2.9) was similar to but 
varied slightly from those reported for ICCS 2016. In comparison with ICCS 2016, the ICCS 2022 test 
instrument contained a lower proportion of items associated with content domain 1 and a higher proportion 
of items associated with content domains 2 and 3. These differences can be attributed to:
1.	
The reallocation of selected content from content domain 1 to content domain 4 in the ICCS 2022 
assessment framework; 
2.	
The addition of the sustainability sub-domain under content domain 2, and a consequent increase in 
the number of items associated with content domain 2 as a result of the increased number of items 
addressing ESD in 2022; and
3.	
An increase in the number of items associated with content domain 3 in 2022 resulting from the inclusion 
of the three computer-based delivery only clusters with themes that focused on civic participation.
Across both previous ICCS cycles, civic knowledge, as described across the four content domains in the 
relevant assessment frameworks, was reported as a single measurement dimension. The slight variation in 
the distribution of test content across the four domains in ICCS 2022 was not anticipated to prevent ICCS 
2022 test data from being equated to and reported on the single ICCS civic knowledge scale. However, 
in the ICCS 2022 test instrument, there were sufficient GCED and ESD items (Table 2.9) to evaluate 
empirically the justification or need to report GCED and or ESD as separate measurement dimensions. 
Based on evaluation of the dimensionality within the ICCS 2022 test data, the decision was made to report 
civic knowledge as a single scale in ICCS 2022 (see Chapter 11 for full details of these analyses).

QUESTIONNAIRE DEVELOPMENT
43
Chapter 3:
Questionnaire Development
Gabriella Agrusti, Valeria Damiani, Tim Friedman, and Wolfram Schulz
3.1 Introduction
This chapter describes the development of the questionnaires for students, teachers, schools, and national 
research centers used in ICCS 2022. While there has been a growing recognition of the importance of 
questionnaires as crucial parts across international studies of education, for studies of civic and citizenship 
education these instruments have always played a particularly important role in the measurement of 
affective-behavioral data as learning outcomes and the factors related to the varying contexts for civic 
and citizenship education across participating countries. The development followed elaborate procedures 
to appropriately consider the diversity of education systems involved in a study of this type (see Schulz & 
Carstens, 2020). 
The student questionnaire was designed to measure contextual information (i.e., student characteristics 
such as age, gender and expected education, information about the students’ home and their parents or 
guardians), as well as aspects related to students’ attitudes and engagement. The teacher questionnaire 
was designed to gather teacher perspectives on the general school and community environment, teaching 
methods and civic and citizenship education. School principals were asked to report on the school context 
for learning, on school characteristics, climate, and aspects of civic and citizenship education at their schools. 
An online questionnaire for national research coordinators (NRCs), the national contexts survey, was 
designed to collect contextual information at the national (or sub-regional) level about the characteristics of 
education systems, aims, contexts, and implementation of civic and citizenship education, as well as current 
developments (reforms, debates) related to this learning area.
As in the previous cycle, ICCS 2022 also administered regional questionnaires for countries from the 
European and Latin American regions. These questionnaires were developed in close cooperation with 
participating countries from each region and designed to address region-specific topics not covered in the 
international instruments. While at the beginning of the cycle, five or more countries from the Latin American 
region were expected to join the study, only two of these countries (Brazil and Colombia) implemented 
the final main survey data collection. Therefore, the Latin American student questionnaire used in the 
main survey only included item material from the previous cycle except for one question about students’ 
expectations for their individual future adapted from the European regional questionnaire in ICCS 2016.
3.2 Conceptual framework for questionnaire development
The assessment framework provided a conceptual underpinning for the development of the international 
instrumentation for ICCS 2022 (Schulz et al., 2023). It included the civic attitudes and engagement framework 
as well as the contextual framework and both served as reference documents that guided the development of 
ICCS 2022 instruments (international and European student questionnaires, teacher questionnaire, school 
questionnaire, national contexts survey), which we will describe in this chapter.
The civic attitudes and engagement framework outlines affective-behavioral aspects that were measured with 
student questionnaire items and included in international and regional instruments. These are regarded 
as important learning outcomes and had a similar standing in the process of development, analysis, and 
reporting as cognitive measures of students’ civic knowledge. Student attitudes, perceptions, and behaviors 
relevant to civic and citizenship issues are organized according to the following two affective-behavioral 
areas:
•	
Attitudes (e.g., judgements in relation to ideas, people, objects, events, or situations) related to the 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
44
following three subareas: (i) Civic principles, (ii) civic issues and institutions, and (iii) civic roles and 
identities.
•	
Engagement (e.g., interest in, and expectations of, civic engagement through civic action and future 
political participation) related to the following three subareas: (i) Experiences with engagement, (ii) 
dispositions toward engagement, and (iii) expected future engagement. 
As in previous ICCS frameworks, the contextual framework for ICCS 2022 distinguishes the following four 
levels at which factors influencing civic learning outcomes may be located: 
•	
The context of the wider community is multi-layered and comprises the broader context within which 
schools and home environments operate (ranging from local to global levels) with relevant factors 
found at local, regional, and national levels, also considering the importance of the supra-national level 
(e.g., for data collected within the European Union). 
•	
The context of schools and classrooms comprises factors related to teaching and learning, the school 
culture, and the general school environment.1
•	
The context of home and peer environments comprises factors related to the home background and the 
immediate social out-of-school environment of the student (e.g., peer-group activities).
•	
The context of the individual refers to the individual characteristics of the student (e.g., their gender or 
educational aspirations).
Further, the ICCS 2022 contextual framework distinguishes the following types of variables: 
•	
Antecedents are defined as pre-existing variables that shape how student learning and acquisition of 
civic-related understandings and perceptions takes place. 
•	
Processes are defined as those variables that are related to civic-related learning and they are 
constrained or enabled by antecedents. 
Figure 3.1: Contexts for the development of learning outcomes related to civic and citizenship education in ICCS 2022

45
Figure 3.1 illustrates how these two types of contextual variables and civic learning outcomes are 
interrelated at each of the four levels. It is important to acknowledge that there may also be reciprocal 
associations between processes and outcomes variables, for example, civic learning processes may be 
positively influenced by already higher levels of civic knowledge among students in a given classroom. While 
the model assumes unidirectional relationships between antecedents and process-related variables, it is 
also possible that, from a long-term perspective, there could be effects from outcomes on antecedents (for 
example, through the provision of increased educational resources to schools with lower learning progress). 
Different instruments administered as part of ICCS 2022 provided data on different types of variables 
located at varying levels. Table 3.1 shows examples of variables collected by ICCS 2022 instruments that 
were administered to students, teachers, schools, and national experts. The national contexts survey and 
data from published sources provided information at the level of the wider community, while also teacher and 
school data contributed to the description of national contexts. Teacher and school questionnaires are the 
primary sources for collecting data about the school and classroom contexts, but the student questionnaire 
also provided relevant information from the perspective of learners (e.g., regarding the school climate). The 
latter instrument, apart from measuring civic attitudes and engagement, was the main information source 
about student background, home, and peer contexts.
