3.3 Student questionnaire development
The development of the international student questionnaire was conducted in three phases:
•	
The first phase included discussions about retaining or modifying ICCS 2016 questionnaire items 
(including items that had already been administered in ICCS 2009) and reviews of first new draft 
material by national centers and experts, accompanied by piloting of a draft questionnaire;
•	
In the second phase, first draft material was finalized following an expert and national center review and 
tested in an international field trial undertaken across participating countries;
•	
During the final phase, the results from the field trial were discussed with experts and national centers, 
followed by a final selection of main survey items.
During each of these phases, the following criteria were applied when selecting proposed item material:
•	
Variables measured with the instrument needed to be relevant with regard to the ICCS 2022 
assessment framework;
•	
Question wording and content needed to be deemed appropriate across national contexts in 
participating countries;
•	
Item material should have high psychometric quality in terms of measuring latent traits postulated in 
the initial formulation and confirmed during piloting and field trialing.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
46
A pilot version of international student questionnaire material was carried out in five countries (Chinese 
Taipei, Colombia, Norway, Spain, and Sweden). Three forms each of about 10-15 minutes duration were 
administered to convenience samples of target grade students to pilot a total of 137 questionnaire items. 
For parts of the sample, national centers also carried out qualitative group discussions following the 
administration of the instruments. The results from this pilot study were used in conjunction with feedback 
from national centers and experts to elaborate a draft student questionnaire for the field trial. 
The ICCS 2022 field trial student questionnaire included a total of 273 items (including 34 items that were 
optional for countries) and was administered to samples in 20 participating countries during an extended 
international field trial period between late 2020 and mid-2021.2 Due to the impact of the COVID-19 
pandemic on education, most countries had to implement their field trial in the first half of 2021 and not in 
the originally planned time window at the end of 2020. Three different questionnaire forms were used to 
trial a larger pool of questionnaire items than would have been possible in a single form.  We allocated items 
to these forms in a way that allowed analysis of all possible combinations of item sets and scales. In each 
participating country, data were typically collected from about 1,000 students (on average). As part of the 
field trial, in countries that used computer-based delivery and had participated in the previous cycle, student 
questionnaires were administered both on paper and on computer (using smaller extra samples) to allow an 
analysis of differences across the two modes, which showed only very small effects on questionnaire item 
responses.
The analyses of field trial data were designed to provide empirical evidence for the selection of the main 
survey material with a particular emphasis on the review of the cross-national validity of measures derived 
from the ICCS 2022 questionnaires (see an overview of different approaches in Schulz, 2009). Field trial 
outcomes and a draft student questionnaire for the final data collection proposed by the international study 
center were discussed with national coordinators and experts before selecting items for the main survey.
The final international student questionnaire consisted of 218 items (including 34 items that were optional 
for countries). Thirty-two of these items (14 of which were optional to countries) were designed to capture 
student background information, while 186 were designed to measure the affective-behavioral domains 
specified in the ICCS 2022 civic attitudes and engagement framework, including 20 items that were optional 
for countries. The main survey student questionnaire was designed to take about 40 minutes to complete 
and consisted of the following sections:
•	
About you included questions about student characteristics such as age, gender and expected education;
•	
Your home and your family comprised questions about characteristics of the household and the students’ 
parents;
•	
Your activities outside school consisted of questions about activities reported by students at their home, 
peer, and community environment;
•	
Your school included questions about students’ reports on different aspects of their schools, as well as 
their participation in civic activities at school;
•	
Citizens and society contained questions about the political system, democracy, and the importance of 
different behaviors for good citizenship;
•	
Rights and responsibilities included questions designed to measure student support for equal rights for 
gender groups, all ethnic groups in society, and immigrants, as well as attitudes toward environmental 
protection;
•	
Institutions and society contained questions regarding student trust in civic institutions, and their level 
of concern regarding different potential threats to the world’s future;
•	
Participating in society consisted of questions about students’ self-confidence with regard to active 
participation, and about their expectations of future participation in different civic activities; and
•	
You and religion included optional questions pertaining to the international option about religious 
background and practices, and about their attitudes toward the influence of religion on society.
2	 Due to long-term school closures during the COVID-19 pandemic, a few countries administered their field trial (often using 
smaller samples) only later in 2021 and their results could not be included in the field trial analyses.

QUESTIONNAIRE DEVELOPMENT
47
Most of the items were developed by the international study center, but national centers also proposed new 
items or modifications to student questionnaire material that were included in the final survey instrument. 
In light of the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic, an additional item set measuring students’ support for 
restrictions during national emergencies was developed in close liaison with national centers.
Optional items were designed to capture variables that were perceived as either not relevant or 
inappropriate in some of the participating countries, but that were regarded as crucial in a large number of 
other countries. In these cases, single questions or sets of questions were included as international options 
where each national center could choose whether it wanted to administer this material. In addition, there 
was some interest in measuring aspects of the European region (e.g., trust in European institutions) within 
the context of the international student questionnaire, and a number of optional Europe-related items were 
added to some of international item sets.
The following international options related to students’ background were offered to countries participating 
in ICCS 2022:
•	
Students’ ethnicity (administered in nine education systems);
•	
Household composition (administered in 18 education systems); 
•	
Number of languages spoken by students (administered in 21 education systems);
•	
Use of ICT at home (administered in 20 education systems); 
•	
Religious affiliation (administered in 20 education systems); and 
•	
Attendance of religious services (administered in 19 education systems).
The ICCS 2022 student questionnaire also included questions related to attitudes that were optional for 
national centers:
•	
Endorsement of equal rights for all ethnic groups in society (administered in 20 education systems); 
and
•	
Students' positive attitudes toward the influence of religion in society (administered in 21 education 
systems).
3.4 Teacher questionnaire development
The teacher questionnaire was developed to collect information about school and classroom contexts, the 
links between schools and local communities, the perceived objectives of civic and citizenship education, and 
the delivery of this learning area at school.  In detail, the instrument aimed to gather data on characteristics 
of the school context, including school and classroom climate, teachers’ and students’ participation in school 
governance, teaching practices, students’ behavior at school and classroom levels, activities in the classroom 
to address diversity, and issues related to digital technologies, sustainability, and global citizenship. It also 
captured the relationships between schools and local communities through the measurement of civic-
related activities carried out by teachers with their target grade students within the local community. 
Some of these constructs were also assessed through the school questionnaire with the aim of collecting 
data on the same issues from the double perspective of teachers and school principals. Specifically, we 
included some questions about school climate, social relations and participation at school, sustainability, 
global citizenship education, activities related to digital technologies and diversity, the link between the 
school and the local community, and civic and citizenship education objectives in both teacher and school 
questionnaires. 
Under the assumption that teaching staff constitute an important factor in determining school and 
classroom climate, and in shaping students’ school experience, the teacher questionnaire was designed to 
be completed by teachers teaching across all subject areas in the school curriculum for the ICCS target 
grade. The maximum time envisaged to complete the questionnaire was about 30 minutes. 
The questionnaire also included an international option directed at teachers teaching subjects that 
were directly related to civic and citizenship education in a country. The subjects regarded as related to 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
48
this learning area were determined by the national centers. This international option was chosen by all 
participating countries. It included questions about teaching and assessment approaches to civic and 
citizenship education, teachers’ activities related to global issues, teachers’ preparedness and training in 
civic and citizenship education, and their perceptions of students’ opportunities to learn about civic topics 
and skills. 
The questionnaire development process took place in four phases: 
•	
The first phase included development and review of the draft material by the international study center 
and NRCs. The first questionnaire draft was piloted in a qualitative small-scale pilot study implemented 
in seven countries. The results from the pilot were used to refine the item material. 
•	
In the second phase, material from the pilot was revised, and the international study center developed a 
first draft field trial questionnaire, which was subsequently reviewed by national centers and by experts 
from the Project Advisory Committee (PAC).  
•	
During the third phase, the revised item material was administered to samples of teachers as part of the 
international field trial in all participating countries. 
•	
The final phase comprised a review of the results from the international field trial and the final selection 
of main survey item material following discussions with the PAC and the national centers. 
For the selection of main survey item material, we applied similar criteria to those used for the student 
questionnaire (see previous section). When selecting items, we considered the empirical evidence from the 
field trial in conjunction with suggestions from the PAC and the results of a review of the appropriateness 
of the questionnaire material by national centers, which aimed at highlighting existing differences between 
participating education systems, as well as between schools within each system. 
The field trial teacher questionnaire included 28 questions, with a total of 188 items (3 items were optional) 
and one filter question introducing the international option for teachers of civic-related subjects at the 
target grade. It was administered to target grade teachers in the schools selected for the field trial. On 
average, field trial teacher samples consisted of about 400 teachers in each participating country. The final 
main survey teacher questionnaire included 26 questions (179 items, with 3 optional items) and was divided 
into the following four sections:
•	
General included questions about teacher background characteristics; 
•	
The school comprised questions about the school environment and issues related to participation in 
teaching and learning activities at school and in the community;
•	
Civic and citizenship education at school consisted of questions about the aims of civic and citizenship 
education and about teachers’ perception of the importance of citizenship behaviors; and
•	
Teaching of civic and citizenship education contained questions for teachers of subjects directly related to 
civic and citizenship education and offered as an international option to countries.
3.5 School questionnaire development
The school questionnaire was designed to collect information about school characteristics and school 
principals’ perceptions of school processes that are expected to have an influence on students’ civic and 
citizenship education, for example, school contexts for civic and citizenship education, the context of the 
local community where the school is located, and the opportunities offered to students to participate in the 
school life and in civic-related activities in the wider community. The variables included in this questionnaire 
are related to school size and resources; the school environment; school collaboration with the local 
community; school autonomy in planning civic and citizenship education; student, teacher, and parent 
participation in the running of the school; initiatives related to sustainability and global citizenship, and to 
the use of digital technologies for civic and citizenship education; activities to deal with diversity at school; 
approaches to civic and citizenship education. In addition to this, the school questionnaire also measured 
aspects related to the local community context, such as resources available to students in the local area, and 
issues of social tension within the local community.  

QUESTIONNAIRE DEVELOPMENT
49
Some of the constructs assessed through the school questionnaire were also measured through the teacher 
questionnaire, with the aim of collecting data about these issues which considered the perspectives of both 
teachers and school principals. 
The school questionnaire was designed to be completed by school principals with an estimated completion 
time of up to 30 minutes. 
The questionnaire development process took place in four phases: 
•	
The first phase included development and review of the draft material by the international study center 
and NRCs. The first questionnaire draft was piloted in a qualitative small-scale pilot study implemented 
in seven countries.
•	
In the second phase, material from the pilot was reviewed and a subsequent first draft of the field trial 
school questionnaire was revised by national centers and by the experts on the PAC.
•	
During the third phase, draft material was administered to smaller samples of schools in an international 
field trial undertaken in all participating countries. 
•	
The final phase consisted of a review of field trial results, which formed the basis for the final selection 
of main survey questions and items. The selection took place following discussions with the Project 
Advisory Group and national center representatives. 
During each of these phases, we adopted the same criteria used for the other questionnaires when selecting 
material, that is, we gave particular attention to the appropriateness of questionnaire material for the large 
variety of national contexts in participating countries, as well as to existing differences between education 
systems and between schools within each participating education system. This latter consideration was 
particularly relevant for those education systems that allow schools to exercise a comparatively high level of 
autonomy in school curricula development and delivery. 
The field trial version of the school questionnaire included 25 questions with a total of 140 items (4 items 
were optional) and was administered to the principals of schools that were selected across ICCS 2022 
countries for inclusion in the field trial. In most countries, principals from about 30 schools per country 
provided responses to the field trial questionnaire.
The analyses of field trial data were designed to provide empirical evidence for assisting with the selection 
of the main survey material. Given the relatively small number of responses in each of the participating 
countries, there were some limitations with regard to the scope of analyses that could be carried out with 
the field trial data gathered with this instrument. The school questionnaire results from the field trial were 
discussed with national center representatives and experts of the Project Advisory Group prior to the final 
selection of item material for the main survey. 
The final school questionnaire consisted of 24 questions containing a total of 129 items (with 3 optional 
items), and was divided into the following five sections:
•	
General included only one question about the principals’ career;
•	
The school environment included questions about school governance and students’, teachers’, and 
parents’ participation at school; students’ activities in the community, and initiatives related to diversity, 
sustainability, use of digital technologies for civic and citizenship education and global citizenship;
•	
The local community contained questions about the resources available to students in the local area and 
about issues of social tension within the local community;
•	
Civic and citizenship education at school included questions about how civic and citizenship education is 
implemented at school, schools’ autonomy in civic-related activities, and aims of civic and citizenship 
education; and
•	
School size and resources comprised questions about basic school characteristics like school size, 
numbers of students, or school location.

3.6 European student questionnaire development
The European questionnaire measures issues that were viewed as of particular relevance for the region. 
The European-related topics to be included in the instrument were selected after a mapping and reviewing 
process of potential regional issues against the broader ICCS 2022 framework (Schulz et al., 2023). These 
issues cover specific attitudes in addition to those already measured within the international student 
questionnaire.  
The development of the European questionnaire was a collaborative effort undertaken in close cooperation 
between the international study center, the Project Advisory Group, and national centers. In particular, 
the selection of the region-specific issues to be measured through the instrument was made in close 
cooperation between the international project team and European national center representatives. 
These issues, deemed to be of high importance within the regional context, were related to the European 
identity, opportunities for learning about Europe at school, freedom ad restriction of movement within 
Europe, cooperation among European countries on a different range on issues, including environmental 
sustainability, sustainable behaviors, the European Union, and the future of Europe. 
The development of the European questionnaire consisted of three interrelated phases: 
•	
The international study center made recommendations for the initial instrument development and 
discussed them with country representatives. NRCs provided suggestions for a refinement of the draft 
proposal. Some countries also submitted materials to be included in the questionnaire. A first version 
of the questionnaire was drafted by the international study center based on feedback received from 
country representatives. 
•	
The draft version of the European questionnaire was tested in an international field trial. All European 
countries participating in ICCS 2022 administered the field trial version of the European student 
questionnaire.  
•	
The international study center conducted a final revision of the European questionnaire, informed by 
the field trial analysis and results, in cooperation with the Project Advisory Group and national centers. 
In each of these phases, we adopted the same criteria as for the other questionnaires when selecting the 
material. The version of the European questionnaire administered in the field trial contained 13 questions 
(including one optional question) comprised of 103 items (4 optional items). The final European student 
questionnaire had an estimated completion time of up to 20 minutes and consisted of 12 questions 
comprising a total of 92 items (with 4 optional items) related to: 
•	
Students’ sense of European identity;
•	
Students’ reports on opportunities for learning about Europe at school;
•	
Students’ attitudes toward freedom of movement within Europe;
•	
Students’ attitudes toward cooperation among European countries;
•	
Students’ endorsement of environmental cooperation in Europe;
•	
Students’ perceptions of discrimination in their own countries;
•	
Students’ expectations for the future of Europe;
•	
Students’ expectations for their own individual future;
•	
Students’ perceptions of the importance of some aspects of their life in the future;
•	
Students’ reports on political consumerism behaviors;
•	
Students’ reports on their sustainable behaviors; and
•	
Students’ attitudes toward the EU.
3.7 National context survey development
The ways in which students acquire civic knowledge and understanding, and how they develop dispositions 
toward civic society and civic engagement are expected to be strongly influenced by factors at the 
national context level. Factors of interest include, among others, the historical background, the nature 
of the political system, the structure of the education system, and the curricular framework for civic and 

QUESTIONNAIRE DEVELOPMENT
51
citizenship education. The national contexts survey was designed to systematically collect relevant data and 
information at the country- or system-level about both antecedents and processes that were not readily 
available from published sources. 
The development, coordination, analyses, verification, and reporting of the national contexts survey was 
coordinated by the ICCS consortium and in cooperation with NRCs from participating countries. Staff at 
the international study center were responsible for the implementation of this data collection as an online 
survey completed by national centers and drawing on available expertise in their countries. 
The national contexts survey for ICCS 2022 was largely sourced from the version used in ICCS 2016, which 
had undergone major revisions since the inception of this instrument in ICCS 2009 (see Agrusti et al., 2018). 
The development of the survey consisted of the following process and implementation steps:
•	
An initial development phase was undertaken between the first and second meetings of NRCs, including 
initial discussion about the scope and a revision of the questions used in ICCS 2016. 
•	
A second phase consisted of reviewing a draft questionnaire by national coordinators and experts from 
the PAC, with the aim of further revision and refinement. 
•	
A third phase included gathering NRC feedback, which assisted the finalization of content by 
international study center staff. 
•	
The fourth phase consisted in setting up the instrument as an online survey that was completed by the 
national centers after the respective main data collections from students, teachers, and schools in their 
countries. 
Using the ICCS 2016 version of the national contexts survey as a starting point, the following aspects were 
considered when revising the instrument:
•	
The intended structure of national contextual chapters of international/regional reports;
•	
New content reflecting new and expanded focus areas;
•	
The reduction of content that had been included but not been used previously and was unlikely to 
become relevant for the current cycle;
•	
Recent changes in education systems and policies related to civic and citizenship education;
•	
Impacts of COVID-19 pandemic on education systems;
•	
Impacts of the war in Ukraine on student learning and education systems;
•	
The reduction of subjectivity of national center responses, where possible; and
•	
The reduction of response burden on national centers.
During the process of the development of the content, we applied the following criteria when considered 
the contexts and questions to be included in the national contexts survey: 
•	
Its relevance with regard to the ICCS assessment framework; 
•	
Its additional value in relation to information about national contexts already in the public domain; 
•	
Its appropriateness for the national contexts in participating countries; and 
•	
Its validity in terms of comparability, analysis, and reporting. 
The final national contexts survey included greater inclusion of content related to the new focus areas, 
alignment with changes to the other questionnaires, and inclusion of content related to the COVID-19 crisis 
and the Ukraine conflict. The final survey contained 37 questions organized into the following content areas:
•	
Education system
o	
Background
o	
Structure of the education system
o	
COVID-19 disruptions
•	
Civic and citizenship education in the curriculum
o	
Education policies related to civic and citizenship education
o	
Civic and citizenship education at school
o	
Civic and citizenship education at the target grade
o	
Current reforms and debates

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
52
•	
Teachers and teacher education
o	
Teacher education in general
o	
Teacher education for civic and citizenship education
o	
In-service teacher education for civic and citizenship education
•	
Assessments and quality assurance
•	
The 2022 Ukraine crisis
The online system enabled national center staff to complete the survey in several administration sessions 
(i.e., they could log in and out in order to complete the questionnaire as the required information became 
available). The online data received from each of the 24 participating education systems were checked for 
consistency and plausibility by project staff at the international study center. National centers were invited 
to review the draft tables that were to be used for the international report and would be included in the 
database.  Corrections were applied where appropriate following feedback from participating countries. 
The international project team used the outcomes of the national contexts survey in conjunction with data 
from published sources to inform the descriptions of the education systems and contexts for civic and 
citizenship in ICCS 2022 reporting.

Chapter 4:
ICCS computer-based systems
Tim Friedman and Julian Fraillon
4.1 Overview
In the first two cycles of the International Civic and Citizenship Education Study (ICCS), all student response 
data were collected on paper. As part of a planned transition to computer-based data collection, ICCS 2022 
offered countries the option to collect student response data on computer rather than on paper. In ICCS 
2022, 16 countries and the two German benchmarking participants chose to participate in the computer-
based data collection while six countries collected all student data on paper. 
This chapter describes the key aspects of the computer-based delivery system used in ICCS 2022. It 
also details some of the challenges of using computer-based systems to collect computerized data in an 
international large-scale assessment (ILSA) such as ICCS.
The focus of the chapter is on the overall approach, architecture, and design of the computer-based 
assessment (CBA) system, as well as the relationship of the components of the system to other technical 
systems used for the survey operations in ICCS. Details and procedural aspects related to the operational 
use of the components of the CBA system as well as reviews of mode effects are described in chapters 
associated with their use in ICCS 2022: assessment and test design in Chapter 2, translation and adaptation 
in Chapter 5, sampling design and implementation in Chapter 6, field operations in Chapter 7, data flow and 
integration in Chapter 9, scaling and analysis of the test (Chapter 11), and questionnaire data (Chapter 12).
4.2 ICCS 2022 computer-based components and architecture
Preexisting components
Before the inception of computer-based test delivery, IEA was already using computer-based applications to 
organize and support countries to coordinate field operations and collect questionnaire data from teachers 
and schools. These applications, developed by IEA, include the following:
•	
The IEA Windows Within-School Sampling Software (IEA WinW3S), which supports countries to 
manage within-school sampling and test administration procedures;
•	
The IEA Data Management Expert (IEA DME), which is used to capture data from paper-based 
questionnaire material and to integrate and verify national databases;
•	
The IEA Coding Expert, which is used to code the student responses with respect to parental occupation; 
and
•	
The IEA Translation System, which is used to translate and review the school, ICT coordinator, and 
teacher questionnaires online.
Versions of each of these software applications were configured for and used in ICCS 2022.
RM ResultsTM (previously SoNET Systems) provided the AssessmentMaster (AM) software suite, used to 
prepare, deliver, and score the computer-based test and questionnaires to students, and the questionnaires 
to teachers and school principals. The software components specifically relating to the delivery of the ICCS 
2022 materials include: an administration module (AM Manager); an item authoring and translation module 
(AM Designer); a delivery engine module (AM Examiner), and a scoring module (AM Marker).
4.3 Procedures and software user manuals
In all IEA studies, the Survey Operations Procedures (SOP) manuals guide the work of national center staff. 
IEA, in close cooperation with the ICCS consortium, has developed and refined instructions and guidelines 
for IEA WinW3S and IEA DME, and included these in the SOP manuals (see Chapter 7 for details).

ICCS COMPUTER-BASED SYSTEMS
55
During ICCS 2022, separate user manuals were developed for the AM software components. These 
manuals were further refined between the field trial and the main survey. Use of the relevant features in 
AM Examiner were incorporated in the manuals for school coordinators and test administrators.
4.4 ICCS 2022 system architecture

support the preparation, administration, and post-processing of the study. Table 4.1 provides an overview 
of the ICCS 2022 computer-based systems. The table also shows how these systems interacted. Table 4.1 is 
organized by the major components of the systems and references the ICCS 2022 respondent populations 
and response modes.

4.5 Functions of the computer-based delivery platform for ICCS 2022
Instrument Preparation using AM Designer
Authoring
All instruments were authored within AM Designer. The authoring functions allowed users to create 
and edit items (together with any stimulus materials) as well as any ancillary instrument content, such as 
instructions or information messages. In addition, item metadata reflecting fixed characteristics of items 
(such as the item format) were recorded automatically within AM Designer and configurable metadata 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
56
(such as framework references) could also be stored. All instrument content developed within AM Designer 
was consequently linked to the adaptation and translation facilities within the system.
Simple item content, based on existing item entry templates, was authored by staff in the International 
Study Center (ISC). Bespoke authoring, such as for the computer-enhanced dynamic content, was authored 
by RM developers in consultation with ISC staff.
AM Designer was also used to allocate test and questionnaire content to their respective instruments. This 
was managed within the system by ISC staff.
Adaptation
Two types of adaptation were possible for the ICCS instruments. 
Structural adaptations were possible for the questionnaires but not for the student test. Structural 
adaptations include some adaptation of the structure of a question for use in a specific country. These could 
be, for example, the addition of some national items within an international question, the addition of an 
entirely new question, or creation of additional response categories (that are sub-categories of the original 
international response category). Structural adaptations needed to be bespoke coded into the instruments 
for each country by the RM development team. Once structural adaptations requested by a country were 
approved by the ISC, a request was made to the RM team to implement the agreed adaptations in AM 
Designer. These adaptations were then checked by the ISC and national centers. 
Language adaptations involved the replacement of specific words or phrases in an instrument with locally 
equivalent terms. This differs from translation in that, in some instances, a direct translation of a word 
or phrase in the source language may not convey the intended meaning in the target language. Terms or 
phrases requiring language adaptation were shown in the instruments in square brackets []. Once language 
adaptations requested by a country were approved by the ISC, they were included in the translations 
entered into AM Designer by the national center team (see below).
Translation
In ICCS 2022, translations for all instruments were completed in AM Designer. To access the system, users 
needed computers with the following requirements:
•	
A computer with a high-speed internet connection; 
•	
Any compatible operating platform (e.g., Windows, Mac OS, or Linux) and;
•	
A recent version (i.e., less than 12 months old) of a major web-browser (e.g., Google Chrome, Internet 
Explorer, Microsoft Edge, Mozilla Firefox, Opera, Safari).
Development of the translation systems provided the following features:
•	
Some selective functionality relating to the role of the user (e.g., administrator, translator, translation 
reviewer, translation verifier, verification reviewer);
•	
Opportunity to enter translated text for all screen elements;
•	
Ability to view the translation history of a text element;
•	
Ability to view the source text, the translated text, and any previous revisions of the translated text, and 
to compare different versions of translations;
•	
A comments interface to allow translators, reviewers, and verifiers to enter comments linked to 
elements of text;
•	
Opportunity to view “live” translated versions of the tasks at any point during the translation and to 
simultaneously view (in a separate window) a live version of the source question or task;
•	
Ability to enter text as plain text, including editable HTML tags or to enter and use formatting tools to 
format text;
•	
Ability to view translated text elements as plain text or as rendered HTML;
•	
Ability to search for and/or selectively bulk-replace text within and across tasks;

ICCS COMPUTER-BASED SYSTEMS
57
•	
Ability to bulk-import the field trial translations so that they could be used as a starting point for main 
survey translations; and
•	
Opportunity for translators, reviewers, and verifiers to monitor the progress of translation for each 
item throughout the translation workflow.
Instrument Delivery using AM Examiner
Delivery of the student instruments
In order to maximize participation, the consortium worked with RM results to ensure that the delivery could 
be as flexible as possible, allowing for two main forms of delivery of the student instruments:
i.	
Online (internet-based) delivery; and
ii.	
USB-based delivery for use with school computers running Windows operating systems.
The test delivery engine itself was web-based. Offline solutions made use of a locally installed web server 
(on USB or on the local server) through which the application could be accessed. Although the USB-based 
delivery engine was Windows-based, it could be run using some Windows emulation software on Mac OS 
X or Linux. 
All offline solutions used a portable version of the Mozilla Firefox browser. All ICCS 2022 materials were 
developed to be delivered using recent (not more than one year old) versions of Mozilla Firefox, Google 
Chrome, MS Internet Explorer 11, MS Edge, Apple Safari, and Opera. AM Designer, AM Marker, and AM 
Manager functioned on recent versions of all major browsers. As part of the field-operation procedures, 
data collected using offline solutions (USB or local server) needed to be uploaded to a central database as 
soon as practicable after each test session. Web-based delivery did not require any additional steps, as the 
data were immediately written to the AM database during each test session. 
Two backup delivery alternatives were also available to countries:
i.	
Taking laptop computers with AM Examiner already installed into schools; or
ii.	
Connecting a single laptop computer into the school network to act as a web server that hosts the 
student instruments (this was recommended for use with schools using networked computers that did 
not use Windows operating systems such as Mac OS or Linux). This method is hereafter referred to as 
the “server method.’’
Minimum specifications for each delivery mode of the student instruments
For online delivery, the following minimum specifications were required:
•	
Internet access with SSL security;
•	
Screen resolution 1280x800;
•	
Screen size 9.7 inches; 
•	
Any OS that can run a modern browser (IE 11, Edge, Opera, Firefox, Chrome, Safari);
•	
Sufficient bandwidth (assessed by the School Coordinator using a practice test);
•	
Power supply, keyboard, mouse.
For USB-based delivery, the following minimum specifications were required:
•	
Screen Resolution of at least 1280x800;
•	
Windows 8 operating system (preferably with patches) or later;
•	
Display (fonts) set to the optimal size (minimum: 100%; the system font size can be set from the 
computer’s control panel/system settings);
•	
A USB Port 2.0 or higher; and
•	
A quality well-known brand of USB stick with a transfer Rate of at least 3 MB/s.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
58
For local server delivery,1 the following minimum specifications were required:
Requirements for the server computer:
•	
Windows 8 operating system (up-to date with patches) or later;
•	
2.2 GHz Processor;
•	
8GB Memory;
•	
Available Storage Space: 10GB; 
•	
Admin rights;
•	
LAN or Wi-Fi connectivity.
Requirements for the client computers:
•	
Screen Resolution of at least 1280x800;
•	
Connectivity to the same LAN or Wi-Fi network as the server computer; 
•	
Recent version (less than 12 months old) of Mozilla Firefox, Google Chrome, MS Internet Explorer 11, 
MS Edge, Apple Safari or Opera;
•	
Display (fonts) set to the optimal size (minimum: 100%; the system font size can be set from the 
computer’s Control Panel/Appearance and Personalization/Display).
Checking the suitability of school resources to deliver the ICCS 2022 student instruments
School coordinators were provided with a compatibility check program that was conducted to assess the 
computers for suitability for administration of the ICCS 2022 main survey student instruments. National 
Research Coordinators (NRCs) were advised to provide the compatibility check program to school 
coordinators long enough before the scheduled administration dates to enable time to deal with any issues, 
but not so long that large changes to schools IT infrastructure were likely to have occurred in the interim. In 
most cases, the compatibility checks were conducted approximately two months before the dates of testing. 
The compatibility check program contained three components:
1.	
The ICCS 2022 technical readiness test: This involved accessing a URL that assessed whether 
the browser of the computer, the JavaScript version, and the screen resolution met the minimum 
requirements for delivery of the ICCS student instruments using online delivery;
2.	
The ICCS 2022 bandwidth test: This involved running a simulated test session using four screens, so 
that the school coordinator could make a subjective decision on whether the internet bandwidth was 
sufficient for online delivery; and
3.	
The ICCS Compatibility Check program: This was an executable file run via USB and was used to assess 
whether the computer meets the minimum requirements for delivery of the student instruments using 
USB-based delivery.
Delivery of the ICCS 2022 school questionnaires
In ICCS 2022, the teacher and principal questionnaires were authored and translated in AM Designer, 
and subsequently delivered using the AM Examiner. These instruments were delivered in the online mode 
only. Respondents received unique user credentials and national URL to access the instruments online. As 
with the online delivery of the student instruments, responses were written to the AM online database 
as they were completed. The online questionnaires were available to respondents to access for as long 
as staff in national centers deemed appropriate. This was typically between six weeks and two months. 
Respondents could complete the questionnaires in more than one session if they wished, and they could 
return to their questionnaire to review and edit responses as often as required until they ‘submitted’ their 
responses. Respondents submitted their questionnaire responses by clicking on a button at the end of the 
questionnaire. Respondents could not log back in after they had submitted their responses.
1	 The server method could only be used in schools with an existing Local Area Network (LAN).

ICCS COMPUTER-BASED SYSTEMS
59
Scoring using AM Marker
AM Marker was used for the scoring of those ICCS 2022 test responses that were collected on computer. 
AM Marker was developed to include the following features:
•	
Selective functionality relating to the role of the user (e.g., administrator, scoring trainer, team leader, 
scorer);
•	
Facility to specify a proportion (in ICCS 2022, 20%) of student responses to be blind double- scored for 
the purpose of monitoring inter-rater agreement; and
•	
Capacity to begin scoring before all student responses had been uploaded to the system (i.e., before 
completion of data collection in schools) without compromising the double-scoring procedure.
Scorers were able to:
•	
View tasks (as they appeared to students in the test) along with student responses on screen;
•	
Enter a score for each student for each task;
•	
Flag responses for follow-up with a more senior staff member;
•	
Navigate back to previously scored responses and amend scores;
•	
View computer-enhanced items with full functionality; and
•	
Enter a “training” mode to score pre-scored responses and receive feedback on scoring accuracy.
In addition, team leaders could:
•	
Review (check-score and amend) scorers’ scores; and
•	
Monitor and respond to flagged responses.
In addition, scoring trainers could:
•	
Select, score, order, and annotate responses for use in scorer training.
In addition, scoring administrators could:
•	
Allocate scorers to teams; and
•	
View reports of interscorer reliability.
Managing the online systems and data collection using AM Manager
The ICCS 2022 administration module, AM Manager, was configured to (i) support national center staff 
monitor the progress of test sessions (i.e., data upload), create user accounts, and define roles for users of 
the other modules (scoring and translation), and (ii) export test-session data for importing into IEA WinW3S.
The administration module enabled users to:
•	
Create user accounts and allocate roles;
•	
View national test session details;
•	
Monitor national test session status; and
•	
Export test session data for use in IEA WinW3S.
4.6 Monitoring data completeness from computer-based delivery in ICCS 2022
As ICCS transitioned to computer-based administration of student instruments for the first time, there 
were some challenges. Firstly, there was a challenge to ensure students who were administered the 
instruments on computer were not unfairly advantaged or disadvantaged. A bridging study was included 
in ICCS 2022 within selected countries using computer-based delivery. The purpose of the bridging study 
was to investigate any relative impact of the delivery mode (computer or paper) on student test scores and 
questionnaire responses (see Chapters 11 and 12 for further details).  
Successful collection of data in large-scale computer-based surveys relies on individual participants being 
able to provide responses on computers in controlled uniform environments from which data can be 
recorded, organized, and stored for later use. 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
60
Paper-based assessment and CBA present different challenges regarding the uniformity of test presentation 
and administration. As the previous cycles of ICCS were conducted on paper, ICCS had well established 
protocols regarding the production and distribution of paper-based instruments and their administration 
within schools. The introduction of CBA into ICCS brought with it a new set of administrative challenges 
to maximize the uniformity of the test-taking experience as well as technical challenges associated with 
ensuring that the visual on-screen display and user experience was consistent across students. Differences 
in the user-experience may affect students’ performance, and any differences may be more pronounced in 
complex tasks, such as those involving interactive stimuli. These tasks place greater demands on delivery 
methods than standard, flat, stimulus material, and multiple-choice response options.
The ICCS 2022 test-delivery platform, including both USB and online-delivery options, was designed 
to minimize the potential for variation in students’ test-taking experience. The layout of the ICCS 2022 
instruments could be checked (as a feature of the translation module) and corrected throughout the 
translation and verification processes and was formally verified during the layout verification process. 
Chapter 5 of this report describes these processes in detail.
The data collected using AM Examiner during ICCS 2022 needed to be extracted, transformed, and then 
loaded into IEA systems for processing, weighting, and analysis. These tasks required the interaction of a 
complex set of computer-based system components (as shown in Table 4.1). As is the case with any system 
comprising interconnected components with a range of specific functions, the interfaces between each 
component are potential points of failure. To ensure that the system worked successfully, national research 
center staff, in collaboration with ISC staff needed to monitor both the integrity of the functioning of the 
individual components of the system (paying special attention to ensuring results data were being stored) 
and the interfaces between the components.
It is not possible in this section of the report to look at the full range of sources and types of information 
described above. Instead, the focus is on key findings relating to one particular type of information, that 
is, the proportion and typology of cases that school coordinators, test administrators or national center 
staff classified as technical problems during administration. Nonresponse at the unit or item level, whether 
due to a lack of cooperation, technical issues, or flawed administration, is one of the more serious factors 
influencing the robustness and stability of inferences from sample surveys. Therefore, those responsible for 
implementing a survey nationally regularly monitor response and participation rates, especially as these are 
used as key indicators of success, quality, and fitness for use.
During ICCS 2022, the examination of unweighted but cleaned data from all 18 participating education 
systems that administered the ICCS student instruments using CBA yielded some interesting insights. Of 
the 62,055 sampled students with response data, just over one-third (36%) used USB delivery, and just 
under two-thirds (63%) used online delivery. Only a small proportion (less than 0.5% of all students), used 
the server method. CBA was exclusively used in 19 educational systems for the teacher questionnaire, and 
in 18 educational systems for the school questionnaire. Approximately 80 percent of all teachers completed 
the teacher questionnaire online, and approximately 74 percent of all principals completed the school 
questionnaire online.
The online teacher and school questionnaires were available to be completed within a period of several 
weeks and in multiple sessions at the discretion of the respondent. As such, point-in-time technical issues 
in delivery of the teacher and school questionnaires were not able to be recorded systematically by a single 
test administrator.
A review of the survey activities questionnaire (SAQ) completed by NRCs (see Chapter 8 for further details) 
revealed that national centers had many difficulties associated with the computer-based delivery in ICCS 
2022. Difficulties encountered within countries included:
•	
Various problems with the translation system;
•	
Problems with production of paper-based materials using the AM suite (extended issues and delays 
required ISC staff to manually produce these for countries) (see Chapter 5 for further details);
•	
Issues with the web application, half of the respondents to the SAQ indicated there was a problem;

ICCS COMPUTER-BASED SYSTEMS
61
•	
Instances of technical problems in the field where the instruments failed to load properly;
•	
Instances of source material showing for respondents, instead of nationally translated text;
•	
Instances of unavailability of participation monitoring reports;
•	
Issues with the marking system for particular items; and
•	
Instances of data captured using USB delivery not being uploaded to the server properly. Five of the 
nine respondents to the SAQ indicated a problem here.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
62
Chapter 5:
Preparation and verification of the ICCS 2022 
study instruments
Lauren Musu, Kateřina Hartmanová, Jan-Philipp Wagner, Tim Friedman, and Laila Helou
5.1 Introduction 
This chapter describes the preparation procedures for national versions of the International Civic and 
Citizenship Education Study (ICCS) 2022 study instruments. The instrument preparation process aims 
to maximize comparability of the national instruments to the international versions, maintain equivalence 
across countries and languages, and ensure appropriateness for each country and participant’s national 
context and education system. To achieve the overarching goals of fidelity to the international source 
instruments and cross-national comparability, all national ICCS 2022 study instruments underwent a 
thorough and highly standardized preparation process described in detail in this chapter.
The chapter focuses on the following major topics: 
•	
Adaptation and translation of the international source version of the ICCS 2022 instruments into 
national languages;
•	
International verification of the national adaptations and translations; and
•	
International layout verification and quality control of the final national instruments.
The international versions of the ICCS instruments (see Chapters 2 and 3 respectively for details on the 
development of these instruments) were produced in English by the ICCS 2022 consortium, in close 
collaboration with ICCS 2022 national centers. After the release of the international source version of the 
instruments, all national centers were required to adapt and translate the international version into their 
language(s) of instruction. 
The International Study Center (ISC) at Australian Council for Educational Research (ACER) and 
International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) Amsterdam oversaw the 
instrument preparation procedures and were responsible for different stages of the whole process. Figure 
5.1 shows the overall workflow for preparing the national ICCS 2022 study instruments. The overall process 
was conducted once before the field trial and then a second time before the main survey. To the extent 
possible, national centers were encouraged to use the field trial adaptations and translations as a basis for 
their main survey instruments.
Figure 5.1: Instrument preparation workflow for the ICCS 2022 Main Survey

PREPARATION AND VERIFICATION OF THE ICCS 2022 STUDY INSTRUMENTS
63
5.2 Adaptation and translation of the ICCS 2022 study instruments 
ICCS 2022 instruments requiring adaptation and translation
The ISC provided national centers with the source versions of all materials to be adapted and/or translated. 
These included:
•	
The student cognitive test (including covers and instructions);
•	
The international questionnaires for students, teachers, and school principals, (including covers and 
introductions); and (where applicable)
•	
The regional student questionnaire (including covers and introductions).
In addition to conventional cognitive items, this cycle included six computer-enhanced items (CEIs) for 
computer-based administration only (see the Layout preparation section for more details). The CEIs, 
included in ICCS for the first time, were designed to assess civic knowledge using more interactive 
functionality made possible by the computer-based delivery mode (see Chapter 2 – Test Development for 
further details). 
All instruments were released together with multiple supporting resources set up to describe and 
document each step of the adaptation, translation, and verification processes. As written guidance, the 
Survey Operation Procedures (SOP) Unit 3 described in detail the procedures and all necessary steps of 
the translation and verification process. Training webinars were also provided to complement the written 
guidance.
In addition to the ICCS instruments, the procedural manuals and scoring guides for constructed-response 
items were translated into the national languages. These materials were not subject to the international 
verification procedures. However, the translations of Test Administrator Manual and School Coordinator 
Manual underwent a final quality control spot check by an independent quality observer (for further details 
please see Chapter 8).
Languages used in ICCS 2022
For most countries, the language of the assessment (the target language) is the dominant language used in 
both public and private sectors of society, including the education system. However, some countries use more 
than one language of instruction in their education system and in such cases, on the basis of consultation 
with national centers, the student instruments could be translated into multiple languages to ensure that 
the assessment was appropriate for the defined population of students in the country. The decision on 
which language(s) were used in each country depended on multiple factors such as the population definition 
of students, the inclusion or exclusion of given students in that definition and consequently whether schools 
that provide instruction in a minority language were included in the sample of students in a given country. 
For ICCS 2022, a total of 22 countries and 2 benchmarking participants prepared 36 different sets of materials 
in 31 different languages (see Table 5.1). Of the participating entities, eight administered the instruments in 
more than one language. For the convenience of countries that participated in the Latin American Module 
(LAM) and had Spanish as their assessment language, a source version of the LAM questionnaire was also 
released in Castilian. Table 5.1 also shows the delivery mode for each set of instruments. The majority of 
countries administered the student instruments using one of the computer-based assessment (CBA) modes 
(online, USB or both) (see Chapter 4 for further details). Those countries that participated in ICCS 2016 and 
delivered the student instruments using CBA in 2022, also had to complete the ICCS bridging study (see 
Chapters 2 and 11 for details). Countries completed the teacher or school questionnaires using paper or 
online delivery, but not both.
