PREPARATION AND VERIFICATION OF THE ICCS 2022 STUDY INSTRUMENTS
65
Administration modes and instrument formats
Sixteen countries and the two benchmarking participants administered ICCS as CBA and the remaining six 
countries as a paper-based assessment (PBA). In addition, the 11 CBA countries who participated in ICCS 
2016 also administered paper booklets to an additional sample of schools, to provide a link between the two 
administration modes (see Chapters 2 and 11 for further details about the bridging study).
To maintain comparability across delivery modes, the ICCS 2022 items common to CBA and PBA were 
designed to have similar visual structural features to students. The six CEIs were administered using CBA 
only and did not have PBA equivalents. 
All instrument content was available for translation within the RM Assessment Master (AM) system (see 
Chapter 4). The procedures for translation and verification took place in the online translation system for 
both administration modes.
In the CBA mode, the RM AM system assembled and delivered the translated test and questionnaire 
content based on the prescribed instrument design that was configured within the system. In for the PBA 
mode, countries were provided additional support documents showing the test design so that they could 
manually assemble and check the configuration of the PBA test content. This approach allowed countries/
participants to translate each component once only and assemble materials at the end into different 
booklets to be administered.
The translation system
To prepare the ICCS 2022 study instruments for the main survey, including translation, the national centers 
used the Designer module of the Assessment Master (AM) online system from RM Results (see Chapter 4 
for more details about the system). The system allowed users to apply and edit their translations, keep a full 
record of the editing history for the text, and to retrace changes made by other users ensuring transparency 
of the translation and verification process. In addition, the system displayed the differences between a 
previously saved translation and the current translation in the form of tracked changes, as well as allowed for 
exports of the translations in the form of an Excel or PDF file. These functions supported national research 
coordinators (NRCs) and translation verifiers with their reviewing process. 
Documentation supporting the instrument preparation process
Any changes, selections, and adaptations to the survey instruments were done with the goal of attaining 
comparable data from all participating education systems and were documented in the national adaptation 
form (NAF) for each language and set of instruments. In addition, changes to trend translations (where 
applicable) were documented in additional trend verification forms (TVFs) for each language. 
The national adaptation form (NAF)
The NAF was an Excel document containing a comprehensive adaptation and verification history of each set 
of national instruments. It consisted of individual worksheets for each separate instrument (the cognitive 
test, international student questionnaire, teacher questionnaire, school questionnaire, and regional student 
questionnaire where applicable), as well as separate worksheets for completion instructions, additional 
country documentation (e.g., language information, version number, inclusion status of international 
optional questions) and a style guide. The style guide included information indicating the language variant, 
form of address, and national conventions for numbers, time, grammar, and measurement used in the 
materials. During various stages of the instrument preparation process, sections of the form were entered 
and reviewed.
The first version of the NAF was to be completed within national centers. This was done, usually as national 
center staff began the processes of adapting and translating the instruments. Adaptations recorded in 
the NAF then required approval by the ISC and their implementation (based on the agreed and approved 
adaptations) was checked by international verifiers during translation verification. International verifiers 
and NRCs updated and revised the NAF accordingly after each round of international verifications.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
66
Documenting an adaptation in the NAF required entering the proposed adaptation in the target language, 
an English back-translation of the adaptation, recoding instructions (if applicable), and a justification for 
any further alternations. For the ease of use and documentation of the different stages of verification, the 
NAF included designated areas for each item, user, and instrument. For countries measuring comparisons 
in achievement over time, adaptations for existing ICCS 2016 items had to be documented in the NAF, in 
the same way as the other national adaptations, and the adaptations needed to be kept unchanged as much 
as possible.
The NAF was an important record of each country’s final instrument versions, as it contained information 
used throughout the different stages of the translation and verification processes. The international quality 
observers also used the NAF after data collection to review the implementation of translation and layout 
verification feedback (see Chapter 8 for further details).
The NAF was referenced when adding national data to the international database and during data analysis.
Trend verification forms (TVFs)
TVFs were Excel documents with a structure similar to the NAF, created to keep a record of changes made 
to trend translations. There were two separate TVFs, one for the cognitive test items and one for the 
questionnaires.
Documenting a change to a trend translation in the TVF required entering the original (2016) translation, 
the proposed new (2022) translation, selecting the category of  the proposed change (major linguistic error, 
minor linguistic error, necessary update to terminology, proposal for alternative wording) and providing a 
justification for the change to the trend translation, including an English back translation. During translation 
verification, international verifiers reviewed the TVF, commented on the degree of agreement between the 
original translation and the new translation, and provided linguistic insight on whether the proposed change 
to translation was necessary. The final decision to keep or reject the change to trend translation was made 
by NRCs, in consideration of the comments provided during translation verification. 
The TVFs were important to keep record of each country’s trend translations (where applicable) and 
additionally used to gain an overview of changes made to these translations. Since such changes in translation 
could lead to changes in item performance, the overview of changes was taken into consideration during the 
review of main survey data. 
Adaptation process and overview
Together with the instrument release, the ICCS 2022 Consortium provided national centers with written 
guidance on the procedures required to adapt and translate the cognitive items and questionnaires 
appropriately, within the SOP Unit 3. 
Adapting the instruments to fit the country/participant’s context and education system was the first of the 
key steps in the national instrument preparation process. There were two different types of adaptations that 
could be applied to the instruments: structural and non-structural adaptations. Structural adaptations refer 
to alterations that change the structure of the study instruments. This includes adding national questions; 
adding, splitting, or omitting answer options; and removing questions. National centers were allowed to 
apply this type of adaptation solely to the background questionnaires and not to the cognitive test items. 
Non-structural adaptations were defined as any adaptation that did not alter the structure of the study 
instruments but could change the meaning of text to fit the local context. The international version of the 
study instruments indicated where such adaptations were necessary using square brackets to note terms to 
be adapted (i.e., required adaptations). For example, required adaptations were the [target grade], personal 
names (e.g., [Male name 1]), places (e.g., [M-town]), or International Standard Classification of Education 
(ISCED) level equivalents (see http://www.uis.unesco.org/Education/Documents/isced-2011-en.pdf).
NRCs could also add non-structural adaptations to their instruments wherever they considered these 
necessary to ensure that terms or phrases fit the national context and were appropriate for the target 
audience.

PREPARATION AND VERIFICATION OF THE ICCS 2022 STUDY INSTRUMENTS
67
To help countries/participants adapt their materials to the national context, the ISC provided them with 
notes that included brief descriptions of each required adaptation clarifying the meaning of the terms used 
(and thus enabling the translators to select the appropriate national term or expression necessary to convey 
the intended meaning). In addition, general guidelines for translation and adaptation were provided (listed 
in the Translation and adaptation of cognitive items section below). 
Although NRCs were strongly encouraged to keep adaptations to a minimum, some (required) adaptations 
were necessary in order to prevent students from facing unfamiliar contexts or vocabulary that could 
hinder their ability to read and understand an item. These included changes to follow national conventions, 
punctuation, and expressions of date and time. Where the names of fictional cities or towns were adapted, 
the use of real place names was not advised to prevent students’ responses from being influenced by their 
perception and knowledge of the names. Instead, names in the target language of similar length, familiarity, 
and complexity were recommended, to convey the same meaning and maintain the style of text used in the 
international source version.
In the specific area of civics and citizenship, direct translations of some terms from English may not result in 
the term maintaining its meaning across countries. Terms relating to specific institutions and organizations 
required adaptation. For example, the term “parliament” (intended to refer to a legislative body at the 
national level) might be used in some unicameral systems (e.g., “Parliament of Denmark”) but in bicameral 
systems contexts may vary, and the appropriate term would have to be selected (e.g., the “Staten-Generaal” 
or the parliament of the Netherlands consists of the “Tweede Kamer,” or the lower house, and the “Eeerste 
Kamer,” or the upper house, and each has different tasks). The goal of such adaptations was to make the 
questions equally familiar to all students, while maintaining the same meaning and level of difficulty. Some 
terms in the text were not to be changed or adapted beyond translation. Examples include proper names of 
actual people and institutions. 
Translation process overview
Along with adaptation, another key part of the national instrument preparation process was the translation 
of the materials into the target language(s). 
Countries were strongly advised to hire highly qualified translators and reviewers who were well suited to 
the task of working with the ICCS 2022 materials. The translators had primary responsibility for translation 
of materials. Reviewers were primarily responsible for assessing the readability and accuracy of the 
translations for the target population. Essential qualifications for translators and reviewers included:
•	
Excellent knowledge of English;
•	
The target language as mother tongue; and
•	
Some experience in the country’s present cultural and educational context.
IEA also advised the countries that the translators and/or reviewers should have experience with translating 
texts on social or political issues, translating achievement tests, and working with students in the target grade. 
Each national center was responsible for ensuring the suitability of their translators and reviewers. All 
countries were required to include at least one translator and one reviewer in the instrument preparation 
process. Some countries employed multiple translators and reviewers, either working together to complete 
the tasks, or working independently to provide multiple perspectives. Countries were responsible for 
ensuring consistency of the reviews across the translated materials.
If countries prepared translations in more than one language, IEA suggested the involvement of professionals 
familiar with the various languages to ensure equivalent translations across all the national versions. 
Translation and adaptation of cognitive items
One of the main challenges when translating the ICCS 2022 cognitive test material was to ensure that 
appropriate terms and expressions in the target language(s) of each country conveyed the same meaning 
and that the style of the text was consistent with the international source version.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
68
In the translation guidelines, translators and reviewers were asked to ensure that:
•	
The translated text did not explain or remove text from the source text, did not add more information, 
and was at an appropriate level for the target population;
•	
The translated text used terminology equivalent to that of the international version;
•	
The translated text had the same level of difficulty and register (language level and degree of
•	
formality) as the international version;
•	
The translated text used equivalent social, political, and historical terminology appropriate to the target 
language;
•	
Idiomatic expressions were translated appropriately, not necessarily word for word; and
•	
The translated text used correct grammar, punctuation, qualifiers, and modifiers, as appropriate for the 
target language.
Translators also needed to ensure the consistency of adaptations and translations from item to item. For 
multiple-choice items, translators were instructed to pay particular attention to common text that appeared 
in the question stem and answer options in the source version, to ensure these were maintained (or omitted) 
in the translated national version. As previously mentioned, no additions were permitted for the assessment.
Cognitive items for trend comparison over time
Fifty-five test items were carried over from the previous cycle (ICCS 2016) in order to measure changes 
in student achievement over time. These items (trend items) provide the basis for comparisons of changes 
in achievement from the earlier assessment for those countries that participated in both study cycles with 
the same target population. To ensure the quality of comparisons over time, these items needed to be 
administered in the same way in each cycle. 
For countries that previously participated in ICCS 2016, the translations of the items used in the previous 
assessment (trend translations) were compared against the ICCS 2022 translations. If a national center 
determined that changes to these specific items were necessary (e.g., in order to correct a mistranslation 
discovered in a previous translation), the changes were carefully documented in the TVF and referenced 
during the data analysis. Some items with changes were not included in the comparison analyses over time 
for that country. For countries that did not participate in ICCS 2016, the preparation of these specific items 
followed the same general procedures as the newly developed test items for the current cycle.
Translation and adaptation of the questionnaires
National centers were also required to adapt some terms in the questionnaires and ensure that questions 
were appropriate for the national context and education system. Concepts and expressions that were not 
common to all cultures and not related to the substance of the questions needed to be adapted.
The terms requiring adaptation were listed in angle brackets in the international source version. For instance, 
<language of test> and <target grade> were adapted to the name of the actual language and grade in which 
the assessment was administered; for example, in Bulgaria, these terms would be replaced by equivalents 
“Bulgarian” and “grade 8.” Some terms related to specific aspects of students’ participation in school 
leadership were also designated for adaptation. For example, <school parliament> needed to be adapted to 
the local term that denoted any type of student representation that was democratically elected by students 
at the school, for example, in Estonia the translation would be equivalent to “students’ representative body” 
in English. 
Countries were permitted to add a small number of national questions or categories within the existing 
questionnaires (these are examples of structural adaptations). NRCs were instructed to place all substantive 
national items at the end of the international questionnaires. All national questions needed to be documented 
and approved by the ICCS 2022 Consortium before they could be included in the questionnaires.

PREPARATION AND VERIFICATION OF THE ICCS 2022 STUDY INSTRUMENTS
69
Layout preparation 
The final step in the country’s preparation of national instruments was layout preparation. NRCs were asked 
to review each question in the proposed delivery mode (paper-based and/or computer-based) to ensure 
that the content was accurate, and that the layout and functionality were working as intended.
For all instruments (irrespective of delivery mode), NRCs were asked to check for the following:
•	
Content is accurate and matches translations entered;
•	
Source version content is not displayed at all (unless this is intended);
•	
No adaptation brackets or html tags are displaying;
•	
National extensions are displaying appropriately; and
•	
All content is displayed appropriately (e.g. text not spilling outside of boxes/screen or stimulus material 
not spanning across pages in a printed document).
5.3 International verification processes 
Adaptation verification 
Following completion of adaptation and translation at the national centers, NRCs were required to consult 
with ISC staff for a final review of all proposed national adaptations (see Figure 5.1). In particular, they were 
strongly encouraged to discuss any adaptation that might result in a serious deviation from the international 
instruments. National centers began completing the NAF (Version I) after reviewing the international source 
version of the survey instruments and submitted the NAF for consultation with the ISC. Following its review, 
the ISC provided the national centers with feedback on their adaptations and, where appropriate, made 
suggestions for better alignment to the source version. Some of the common issues that were identified 
during adaptation review were:
•	
Incorrect completion of the NAF;
•	
Inconsistent adaptations used within or across test booklets or questionnaires;
•	
Inconsistent adaptations used in comparison to previous cycle;
•	
Difficulties in establishing country appropriate adaptations for ISCED levels;
•	
Difficulties identifying an appropriate set of subjects that are related to Civic and Citizenship education 
in the national context.
National centers were requested to take the recommendations into account and update the forms 
accordingly so that these updated forms (Version II) could be used during the translation verification 
process to evaluate the quality and accuracy of the translations.
Translation verification 
The national translations of the instruments underwent international translation verification. IEA oversaw 
the international translation verification process in coordination with an external translation verification 
company, cApStAn (based in Brussels, Belgium). The international translation verifiers were responsible for 
reviewing and documenting the quality and comparability of the national instruments to the international 
instruments. The required qualifications for verifiers included:
•	
Fluency in English;
•	
Mother tongue proficiency in the target language;
•	
Being a professional translator/interpreter/translation verifier; 
•	
A good level of computer literacy; and
•	
Residency in the target country, or close contact with the country and its culture.
Verifiers were trained through an online seminar, where they received detailed instructions for reviewing the 
survey instruments and documenting deviations from the international source version. They also received 
general information about the study and design of the instruments, together with a brief description of the 
translation procedures used by the national centers.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
70
Verifiers were provided with comprehensive materials to support their work. Each verifier received access 
to the relevant manuals and instruments in the online system, as well as a comprehensive set of directions, 
instructions and relevant examples for reviewing the national instruments and documenting deviations 
from the international source version. IEA and cApStAn also ensured all international translation verifiers 
received continuous support and provided the verifiers with constructive ongoing feedback as needed.
There were three modes of translation verification in the ICCS 2022 main survey: full verification of new 
translations, focused verification of translations which were already verified for the field trial and trend 
verification of trend translations. The instructions and training given to the verifiers emphasized the 
importance of maintaining the same meaning and difficulty level in the translations and adaptations as in the 
international source version and ensuring that translations and adaptations were adequate and consistent 
within and across national instruments and languages of administration. 
The international translation verification process involved:
•	
Checking the accuracy, linguistic correctness, and comparability of the translation and adaptations 
of the cognitive items and questionnaires, checking correct implementation of national adaptations, 
consistent use of words/terms listed in the style guide, and identicalness of translation with previously 
used translations (where applicable);
•	
Documenting any deviations (between the national and international source versions, or between new 
and previously used translations), including additions, deletions, and mistranslations; and
•	
Suggesting an alternative translation/adaptation to improve the accuracy and comparability of the 
national instruments. 
Materials undergoing focused verification for the main survey had already completed a full translation 
verification during the field trial. Therefore, this process was only applied to items where a translation 
export provided to verifiers by IEA (via cApStAn) indicated a change in the translation between the field trial 
and the main survey. 
Verifiers provided feedback from translation verification in both the AM online system and in the associated 
NAF. Verifiers were asked to correct the text of the cognitive test items and questionnaires and/or to 
add comments specifying errors using the edit and comment functions in AM Designer. Corrections or 
suggestions could be applied directly to the text elements in the online environment. Any changes made 
by the verifier were displayed as track changes in the text element. Using the comments, verifiers were 
required to document their corrections and suggestions, describe the issue, and provide an English back 
translation for every affected text element.
For materials undergoing trend verification, a key part of the verifiers’ feedback was focused on the changes 
in the translation of the items that were meant to be kept identical for the comparison over time. This 
involved:
•	
Checking that each of the trend items for the current cycle remained identical to the trend items as 
they were administered in the previous cycle; 
•	
Checking proposed changes to trend translations documented in the TVF, their adequacy and correct 
implementation; and
•	
Documenting any undocumented differences in trend translations in the TVF.
During translation verification, some of the typical errors identified by the verifiers included mistranslations, 
omissions/additions of text, inconsistent translations, and grammar. In the main survey, for some items the 
order of choices in multiple-choice items was wrong, as some countries mistakenly kept the order from the 
field trial. Some of the domain-specific concepts in civics and citizenship (e.g., “parliament”) and the ISCED 
levels were a particular challenge to adapt for some countries. Countries were informed of this and asked to 
address all discovered issues. Any adaptations reported in the NAF were also reviewed by the verifiers, who 
were asked to comment on their adequacy.

PREPARATION AND VERIFICATION OF THE ICCS 2022 STUDY INSTRUMENTS
71
With the documented comments and suggestions from the verifiers, NRCs were able to revise and improve 
their national versions. Each deviation of the translated text from the international source was allocated one 
of five codes that indicated to the NRCs the severity and type of deviation:
Code 1: Major Change or Error. Examples include an incorrect translation which changes the meaning 
or difficulty of the item; incorrect order of choices in a multiple-choice item; omission of a question; 
incorrect order of items/questions; deviations from the text of the link items to compare results across 
cycles (trend items); and agreed national adaptations not being implemented in the files. 
Code 1? was assigned where the verifier was in doubt over the severity of an error or where the verifier 
was unsure how to correct a possible error. It was also used when the verifier suggested corrections to 
national adaptations or trend items when the translation was incorrect.	
Code 2: Minor Change or Error. Examples include purely linguistic errors and spelling errors that do not 
affect comprehension.
Code 3: Suggestion for Alternative. The translation may be adequate, but this code indicates the verifier 
suggested a different wording.
Code OK: Acceptable Adaptation. The adaptation (either required, where <text is in angle brackets>, or 
nonrequired yet necessary) is acceptable and appropriate.
All issues that the verifiers deemed to be severe deviations from the source (i.e., Code 1), as well as all 
issues related to required adaptations, were documented in the NAF where they could be addressed by the 
country and reviewed by the ISC. 
After translation verification, IEA reviewed the verifiers’ feedback and returned the materials to the national 
centers. NRCs were responsible for finalizing their instruments and thus had to review the feedback 
carefully and consider the corrections and suggestions made by the verifier.
The procedure required NRCs to react to every comment the verifier made in the AM Designer, the NAF, 
and TVFs (where applicable). In principle, NRCs could accept, modify, or reject the verifier’s suggestion. 
For the latter two options, NRCs had to give an explanation for why they changed or disagreed with the 
suggestion. 
For issues involving adaptation implementation and trend translations, NRCs were further requested 
to discuss any undocumented or unclear changes with the international project team. The project team 
additionally assessed the appropriateness of each trend change (using the verifiers’ linguistic expertise and 
input) and advised the NRCs on the consequences of implementing the changes. This information supported 
NRCs in making their final decision to keep or reject the trend change. Information about trend changes 
across items and languages was later used as a reference during the analysis of main survey data. Findings 
from an analysis of the 121 documented trend translation changes showed no significant associations 
between those changes and DIF (differential item functioning) for the trend items. 
Layout verification 
Once translation verification had been completed, the ISC asked national centers to make any changes 
resulting from translation verification and notify the ISC that their student materials were ready for layout 
verification. Layout verification is the final external review of the assessment instruments and questionnaires, 
its main goal is to ensure comparability of assessment instruments across all countries. During this stage, 
the ISC checked each participating country’s materials to ensure they were appearing correctly, had the 
correct structure and function and importantly that they were comparable to the international source. 
For those CBA countries and benchmarking participants, layout verification also included reviewing their 
instruments in their final test forms to verify that all elements of the instruments including system login, 
orientation, section logins, scrolling reminders, navigation, progress bars, and filter rules all functioned and 
appeared correctly. 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
72
Countries using paper instruments had their instruments reviewed in AM. These were then exported from 
the platform into PDF format where they were reviewed for pagination and alignment with the international 
source and as well for formatting issues. Where countries were using both paper and computer-based 
instruments the final versions of both were compared to ensure consistency. 
The most common layout issues that were identified across the instruments were:
•	
Formatting issues (e.g., spacing, font size, ensuring consistency with international source version); 
•	
Issues with structural changes in the questionnaires; and
•	
Technical issues related to the translation system.
Some of the interactive units presented issues that required correction by the software developer team, 
especially for languages where the translated segments were much longer than the source. 
All inconsistencies were listed against the relevant section in the NAF and reported back to the national 
centers for their review and revision. Countries using the computer-based assessment were given test 
form logins to review their draft final forms and indicate any unresolved issues. While countries using paper 
forms were provided with their final forms in PDF format for final review.
This final review by the national center sometimes resulted in the identification of issues that required 
correction. These were mostly spelling corrections that were corrected in the platform by the national 
center and then the final instruments were reproduced and reviewed by the ISC before sending them back 
to the national center for final review. 
The production of instruments and layout verification were impacted to some degree by issues within the 
online system (see Chapter 4 for further details). Consequently, some layout issues that were intended to be 
fixed by national center staff, had to be fixed by staff from the ISC or from the software development team. 
The overall quality of the instruments was not impacted, but it did result in delays in finalizing instruments 
for some countries.
Quality observers’ review 
As a final step, international quality control observers reviewed the implementation of verification feedback 
after the data collection period and documented irregularities, if any. Furthermore, they reviewed the 
translations of a few crucial parts of the Test Administrator and School Coordinator manual and their 
alignment with the international source (for more details see Chapter 8 on quality assurance procedures).

SAMPLING DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION
73
Chapter 6:
Sampling design and implementation
Diego Cortes and Umut Atasever
6.1	Overview
To achieve accurate, precise, and internationally comparable estimates of the population parameter of 
interest, the International Civic and Citizenship Education Study (ICCS) aims to select high-quality random 
samples of students and teachers. This chapter describes the random sampling procedures used to select 
samples of teachers and students to participate in ICCS 2022. These were developed in accordance with the 
requirements for sampling quality outlined in the Technical Standards for IEA Studies (Martin et al., 1999).
The approach to sampling for ICCS 2022 followed a stratified two-stage random sampling design. During 
the first sampling stage, schools were sampled with probability proportional to their size, where size was 
defined as the number of students enrolled at school that belong to ICCS target population. Across sampled 
and participating schools, during the second stage, one or more intact class(es) of target-grade students 
were randomly selected for inclusion in the student survey. All classes within the same school had the same 
probability of being selected to participate. Further, a fixed number of target-grade teachers was randomly 
selected in each sampled and participating school for the teacher survey. This chapter provides a description 
of this sampling design, with special attention to the following topics:
•	
The definition of all relevant populations that were targeted in ICCS 2022;
•	
The definition of the parts of the population not covered by or excluded from ICCS 2022;
•	
International sample design with special attention to;
o	
Sample sizes,
o	
Sampling procedures (school and within-school selection).
An important feature of ICCS 2022 was the introduction of computer-based assessment (CBA) in this 
study (see Chapter 4). Countries were given the choice to administer ICCS using either computer-based 
or (traditional) paper-based delivery, with 18 out of the 24 participating countries or benchmarking entities 
opting for the former. Both versions of the assessment were developed to measure the same latent constructs 
and manifest indicators. Yet, the dual assessment mode presented us with two important challenges. First, 
it was important to measure the risk of, and control for, any systematic difference in student responses 
across the two administration modes. Second, to protect the measurement of trends from previous cycles, 
where appropriate, it was essential to adjust for potential mode effects (for more detail about this, please 
see Chapters 11 and 12). For these reasons, countries that participated in ICCS 2016 and also opted for 
the CBA in 2022 were asked to assess a separate sample of schools and/or students to provide ‘bridging’ 
data, which contributed to the ICCS 2022 Bridging Study. That is, the bridging sample represented the same 
student target population as the sample participating in the CBA but was administrated a paper version 
including a reduced version of test items (in a format that could be delivered in both modes) as well as the 
international and (where relevant) regional student questionnaire material. Since the bridging study was 
developed to inform about mode effects for student test and questionnaire data, it did not include the 
teacher survey. 
Overall, 11 out of the 18 countries administrating the computer-based version of the survey participated 
in the bridging study. These countries were: Bulgaria, Colombia, Estonia, Italy, Lithuania, Latvia, Malta, 
Norway, Slovenia, Sweden, and Chinese Taipei. This chapter also provides information about how schools 
and students were selected to participate in the bridging study.
6.2	Target population definitions
When undertaking a quantitative study, it is essential to clearly define the target population that is under 
study. Survey estimates derived from a random sample allow drawing conclusions about the population 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
74
from which the sample was selected. As ICCS 2022 was designed as both a student survey and a teacher 
survey, two distinct target populations had to be defined. 
Student target population
As in the two previous cycles, ICCS 2022 defined the target population of students as follows (see, for 
example, Weber, 2018):
The student target population in ICCS consists of all students enrolled in the grade that represents 
eight years of schooling, counting from the first year of ISCED Level 1,1 providing the mean age at 
the time of testing is at least 13.5 years. Students older than 17 years are not part of the target 
population. 
For most countries, the target grade was the eighth grade, or its national equivalent. If the average age 
in Grade 8 was below 13.5 in a country, generally because students started formal schooling at age five, 
the target grade became Grade 9. To ensure international comparability, the ICCS national research 
coordinators (NRCs) had to specify their country’s legal school entry age, the name of the target grade, and 
an estimate of the mean age of the students in that grade. As in the previous cycle, Norway assessed Grade 
9 instead of Grade 8 and its results were annotated in the ICCS 2022 reporting tables.
Students who were not covered by the definition above were regarded as “out of scope” (i.e., students in 
a different grade than the target grade). In the following sections, the term “students” is used to describe 
“students in the ICCS 2022 target population.”
Teacher target population
As in previous cycles, ICCS 2022 defined the target population of teachers as follows (see, for example, 
Weber, 2018):
The teacher target population in ICCS consists of all teachers teaching regular school subjects to students 
of the target grade. Teachers are defined as school staff members who provide student instruction through 
the delivery of lessons to students. Teachers may work with students as a whole class in a classroom, in 
small groups in resource rooms, or one-to-one inside or outside of classrooms. Teachers who have joined 
a school after the beginning of the school year are not part of the target population.
This definition included all teachers teaching regular school subjects to students of the target grade 
(regardless of the subject or the number of hours taught) during the ICCS 2022 testing period.
School staff from the following categories were not part of the target population (i.e., were out of scope):
•	
Staff attending to the needs of the target-grade students but not teaching any lessons (e.g., psychological 
counsellors, chaplains, etc.);
•	
Assistant teachers and parent-helpers;
•	
Non-staff teachers teaching (non-compulsory) subjects not in the curriculum (e.g., cases where religion, 
although not a regular subject, was being taught by external persons).
In the following sections, the term “teachers” will be used to describe “teachers of students in the ICCS 
2022 target population.”
6.3	Coverage and exclusions
Population coverage
The ICCS 2022 international sampling team encouraged all participating countries and benchmarking entities 
to include in the study all students and teachers that were covered by the target population definition. However, 
countries could choose to remove larger groups of schools, students, or teachers from the target population 
for political, operational, or administrative reasons. This is referred to as reduced population coverage. Note 
that in ICCS 2022 all countries and benchmarks were considered to have full coverage.
1	 ISCED stands for International Standard Classification of Education (UNESCO, 2011).

SAMPLING DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION
75
Student exclusions
In most ICCS 2022 countries, smaller groups of students had to be removed from the target population for 
practical reasons, such as difficult test conditions or increased survey costs. Such removals were regarded as 
exclusions. Some students were excluded because their entire school was excluded (school-level exclusions). 
Other students were excluded within sampled and participating schools (within-sample exclusions).
The overall exclusion rate was estimated as the sum of the school-level exclusion rate and the within-
school-level exclusion rate. The former was calculated based on information provided by the NRCs prior to 
sampling. Whereas the within-school-level exclusion rate was estimated using the data collected for ICCS 
2022 across participating schools. Each country was required to keep the overall rate of excluded students 
below five percent of the target population. Four participating countries exceeded this limit, and their results 
were annotated in the ICCS 2022 reporting tables.
Prior to the selection of the school sample, national centers had to define the group of schools that had 
to be excluded in their respective national contexts from the ICCS 2022 student survey. However, the 
within-sample exclusions could only consist of students with physical or mental disabilities, or students who 
could not speak the language of the test (typically, students with less than one year of instruction in the test 
language). NRCs were instructed on how to define and identify potential groups of students to be excluded. 
The following definitions where shared with all countries and benchmark entities:
•	
Intellectually disabled students—These are students who are considered in the professional opinion 
of the school principal, or by other qualified staff members, to be intellectually disabled or who have 
been tested psychologically as such. This includes students who are emotionally or mentally unable to 
follow even the general instructions of the test. Students should not be excluded solely because of poor 
academic performance or normal disciplinary problems.
•	
Functionally disabled students—These are students who are permanently physically disabled in such a 
way that they cannot perform in the ICCS testing situation. Functionally disabled students who are able 
to respond should be included in the testing.
•	
Non-native language speakers—These are students who are unable to read or speak the language(s) 
of the test and would be unable to overcome the language barrier of the test. Typically, a student who 
has received less than one year of instruction in the language(s) of the test should be excluded, but this 
definition may need to be adapted in different countries.
Any other types of within-school student exclusions were not permitted. Details about the exclusion 
categories for each country can be found in Appendix B (Characteristics of National Samples) of this report.
Across the 11 countries that assessed a bridging sample, the same set of school- and student-level exclusion 
categories used were used as for the bridging study. 
Teacher exclusions
Unlike the context described above about the student survey, there was no intention to exclude teachers 
from the ICCS 2022 teacher survey. If a teacher was part of the teacher target population, they were eligible 
to participate in the study. Therefore, no minimum exclusion rates for teachers were specified. However, 
exclusions of schools from the survey also had an effect on the coverage of the teacher population. Each 
country was asked to provide information about the proportion of teachers in excluded schools. Because 
statistics about teachers per grade are rarely available, some countries could not provide exact figures, only 
rough estimates, or no estimates at all. Therefore, it was not possible to systematically collect information 
about exclusion rates for the ICCS 2022 teacher survey.
Overall exclusions
Table 6.1 shows the population coverage and the exclusion rates for the student survey across all countries 
participating in ICCS 2022. Since the set of countries assessing a bridging sample used the same exclusion 
criteria in the core survey as in the bridging study, its exclusion rates were estimated using the same 
population parameter. Therefore, Table 6.1 does not distinguish between exclusion rates for ICCS 2022 
core survey and the bridging study.

6.4	Sample size requirements
ICCS has strict standards about sample sizes, defined as the number or expected number of participating 
units. Where units can be schools, as well as students or teachers within participating schools. Importantly, 
the same standards, which are explained below, applied for the bridging study.
Sample size in the student survey
The overall goal of the student sample design was to achieve an effective sample size of at least 400 students 
for the main survey outcome. This means that the complex sample design used in ICCS 2022 should achieve 
the same sampling precision as a hypothetical simple random sample of 400 students. The loss in precision 
associated to a complex sampling design is known as design effect. Importantly, one of the main drivers of the 
design effect in ICCS is a result of the two-stage sampling design, as students from the same schools tend to 
be more alike than students from different schools. Therefore, to have robust estimates it was necessary to 
survey a far larger number of actual students to achieve this goal. 
The civic knowledge score and questionnaire scales reflecting civic-related indicators of attitudes and 
engagement were regarded as main survey outcomes. Given the international metric for these scales, the 
minimum requirements for sample precision were roughly equivalent to obtaining standard errors for civic 
knowledge scores that did not exceed 5.0 score points and for questionnaire scales that did not exceed 0.5 
score points. 

SAMPLING DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION
77
The ICCS sampling team asked, with respect to the ICCS 2022 student survey, each participating country 
to have a minimum school sample size of 150 selected schools. This meant selecting at least one intact class 
from each school. Once non-participation of schools and students had been considered, these requirements 
were expected to result in about 3,000 surveyed students. 
Countries or benchmarking entities with fewer than 150 eligible schools included all schools in the survey. 
In several countries, more than 150 schools were selected. Increases in sample size were implemented for 
different reasons: 
•	
As shown in previous student surveys, variation in student achievement across schools in a country can 
be large depending on the structure of the education system (e.g., due to segregation). Evidence from 
previous studies in ICCS 2022 countries was used to determine whether the standards for sampling 
precision could only be met by increasing the school sample size; 
•	
The average class size in a country was so small that it was not possible to achieve the student sample 
size requirement of 3,000 students based on a sample of 150 schools; 
•	
The NRC requested a sample-size increase in order to increase the amount of data available for analysis 
(e.g., through oversampling of certain sectors in the education system).
Because of non-participation, school closures, and inaccuracies in the school sampling frame, the achieved 
sample size of schools tended to be smaller than the intended sample size in most of the countries. 
In each sampled school, at least one classroom of the target grade was selected. In some countries, more than 
one classroom was selected. This was done because:
•	
The total number of schools in a country was so small that the student sample size requirements could 
not be met by selecting only one classroom per school;
•	
The NRC had asked to select two classes to allow doing class-level variance analysis;
•	
Large sampling weight fluctuations were expected when sampling only one classroom per school.
Each country was required to have about 3,000 participating and tested students. Because of non-
response, school closures, decreasing student populations or other reasons, some countries did not meet 
this requirement. The ICCS sampling team did not regard this outcome as problematic provided the country 
met the overall participation rate requirements.
Countries participating in the bridging study required a sample size of 1,500 students. Two different strategies 
were offered to coordinate the selection of students participating in the core survey and the bridging study. 
The first one was to include a set of schools to participate in the core survey and a distinct set of schools to 
participate in the bridging study. This meant there should not be any overlap between the two school samples. 
The second option was to include schools either in the core survey or bridging study, with the possibility of 
assigning one classroom to the bridging and one or more to the core survey in larger schools. This second 
option meant a partial overlap between both school samples. It is important to note that there were no 
classrooms or students that were selected to participate in both the core survey and bridging study. Chinese 
Taipei, Colombia, and Slovenia opted for the design with a partial overlap between the two school samples, 
while all other countries participating in the bridging study using separate school sample.
Sample size in the teacher survey
The school sample size requirements for the ICCS 2022 teacher survey were the same as those for the 
student survey. Within each selected school, a minimum teacher sample size of 15 teachers was required. 
In schools with fewer than 15 teachers, all teachers were included in the survey. If the number of eligible 
teachers was higher than 15, but fewer than or equal to 20, all teachers were selected to prevent a situation 
where only a few teachers were not included in the survey. Teacher samples sizes were determined similarly 
as for students; that is, the effect of the sample design was considered, and the sample size determined to 
achieve a similar precision level as a hypothetical simple random sample of 400 teachers.
Some NRCs requested that all teachers in sampled schools who were teaching civic-related subjects be part 
of the national teacher sample. Other countries expressed the wish to select all home-class teachers in a 
sampled school. In both cases, teachers from these groups were sampled with certainty.

6.5	School sampling design
Stratification of schools
Prior to sampling, the population of schools was stratified. That is, schools sharing some observable 
characteristics were put into mutually exclusive groups. Generally, ICCS 2022 used stratification for the 
following two reasons: 
•	
To improve the efficiency of the sample design, the national centers were asked to provide stratification 
variables that were expected to be closely associated with ICCS 2022 outcomes;
•	
To apply different sample designs, such as disproportionate sample allocations, to specific groups of 
schools (e.g., states or provinces).
ICCS applied two different methods of stratification—one explicit, the other implicit:
•	
If explicit strata were used, the total sample of schools was apportioned to the explicit strata, and 
independent samples of schools were selected from each explicit stratum;
•	
Implicit strata were used to sort schools within explicit strata. 
The combined use of implicit strata and systematic sampling is a way of ensuring a proportional sample 
allocation of schools across all implicit strata. Each country applied different stratification schemes 
after discussion with IEA sampling team members. Appendix B of this report provides details about the 
stratification variables for each participant.
Countries transitioning to CBA implemented the same stratification design in the bridging study as in the 
core survey.
School sampling frame
To prepare the selection of schools that should participate in ICCS 2022, the IEA sampling team asked 
national centers to provide a list of all schools with students enrolled in the target grade. This comprehensive 
national list of all eligible schools is called a school sampling frame. The team carefully verified that all school-
sampling frames provided complete coverage of the target population and did not include incorrect entries, 
duplicate entries, or entries that referred to elements that were not part of the target population. The team 
then corroborated the plausibility of the information against official statistics.
For each eligible school in the sampling frame, the sampling team required the following information:
•	
A unique identifier, such as a national identification number;
•	
A measure of size (MOS) of the school, which usually was the number of students in the target grade or 
an adjacent grade; 
•	
Information about the intended stratification variables.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
80
Schools sample selection
To select the school samples for the ICCS 2022, the sampling team used stratified systematic sampling, where 
the probability of being selected for each school was proportional to its size. As noted earlier, this method is 
customary in most large-scale social surveys, and notably in most IEA surveys.
The process of selecting the school samples of each country started with the sorting of the school sampling 
frame. Within each explicit stratum, schools were sorted by implicit strata, and finally within each implicit 
stratum by MOS (alternately sorted in increasing and decreasing order).
The team next selected a sample from the sorted school sampling frame with the following steps:
•	
Calculating a sampling interval in each explicit stratum, this interval was defined by dividing the total 
MOS in this stratum by the number of schools to be sampled from that stratum;
•	
Determining a random starting point in each explicit stratum, defined as the product of the sampling 
interval times the value of a random draw from a standard uniform distribution. The school with the 
cumulative MOS that was equal or larger to the corresponding product was the first sampled school;
•	
Selecting schools by adding the sampling interval to the point of the random start and then subsequently 
to each new value every time a school was selected. Whenever the cumulated MOS was equal or larger 
the corresponding value, the team selected the corresponding unit.
Figure 6.1 visualizes the process explained above within an explicit stratum. In this diagram, a box represents a 
school in the sampling frame. The schools in the sampling frame are sorted in descending order by MOS. The 
height of the cells reflects the number of target-grade students in each school. A random start determines 
the second school in the list for selection, and a constant sampling interval determines the next two sampled 
schools. Sampled schools are displayed in blue.
Figure 6.1: Systematic selection of schools with probability proportional to their size
In certain cases, the team systematically deviated from this general procedure. If very small schools are 
selected with probability proportional to size (PPS), there is a risk of obtaining extremely large sampling 
weights for students from those schools and therefore substantially increasing the variability of the 
weights. To prevent this, it was necessary to select small schools with equal selection probabilities. The ICCS 
team regarded a school as small if the number of students enrolled in the target grade was lower than the 
number enrolled in a class of average size in the school’s explicit stratum. Conversely, technical problems 
arise whenever the MOS of a school is larger than the sampling interval. In this case, the sampling team set 
the MOS of the school to the sampling interval, thereby ensuring that the school would be selected with 
certainty but not more than once.

SAMPLING DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION
81
Most ICCS 2022 countries conducted an extensive field trial of the study instruments prior to the main 
data-collection phase. Had a school been selected both for the field trial and for the main survey, this could 
have caused response contamination and a drop in the participation rate for the main survey. The schools, or 
the teachers within the schools, might have been reluctant to participate in both the field trial and the main 
survey. Selecting the same school for both parts of the study was therefore avoided, whenever possible. For 
many countries, this overlap control involved selecting the main survey sample and the field trial sample 
simultaneously. 
The sampling team selected a sample of replacement schools while selecting the primary sample of schools. 
Generally, two replacement schools with similar characteristics were assigned to each originally sampled 
school. The similarity was secured by selecting those two schools adjacent to the sampled school in the 
sorted sampling frame. That is, replacement schools were similar to originally sampled schools in terms of 
size and stratum affiliation. The first replacement school was the one below the sampled school; the second 
replacement school was the one above. Schools that were part of the original sample could not be selected as 
replacement schools. Replacement schools were used to ensure that enough information about the target 
population was collected for ICCS 2022. However, it was emphasized that collecting data within the set 
of originally sampled schools was a priority, as replacing these with (similar) replacement schools required 
making very strong assumptions about distributions.
For countries using CBA that had participated in the previous cycle, the samples of schools for the core 
and the bridging study were drawn simultaneously. In a first step, a relatively large sample of schools was 
drawn. Then, sampled schools were randomly allocated to participate either in the core survey, the bridging 
study, or both (if applicable). For example, a country may have required 150 schools to be sampled for the 
core survey, 75 schools to be sampled for the bridging study and required no school overlap between both 
studies. Moreover, let us assume an average class size of 20 students. Under this scenario, 225 schools 
were sampled in a first step, while in a second step, 75 schools were randomly allocated to participate in the 
bridging study, and the remaining to participate in the ICCS 2022 core survey.
6.6	Within school sampling design
Within-school sampling constituted the second step of the sampling process in ICCS. The NRCs or their 
appointed data managers carried out the selection of classes and teachers. The use of WinW3S software in 
each participating country ensured the random selection of classes and teachers within the sampled schools. 
Student sampling
One or more classes from each participating school were included in the ICCS 2022 assessments. All 
participating schools were asked to list all their target-grade classes and to provide this list to their ICCS 
2022 national study center. Center staff then used WinW3S software to select the classes from these lists. 
Sampled classes could not be replaced or substituted. However, center staff could exclude a class from 
selection if it consisted solely of excluded students.
Systematic sampling was used for selecting classes from lists provided by the participating schools. The 
procedure was similar to the one used for systematic school sampling except that each class in a school 
had the same probability of being selected. Each student in a participating school had the same selection 
probability because all students within sampled classes were selected for participation in ICCS 2022.
Whenever a class was smaller than half of the average class size, it was grouped with one or more other 
classes prior to sample selection to form a so-called pseudo-class. This was done to avoid fluctuations in the 
total student sample size and to ensure efficient use of study resources.
An additional within-school sampling step was required for countries transitioning to CBA and that chose to 
have an overlap between the core survey and the bridging study school samples. In such cases, some schools 
were selected to participate in both studies. Countries were asked to use WinW3S to randomly allocate 
sampled classes to participate in the core survey or in the bridging study.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
82
Teacher sampling
WinW3S software employed systematic sampling with equal selection probabilities to select teachers from 
lists provided by the participating schools. To ensure a proportional allocation of teachers by gender, implicit 
stratification was applied when using WinW3S software to sample teachers.
As mentioned above, it was possible to select specific groups of teachers with certainty. The sampling team 
accounted for higher selection probabilities of these teachers when conducting weight calculations.

FIELD OPERATIONS PROCEDURES
83
Chapter 7:
Field operations procedures
Hannah Kowolik and Falk Brese
7.1	Overview
Successful administration of the International Civic and Citizenship Education Study (ICCS) 2022 depended 
heavily on the contributions of the study’s national research coordinators (NRCs) and national center staff. 
To assist the national centers and to promote consistency in the study administration across countries, the 
ICCS international study center (ISC) at the Australian Council for Educational Research (ACER), as well as 
the associate research center at Lumsa University worked together with the International Association for 
the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) to develop internationally standardized field operations 
procedures. The ICCS operations procedures were designed to be flexible enough to meet the needs of 
individual participants whilst maintaining IEA’s high quality survey standards.
The ICCS operations procedures were developed with reference to those used in ICCS 2016 and those used 
in other previous IEA studies, such as IEA’s Progress in International Reading Literacy Study (PIRLS), Trends 
in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS), and International Computer and Information 
Literacy Study (ICILS). The procedures were tailored to suit the specific requirements of ICCS 2022. The 
2022 cycle of ICCS marked the transition to computer-based assessment, with participating countries having 
the option of using paper-based administration (PBA) or the new computer-based administration (CBA) of 
the student instruments. As occurred in previous cycles of ICCS, the teacher and school questionnaires 
could be delivered on computer or on paper. 
Adapting operational procedures from the existing ICCS operations and integrating the CBA operations 
to create a workflow for this new assessment mode was a significant undertaking. In order to control for 
any assessment mode effects, countries transitioning to CBA were required to administer “bridge” paper 
instruments to an equivalent sample of students, in addition to their nationally representative sample of 
students. This bridge data collection also required integrating operations and procedures into the overall 
ICCS 2022 assessment administration.
All national centers received guidelines on survey operations procedures for each stage of the study. These 
guidelines provided advice on contacting schools, listing and sampling students, preparing materials for 
data collection, administering the assessment, scoring and coding the assessment, and creating the data 
files. National centers were also supplied with procedures for quality control and provided feedback on 
survey activities, gathered via online questionnaires.
7.2	Field operations personnel
National research coordinators
One of the first steps in establishing the ICCS study was the appointment of an NRC (or NRCs) by each 
participating country or education system.1 The NRC acted as the contact person for all those involved in 
ICCS within the country and was the country representative at the international level.
NRCs were in charge of the overall implementation of the study within their countries, had responsibilities 
for the national decisions regarding ICCS and, where necessary, implemented and adapted internationally 
agreed-upon procedures for the national context under the guidance of the international project staff and 
national experts.
1	 The majority of the entities that participated in ICCS were countries. Further, two subunits of countries featuring a distinct 
education system also participated in ICCS: The German states North Rhine-Westphalia and Schleswig-Holstein. For reasons 
of simplicity, the text will refer to both participating countries and education systems as “countries.”

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
84
School coordinators and test administrators
School coordinators and test administrators were key to successful administration of ICCS. Their 
work involved preparing for the test administration in schools and carrying out the data collection in a 
standardized way.
In cooperation with school principals, national centers identified and trained school coordinators for all 
participating schools. The school coordinator could be a teacher or other staff member in a school. The 
school coordinator could also be the test administrator at a school, but was not to be a teacher of any of 
the sampled students. In some cases, national centers appointed external school coordinators, for example 
from their own staff. Each school coordinator was provided with an ICCS school coordinator manual, which 
described their responsibilities in detail and encouraged them to contact the NRC if they had any questions.
The school coordinators’ responsibilities included the following major tasks:
•	
Identifying eligible students and teachers belonging to the target populations to allow the national 
center to perform within-school sampling;
•	
Arranging the date(s) and modalities of the test administration with the national center;
•	
Distribute parental permission forms including data protection declarations (if necessary), and ensure 
that they are signed and returned;
•	
Distributing instruments and related materials needed for test administration and making sure they 
were kept in a secure place and confidential at all times;
•	
Working with school principals, the test administrators, and the affected teachers to plan and administer 
the student testing;
•	
Coordinating the completion of the student tracking forms and teacher tracking forms; and 
•	
Ensuring that the test administrators return all testing materials after the testing session.
For CBA countries, the following additional steps had to be undertaken by the school coordinators:
•	
Running a hardware and software system check on computers intended to be used for the testing, 
using an ICCS Compatibility Check Program (on USB) and/or a check to confirm whether or not the 
test delivery system could be accessed online (for internet-based delivery). School coordinators were 
provided with the “Preparing computers for ICCS: Instructions for School Coordinators” manual to 
support with that task;
•	
Completing the CBA school resources survey (contained in the “Preparing Computers for ICCS” 
manual); and
•	
Arranging for any technical configuration and setup of computers in the school to run the test.
Test administrators were mainly responsible for administering the student test and questionnaires. They 
were employed either by the national center or directly by the schools. Training sessions, run by the national 
center centrally or by the schools, ensured that test administrators were adequately prepared to run the 
assessment sessions, applying a test administration protocol standardized across countries (see section 
Administering the ICCS assessment for more details on the administration procedures).
7.3	Field operations resources
Manuals and documentation
The ICCS survey operations procedures manuals (SOP) were sent to the NRCs in five units, each 
accompanied by additional materials, including more specialized manuals and software packages. The units 
and materials were organized and distributed chronologically according to the different stages of the study.
The five units were as follows:
•	
Unit 1: Sampling Schools defined the ICCS target populations and sampling goals and described the 
procedures for the sampling of schools.
•	
Unit 2: Working With Schools guided the national centers through the process of contacting and working 
with schools (such as obtaining permission from relevant school authorities, establishing contact with 
schools, and identifying and training school coordinators).

FIELD OPERATIONS PROCEDURES
85
•	
Unit 3: Preparing National Study Instruments described the processes involved in preparing the survey 
materials (such as translating and adapting assessment materials, documenting national adaptations 
made to the instruments, preparing for the international verification of translated materials and 
adaptations, preparing for the international verification of the instrument layout, and duplicating 
materials for the assessment).
•	
Unit 4: Data Collection and Quality Monitoring Procedures described the activities involved in working 
with the sampled schools and guided the NRCs through the data collection procedures, including 
the within-school sampling process, the tasks related to test administration, administration of the 
instruments in schools, and the quality monitoring procedures.
•	
Unit 5: Preparing for Scoring, Data Capture, and Parental Occupation Coding described the processes 
around scoring procedures including recruiting and training scorers, manual data capture, parental 
occupations coding procedures, data processing and verification, and final data submission to IEA 
Hamburg. 
Accompanying manuals were as follows:
•	
The School Coordinator Manual described the role and responsibilities of the school coordinator as 
the main contact person within each participating school. The responsibilities included assisting the 
national center in the identification of classes, teachers, and students, supporting the administration of 
the test and questionnaires, and ensuring that test materials in the school are secure and confidential 
at all times. In ICCS 2022 five different possible configurations of test administration modes in schools 
were possible: 
	o
Paper-based: In case of solely paper-based administration in the country. 
	o
Computer-based: In case of the computer-based administration in the country, without 
administering the bridging study.
	o
Computer-based AND paper-based (bridging study): In case of the computer-based 
administration in the country, including administering the bridging study. The respective school 
has been selected for the computer-based and paper-based administration.
	o
Computer-based only (bridging study): In case of the computer-based administration in the 
country, including administering the bridging study. The respective school has been selected for 
the computer-based administration but NOT for the paper-based.
	o
Paper-based only (bridging study): In case of the computer-based administration in the country, 
including administering the bridging study. The respective school has been selected ONLY for 
paper-based administration.
For each scenario a specific manual was provided that included guidelines and information for the respective 
administration mode (for the computer-based and the computer-based only (bridging study) configuration the 
same manuals were used).
•	
Preparing computers for ICCS: Instructions for School Coordinators described the tasks for testing 
and preparing computers for the computer-based ICCS assessment administration, including some 
technical troubleshooting scenarios. 
•	
The Test Administrator Manual described the role and responsibilities of the test administrator, 
including the distribution of the student test instruments according to the student tracking forms, the 
supervision of test sessions, ensuring that the timing of the test sessions was correct, and recording 
student participation. In ICCS 2022 five different possible configurations of test administration 
modes in schools were possible (see the description of The School Coordinator Manual above). For each 
scenario a specific manual was provided that included guidelines and information for the respective 
administration mode.
•	
The International and National Quality Control Monitor Manuals provided international quality 
observers (IQOs) and national quality observers with information about ICCS and described their 
role and responsibilities in the project. The manuals specified the timelines, actions, and procedures 
required to carry out the international and national quality assurance programs.
•	
Supplement 1 to SOP Unit 5- Assessment Master™: Monitoring Participation and Exporting Data 
Availability Status for IEA WinW3S provided instructions for national monitoring of the computer-

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
86
based data collection. It described (i) how to export information from the RM Assessment Master™ 
for import into the IEA WinW3S and (ii) how to monitor computer-based data collection within the RM 
Assessment Master™.
•	
Assessment Master™ Manual provided technical information on how to use the RM Australia Assessment 
Master™ throughout the process of preparing national instruments (translation and translation 
verification, prepare instruments for paper- and computer-based administration and administer the 
instruments in the CBA platform).
•	
Supplement 1 to Assessment Master™ Manual – Exporting PDFs described how to generate and edit the 
instruments and export them as PDFs.
•	
Main Survey Scoring Software Manual provided information about how to conduct the online scoring 
of constructed-response items using the AM Marker component of the Assessment Master™ system.
•	
Scoring Guides for Constructed-response Items provided detailed and explicit guidance on scoring each 
constructed-response item.
Software
To digitally deliver the student test, as well as the student, teacher, and school questionnaires, ICCS 2022 
made use of the Assessment Master™, an assessment platform owned by RM Results (see Chapter 4 for 
further details). National center staff could also use Assessment Master™ to monitor the upload of data. 
Countries also were provided with compatibility check software for checking the suitability of computer 
devices for ICCS 2022 for the intended delivery mode. 
In addition to the computer-based delivery software and consistent with the goal of automating and 
streamlining procedures wherever possible, IEA Hamburg provided NRCs with custom-built software 
packages to assist with sampling and data collection for PBA:
•	
The Windows® Within-school Sampling Software (WinW3S) enabled ICCS national centers to select 
students and teachers in each sampled school in agreement with sample design specifications and 
mandatory sampling algorithms. National centers also used the software to track school, teacher, and 
student information, prepare the survey tracking forms, and to assign test instruments to students; the 
software included facilities to print labels for all paper-based test booklets and questionnaires.
•	
The IEA Data Management Expert (DME) was used to enter information from any paper-based test 
booklets and questionnaires into computer data files. The DME included an international codebook, 
providing specific information on the variables in each of the ICCS 2022 civic knowledge test and 
questionnaire data files. National centers were required to adapt the international codebook structure 
to reflect any approved adaptations made to the national questionnaires.
In addition to the software and manuals, IEA conducted a field operations training seminar in advance of both 
the field trial and main survey. These seminars were designed to train national center staff on all procedures 
and familiarize them with the supporting software, namely the IEA WinW3S, and IEA Data Management 
Expert software.
7.4	Field operations processes
Survey listing and tracking forms
ICCS relied on a series of survey listing and tracking forms to sample classes and teachers, assign booklets 
and questionnaires, and track the participation status of students and teachers. These forms facilitated the 
data collection and data verification processes, and provided the information needed to compute sampling 
weights.
Most of the listing and tracking forms were created automatically by the IEA WinW3S software, then 
completed by schools (as arranged by the school coordinators) and returned to the national centers. The 
following six listing and tracking forms were used in ICCS:
•	
School sampling frame: The IEA Sampling Team provided national centers with an Excel file, listing the 
sampled schools and their replacements, the school identification code, a measure of school size, and 
stratification information. The file was used to track school participation.

FIELD OPERATIONS PROCEDURES
87
•	
Class listing form: A class listing form was created in WinW3S for each sampled school and sent to the 
school coordinators for completion. The school coordinators listed the eligible classes from the target 
grade in the participating schools and provided details about the classes, such as the class streams (if 
applicable), class names, and number of students. The number of teachers teaching students from the 
target grade was also specified on the class listing form.
•	
Student listing form: This form was created in WinW3S for each sampled class and sent to the schools 
for completion by the school coordinator prior to test administration. The school coordinators listed 
the eligible target grade students in the sampled classes of the participating schools and provided 
details about the students, such as the name of the student (if country regulations allowed for names to 
be provided to the national center), date of birth, gender, and exclusion status (if applicable).
•	
Student tracking form: This form was created in WinW3S and sent to the schools for completion by 
the test administrators during test administration. The test administrators used this form to verify 
the assignment of instruments to students and indicate student participation and the use of spare 
instruments.
•	
Teacher listing form: This form was created in WinW3S for each sampled school and sent to the schools 
for completion by the school coordinator prior to test administration. The school coordinators listed 
the eligible target grade teachers of the participating schools, and provided details about the teachers, 
such as their name (if country regulations allowed for names to be provided to the national center), 
birth year, and gender, and indicated whether the teacher needed to be sampled with certainty (an 
option allowing more precise estimates for teachers of civic and citizenship education related subjects 
at the target grade).
•	
Teacher tracking form: This form was created in WinW3S and sent to the school coordinators together 
with the teacher questionnaires. The school coordinators used this form to indicate the completion and 
return status of the teacher questionnaires.
Linking students to classes and schools and teachers to schools
The international project staff established a system assigning unique hierarchical identification codes (IDs) 
to the sampled schools, teachers, and students (see Table 7.1), enabling them to be tracked throughout the 
study. 
Every sampled student was assigned an eight-digit identification number unique within each country. Each 
number consisted of the four-digit identification number identifying the school, followed by a two-digit 
number identifying the class within the school, and a two-digit number identifying the student within the 
class.
Each sampled teacher within each sampled school (i.e., those listed on the teacher tracking form) was 
assigned a teacher identification number consisting of the four-digit school number followed by a two-digit 
teacher number, unique within the school.
