Contacting schools and sampling classes
Once NRCs had obtained a list of the schools that had been sampled for ICCS, (for more information on 
all sampling procedures, please refer to Chapter 6), it was important for the ongoing success of the study 
that good working relationships with these schools were established. NRCs were responsible for contacting 
these schools and encouraging them to take part in the assessment, which often involved obtaining support 
from national or regional educational authorities, depending on the national context. In cooperation with 
school principals, national centers identified school coordinators for all participating schools (see section 
Field operations personnel of this Chapter for details).

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
88
National centers sent a class listing form to each school coordinator and asked them to provide information 
on all the eligible target grade classes in the school. Using this information, the national centers sampled 
classes within the schools. Intact classes had to be sampled, ensuring that classes are mutually exclusive 
and exhaustive groups of students, that is, every eligible student in the school was allocated to exactly one, 
and only one class (course). This requirement formed the basis for obtaining a random sample of classes 
that was representative for all target grade students at the school. Once the class sampling was finalized, 
the national centers sent the teacher listing form and student listing form to the school coordinator. The 
school coordinator was asked to provide information for each single teacher that taught students in the 
target grade at the time of the assessment. Finally, school coordinators were asked to complete the student 
listing form, recording the student’s name, date of birth, gender, and whether the student was excluded from 
testing.2
The national centers worked with schools to sample classes, track schools, teachers, and students, and 
prepare for test administration (Figure 7.1).
2	 There were strict criteria for the exclusion of students. Excluded students may include students with functional disabilities, 
students with intellectual disabilities, and non-native language speakers (as described in the ICCS 2022 school coordinator 
manual).

Although all students enrolled in the sampled classes were part of the target population, ICCS recognized 
that students with a functional or intellectual disability, or students with little experience of using the target 
language may be excluded from participation. Accordingly, the sampling guidelines allowed the exclusion of 
students (i) with severe disabilities, unable to follow even the general instructions of the test or making it 
impossible to perform the ICCS testing situation, and (ii) non-native language speakers who are unable to read 
or speak the language(s) of the test and would be unable to overcome the language barrier of the test. National 
centers were asked to translate these guidelines to their contexts, in order to enable school coordinators to 
apply the definitions in a standardized way in all countries. Countries were required to track and account for 
all excluded students and were cautioned that excluding more than five percent of students would require an 
annotation of their results in the ICCS reports. Conditions under which countries excluded students were 
carefully documented, because the definition of being disabled could vary from country to country.
Preparing the instruments for data collection
ICCS 2022 Survey Operation Procedures Unit 3 provided countries with guidelines and explicit instructions 
on how to prepare the survey materials and thus produce good quality instruments. These included 
adapting the instruments to the national context and translating all instruments to the language(s) used for 
administration.

FIELD OPERATIONS PROCEDURES
91
As outlined in Chapter 5, NRCs were required to document any national adaptations to the international 
instruments in national adaptation forms (NAFs) and submit them to the ISC for review and further 
discussion. These NAFs were provided in Microsoft Excel format and included all question texts (e.g., 
question stem, response options, and answer categories) and variable names.
Subsequently, national centers managed the translation of the instruments from English into the language(s) 
used in the participating countries and later submitted them for independent verification (see Chapter 5 
for details). Following the translation verification and revision of the instruments, the instruments were 
finalized for administration.
For the PBA national center staff assembled the final instruments. The ISC undertook the final layout 
verification of the instruments, and national centers were asked to revise them, where necessary, prior to 
printing.
The CBA option allowed countries to administer all ICCS instruments in digital format, entailing the creation 
of so-called “players” as applications to administer the ICCS student instruments. The computer-based 
instruments for students could be administered in three different ways: (i) on USB sticks; (ii) online using 
a common internet browser; and (iii) via a local network server. For the school and teacher questionnaires, 
participating countries could choose to administer these online instead of using paper-based questionnaires.
To ensure the comparability of the data from the computer-based and online mode, only those countries that 
had field trialed it were allowed to use the computer-based/online option during the main survey. Eighteen 
countries and benchmark participants administered the computer-based student instruments for the ICCS 
main survey: Bulgaria, Brazil, Chinese Taipei, Colombia, Cyprus, Estonia, France, North Rhine-Westphalia 
(Germany), Schleswig-Holstein (Germany), Italy, Lithuania, Latvia, Malta, Norway, Slovak Republic, Slovenia, 
Spain, and Sweden. Twenty countries administered school and teacher questionnaires online during the 
ICCS main survey: Bulgaria, Brazil, Chinese Taipei, Colombia, Croatia, Cyprus, Estonia, France, North Rhine-
Westphalia (Germany), Schleswig-Holstein (Germany), Italy, Lithuania, Latvia, Malta, Norway, Poland, Slovak 
Republic, Slovenia, Spain, and Sweden.
Potential sources of error originating from the use of the two parallel modes had to be kept to the absolute 
minimum to ensure uniform and comparable conditions across modes and countries. To achieve this, ICCS 
questionnaires in both modes were self-administered, had identical contents and comparable layout and 
appearance, and required the data collection for both modes to take place over the same period of time.
After the instruments had gone through translation and layout verification processes, they were prepared 
for delivery online using the RM Results Assessment Master™ software, as described in more detail in 
Chapter 5.
Online data collection for survey activities questionnaires
In order to collect feedback about survey operations from NRCs, the international project team set up an 
online survey activities questionnaire (SAQ). The questionnaire was prepared and administered using the 
IEA Online Survey System.
The purpose of the SAQ was to gather opinions and information about the strength and weaknesses of 
the ICCS assessment materials (e.g., instruments, manuals, scoring guides, and software) and countries’ 
experiences with the ICCS survey operations procedures. NRCs were asked to complete these questionnaires 
with the assistance of their national data managers and other national center staff. The information was 
used to evaluate the survey operations and will also be used to improve the quality of the survey activities 
and materials for future ICCS cycles. Chapter 8 presents insights derived from responses to the SAQ.
The country-specific login information was sent to the NRCs via e-mail. Respondents could log in and out 
as many times as needed during the administration period and resume answering the questionnaire at the 
question they had last responded to in their previous session. Answers were automatically saved whenever 
respondents moved to another screen, and respondents could change any answer at any time before 
submitting the responses to IEA.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
92
Administering the ICCS instruments
Preparing assessment materials and distributing them to the participating schools required careful 
organization and planning by national centers. The assessment materials were provided to the school 
coordinators prior to testing, giving ample time for the school coordinators to confirm the receipt and 
correctness of the materials. 
For the PBA, school coordinators then distributed school questionnaire and teacher questionnaires to 
the designated individuals. Teacher questionnaires were assigned and sent to each sampled teacher, and a 
school questionnaire was sent for completion to the school principal. Paper instruments for students were 
kept in a secure room until the assessment date. 
For the online and CBA, individual login information was provided to ensure confidentiality and separation. 
For the school and teacher questionnaires, the national centers sent the login information, along with 
general information on how to access the online questionnaire, to the school coordinator in the form of 
“cover letters.” In line with the procedures used during distribution of the paper questionnaires, the school 
coordinator delivered the school and teacher login information to the designated individuals. Student login 
information was provided to the school coordinator with the student tracking forms and kept in a secure 
room before they were provided to the Test Administrators. 
For the school and teacher online questionnaires, respondents could log in and out as many times as needed 
during the administration period and resume answering the questionnaire at the question they had last 
responded to in their previous session. Answers were automatically saved whenever respondents moved to 
another screen, and respondents could change any answer at any time before submitting the questionnaire.
According to procedures described in the test administrator manual, national centers assigned a test 
administrator to each sampled class; their role was to administer the civic knowledge test along with the 
international student questionnaires and a regional instrument (where applicable) to students. This person 
was chosen and trained by the national center. In some cases, the school coordinator also undertook the 
test administrator role. The test administrator was responsible for logging in students to the test system 
or distributing materials to the appropriate students, reading the instructions provided in the Test 
Administrator manual to the students, leading students through the assessment, and assuring an accurate 
timing of the sessions. Following the test, they also administered the international student questionnaire 
and a regional instrument (where applicable). 
The administration of the student ICCS instruments consisted of either two or three parts. The first part 
concerned the civic knowledge test booklets. Each sampled student was assigned one test booklet. The test 
booklets were assigned in a completely balanced rotated design so that each test item cluster within the 
booklets was assigned to an approximately equal number of students (see Chapter 2). Each student also was 
asked to complete a questionnaire. If a country opted for administering one of the two additional regional 
student questionnaires, each student was also assigned an additional regional instrument. In the case of 
PBA, questionnaires were labeled in a way that linked them to the corresponding test booklet. In the case of 
CBA, students were automatically directed to the corresponding questionnaire(s).
The time allotted for each of these sections was standardized across countries. To complete all parts of the 
civic knowledge test, target grade students were allowed 45 minutes in total. If students completed the 
test before the allotted time was over, they were allowed to review their answers or read quietly, but were 
not allowed to leave the testing room. To complete the international student questionnaire, students were 
given at least 40 minutes and were allowed to continue if extra time was necessary. In European and Latin 
American countries, additional time was allocated to complete a regional student questionnaire. Each part 
of the student assessment was allocated an approximate time, and test administrators were required to 
document the starting and ending time of each section on the test administration form (Table 7.2).

FIELD OPERATIONS PROCEDURES
93

The test administrator used the student tracking form to distribute the booklets to the correct students or 
to log in students to the test system in case of CBA, and to document student participation. During the CBA, 
technical support was given by the national center, which, in turn, could contact the ISC at ACER if they were 
unable to solve the problem themselves.
Because the national centers were able to monitor the responses to the computer-based and online 
questionnaires in real-time, they could send reminders to those schools where people had not responded 
within the expected period of time. Typically, in these cases, the centers asked the school coordinators to 
follow up with those individuals who had not responded. The school coordinator used the information on 
the participation status to calculate the participation rate. If participation was below 90 percent in any class, 
it was the school coordinator’s responsibility to conduct a makeup session for the absent students before 
returning all of the testing materials to the national center. The national centers entered the information 
recorded on the student and teacher tracking forms into WinW3S.
Countries delivering the instruments using computer-based delivery in ICCS had the benefit of a reduction 
in workload after data collection. Answers to instruments were already in electronic format, and responses 
were stored on central servers hosted by RM Results, thus there was no need for any data entry of the 
survey responses.
7.5	Scoring the ICCS assessment and checking scorer reliability
Scoring the assessment
The success of assessments containing constructed-response items depends on the degree to which student 
responses are scored reliably. The number of items to be scored within a country depended on the delivery 
mode (CBA or PBA) and, for CBA countries, whether or not the country participated in the bridging study. 
As it was critical to the quality of the ICCS results that they were scored in a reliable manner, reliability was 
accomplished by providing national centers with explicit scoring guides, extensive training of scoring staff, 
and continuous monitoring of the quality of the work during scoring procedures.
Scoring responses on the constructed-response items collected online was done online via the RM 
Assessment MasterTM Marker application, which was configured to address IEA standards and reliability 
control procedures. Scoring the paper-based constructed-response items was completed within the test 
booklets, with scorers writing their scores in a designated box in each booklet. These scores were later 
manually entered into the ICCS database.
During the scoring training, which was conducted at the international level, national center staff members 
were trained how to score the constructed-response items in the ICCS assessment. Scoring training took 
place both before the field trial and before the main survey. The training that took place prior to the field 
trial provided participants with their first opportunity to give extensive feedback on the scoring guides for 
the new ICCS 2022 items. These guides were revised on the basis of this feedback. The training conducted 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
94
before the main survey enabled national center staff to give additional feedback on the scoring guides for 
the new ICCS 2022 items in light of their experiences of scoring the items in the field trial, and the guides 
were revised accordingly (see Chapter 2 for more details).
The scoring guides for the trend items stemming from the 2009 and 2016 cycles of ICCS were not 
revised and were identical to those used in ICCS 2009 and ICCS 2016 respectively. Further detail of the 
development and revision of the ICCS Main Survey Scoring Guide for Open-ended Response Items is 
provided in Chapter 2.
To prepare for this task, the ISC provided national centers with suggestions on how to organize staff, 
materials, procedures, and the scoring process. National centers were encouraged to hire scorers who were 
attentive to detail and familiar with education, particularly those with a background in civic and citizenship 
education. In countries participating in the bridging study, it was strongly recommended that scorers score 
both computer-based and paper-based responses. The ISC also provided guidelines on how to train scorers 
to accurately and reliably score the constructed-response civic knowledge items.
Assuring and documenting scoring reliability
Assuring and documenting the reliability of the scoring process within countries was a highly important 
aspect of monitoring and maintaining the quality of the ICCS scored data. Scoring reliability within 
each country was established using different scorers who independently scored a random sample of 
approximately 200 responses per item.
For the CBA mode, double scoring was managed automatically by the RM AM Marker system. Approximately 
200 responses to each item (this was set as a percentage of the total number of responses per item) were 
provided to two different scorers. Scorers were unaware of whether any given response they scored had 
been allocated for double scoring.
For the PBA mode, booklets to be double-scored were selected by the WinW3S software. Reliability 
booklets were assigned so that about 200 responses per item were double scored in each participating 
country. The first scorer designated to a booklet would enter their scores on a separate sheet (not in the 
booklet) and the second scorer would enter their scores in the booklet. 
The degree of agreement between the scores, assigned by the two scorers, is a measure of the reliability 
of the scoring process. Items with low inter-rater reliability within a given country were not used in the 
estimation of student achievement for that country. Chapter 11 outlines the adjudication process relating 
to inter-rater reliability of items.
7.6	Field trial procedures
The ICCS field trial was a smaller administration of the ICCS assessment; on average, approximately 1,000 
students were tested in each participating country. A full-scale field trial of all instruments and operational 
procedures under conditions approximating, as closely as possible, those of the main survey data collection 
was conducted. 
Data collected in the field trial were used to evaluate the psychometric properties of the test and 
questionnaire items to inform revision and item selection for the main survey data collection. The field 
trial also served the purpose of testing the ICCS survey operations procedures to identify and resolve any 
issues prior to the ICCS main survey data collection. This also allowed the NRCs and their staff to become 
acquainted with the activities, refine their national operations, and provide feedback that was used to 
improve the procedures for the main survey data collection. 
The field trial resulted in some small modifications to survey operations procedures and contributed 
significantly to the successful execution of ICCS. The field trial was planned to be conducted during October 
2020 to April 2021, but due to the COVID-19 pandemic not all countries managed the administration in 
the planned administration period. The COVID-19 pandemic greatly disrupted the field trial data collection 
administration period, requiring that some countries postponed the administration period up to six months. 
These were unprecedented circumstances for the ICCS study.

QUALITY ASSURANCE IN ICCS 2022
95
Chapter 8:
Quality Assurance in ICCS 2022
Lauren Musu and Kateřina Hartmanová
To meet the highest standards for the International Civic and Citizenship Education Study (ICCS) 2022 data, 
considerable effort has been put into ensuring standardized materials and survey operations procedures. 
Quality assurance for ICCS 2022 encompassed internal mechanisms built into each stage of the data 
collection process. 
The International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) administered a quality 
assurance program for ICCS 2022 to document data collection activities and verify that the standardized 
procedures were followed. The program consisted of two independent components: An international 
quality assurance program, managed by IEA, and a national quality assurance program, managed by each 
national center and based on the international guidelines and manuals provided. 
8.1	International Quality Assurance Program
Overview
This section provides an overview of the international quality assurance program and the data collected for 
the program. 
The international quality assurance program was implemented by independent international quality 
observers (IQOs) appointed and trained by IEA. The major task of the IQOs was to conduct on-site visits 
during the main survey data collection. In each country, the IQO and the IQO’s assistant(s) visited a sample 
of testing sessions at participating schools. A total of 15 school visits were required for countries using 
paper-based administration (PBA) or those countries who were new to the study and using computer-based 
administration (CBA). In countries who were transitioning from PBA in 2016 to CBA in 2022, IQOs were 
required to visit three additional schools (18 in total) to observe the paper bridge booklet administration. 
For each school visit, IQOs observed the administration sessions and recorded their observations, 
noting any deviations from the standardized administration script, timing, and procedures. In addition, 
IQOs interviewed the School Coordinators (SCs) and Test Administrators (TAs) about their experiences 
coordinating/administering ICCS 2022. Due to the COVID-19 pandemic, there was one country where 
outside observers were not allowed to enter schools to physically observe the ICCS 2022 main survey 
administration. Instead, the IQO completed extended (online or phone) interviews with the SCs and TAs 
to obtain as much information as possible about the administration, including the structure of the testing 
session and any issues that may have occurred. 
Prior to beginning their assignments, the IQOs were required to attend a training session conducted by 
IEA. The online training session was held over two days in January 2022. During the training, IQOs were 
introduced to the ICCS operation procedures, study instruments, and tasks associated with their role. IQOs 
were also provided with a manual detailing their responsibilities and the necessary materials for completing 
their tasks.
In most participating countries and benchmarking entities, the national research coordinators (NRCs) 
assisted IEA in finding qualified individuals to serve as an IQO by nominating two candidates for the role. 
There were also cases where the IEA recruited IQOs who had served the same role in previous IEA studies. 
The independence of the IQOs from national centers is a crucial aspect, and as such the nominated individuals 
could not be a member of the national center, a family member, or personal friend of the NRC. Often, the 
nominees were school inspectors, retired school teachers, or researchers. The IQO was required to be 
fluent in both English and the main language(s) spoken in the country. When necessary, the IQOs recruited 
assistants in order to effectively cover the geographic territory or the schedule of ICCS administration. For 
ICCS 2022, a total of 25 IQOs were trained across the 22 participating countries and two benchmarking 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
96
participants. In turn, the IQOs trained 32 assistants. Altogether, IQOs and their assistants observed 378 
(258 CBA and 120 PBA) administration sessions. 
The IQOs were required to:
•	
Collect the complete set of ICCS 2022 national instruments and manuals, the national adaptation 
form(s), and tracking forms for the selected schools from the national center;
•	
Review selected sections of the School Coordinator (SC) and Test Administrator (TA) Manuals to check 
consistency with the international version;
•	
Review the national assessment instruments and the international translation and layout verification 
feedback to document whether the verifiers’ comments were addressed appropriately in the final 
national instruments;
•	
Select the schools and classrooms to be observed in consultation with the NRC and according to 
specified guidelines;
•	
Contact the SC of each selected school to organize the visit and arrange the interviews;
•	
Observe the administration sessions for their level of adherence to the administration guidelines, 
documenting each session’s activities on the Classroom Observation Record;
•	
Verify the completeness and accuracy of the lists of participating classes, teachers, and students for 
each visited school;
•	
Interview the SC and TA and record the responses on the classroom observation record; and
•	
Submit all collected national materials and completed observation records to IEA.
Observing the ICCS Administration Sessions
IQOs were required to document their observations of testing sessions using standardized Classroom 
Observation Records. The observation records were organized into four sections:
•	
Section A: Preliminary activities;
•	
Section B: Administration process;
•	
Section C: Summary observations; and
•	
Section D: Interview with the School Coordinator and the Test Administrator.
In Section A of the classroom observation record, IQOs registered their observations of the condition of 
the survey materials and (for CBA) testing equipment. Section B addressed activities that took place during 
the actual administration sessions of the student cognitive test, international questionnaire, and regional 
questionnaire (if applicable). Regardless of administration mode, the cognitive test was administered in one 
session of 45 minutes with a break of 5 to 10 minutes before the international questionnaire administration. 
Typically, a break of 10–15 minutes followed prior to administration of the regional questionnaire.
Throughout, IQOs were asked to observe the activities of the TA, specifically the following:
•	
Distributing, collecting, and securing the test booklets and questionnaires (PBA);
•	
Setting up workstations and securing the computers (CBA);
•	
Following the ICCS 2022 administration script; and
•	
Making time announcements during the administration sessions.
IEA received documentation of the international quality assurance program from all 24 countries and 
benchmarking participants in ICCS 2022. The results of the IQO observations are reported in the following 
sections. For each section, combined results from any common questions between CBA and PBA are 
presented first, followed by the results from questions specific to each of the two administration modes.
8.2	Findings from the ICCS 2022 administration
Preliminary activities and condition suitability for the assessment 
As mentioned previously, ICCS 2022 marked the transition from PBA to CBA, with most participating 
countries choosing to administer the student materials on computers and a small number of countries 
administering only on paper. In terms of quality assurance, this meant slightly different procedures and 

QUALITY ASSURANCE IN ICCS 2022
97
preparations took place for each of the administration modes, as reflected in the questions IQOs received 
as part of the observation records. For example, while paper booklets were expected to be distributed at the 
beginning of each part of the administration, workstations for CBA were required to be prepared in advance 
with a single workstation to be used by each student throughout the whole administration. 
For each of the administration modes, IQOs noted their observations of the condition of the survey 
materials, the testing equipment, the TA’s level of preparation, and the suitability of the room where the 
administration took place.
In line with Table 8.1, the survey materials were usually stored in a secure location at the school, online (for 
CBA), or kept with the TA. There was only one incident reported by IQOs where the appropriate security 
measures were not followed and although stored securely, the materials were not sealed (PBA).

students.

In terms of equipment for the CBA administration, computers with screen sizes of at least 15 inches 
were used in most sessions (72%). These were most frequently desktop computers (55%), but portable 
computers with (30%) or without (15%) a power plug were also used. In the majority of the cases (82%), no 
other person besides the Test Administrator, School Coordinator, or IT support person had access to the 
assessment room prior to the testing (see Table 8.3). The people who were reported to have had access to 
the room in the other cases were mostly the class teacher or the school principal.  
The workstations were specifically prepared for each student in most cases (72%) and in the instances they 
were not, the students picked their workstations and were given their assigned login details later. In one 
observed classroom, students used their own laptops to take the assessment using login details provided 
by the TA. Most of the already prepared login screens (86%) displayed the student ID and the remaining 14 
percent displayed the student’s name.

In general, the reported information confirmed that the preparations for ICCS administration were of a high 
standard (Tables 8.1, 8.2, and 8.3). As reported in Table 8.1, IQOs judged most TAs (95%) as being familiar 
with the test administration script. In general, the observers observed no procedural deviations in the test 
preparations that they deemed severe enough to jeopardize the integrity of the test administration. In 
addition, nearly all of the observed administration sessions took place under favorable room conditions that 
were suitable for students to work without distraction (96%). The most commonly reported inconveniences 
were the classroom being cold due to open windows (2%, often following COVID-19 measures), not enough 
space between students (1%), and external noise from other students (3%, especially during break time).
Administration of the Cognitive Test
Table 8.4 shows that IQOs reported that the assessments were conducted in accordance with the 
international procedures, particularly in regard to following the script and adherence to time limits. In very 
rare instances, the IQOs observed and carefully documented significant deviations from the administration 
procedures which will be noted throughout this section. 
In the majority (96%) of cases, paper test booklets were distributed by the TA according to the booklet 
assignment on the Student Tracking Form and booklets label. In one case, the IQO reported that some 
booklets were distributed to the wrong students, but this was corrected before the assessment began as 
the students’ names were on the booklets. In another case, students’ names were called out and booklets 
handed to them, and in one observed classroom, two students distributed the test booklets based on 
the student names written on them. Additionally, in one country the privacy measures prevented the TA 
from writing the names of the students in the Student Tracking Form, resulting in them relying fully on the 
students to make sure to take booklets with the same number for each part of the assessment. Finally, in one 
school the test had been administered a week prior to the IQO’s scheduled visit due to a miscommunication, 
so they completed an extended interview with the SC and TA since it was impossible for the IQO to observe 
this part of the assessment. 
The average time spent on test instructions was 13 minutes across all of the observed sessions, ranging 
from 2 to 51 minutes (with the vast majority (92%) falling between 6–30 minutes and only 2% above). In 
about 60 percent of the sessions, the actual test administration duration was exactly equal to the allocated 
time (45 mins). In about 83 percent of the remaining sessions (about a quarter of all observed sessions), 
all students completed the test before the allotted time has elapsed. Consequently, the average test time 
was 43 minutes (two minutes under the time allocated). It is also important to note that for CBA the timer 
was built into the assessment making it impossible for students to exceed the allocated time. IQOs’ records 
showed that in most of the observed sessions the break between test and questionnaire administration 
sessions did not exceed 10 minutes. In only 4 percent of the cases was there no break between sessions, 
usually at the request of students who preferred to continue.

In accordance with the ICCS procedures, TAs were asked to (collect and) secure the test booklets or 
computers at the end of the testing session. The IQOs reported that this occurred in 89 percent of the 
testing sessions. The majority of the reported exceptions were regarding desktop computers, as students 
either left the room during breaks or after the test was finished (so computers did not have to be secured), 
or students stayed by their computers under the TA’s supervision even during the break. There was only one 
reported case of the students taking the device with them on a break and one case of students being asked 
to leave the room which was then closed but not locked/supervised. 
One of the most important methods of standardizing the assessment administration was to have all Test 
Administrators follow the script in the Test Administrator Manual. IQOs reported that in about three-
quarters of the observations, the TAs exactly followed the script when preparing the students, distributing 
the materials, and giving directions and examples. In the cases where the TAs deviated from the script (92 
total), nearly all modifications were reported as “minor” and characterized most frequently as additions 
(53 cases), followed by omissions (32 cases), and revisions (31 cases). These categories were not mutually 
exclusive and selecting multiple of them to characterize the changes (additions, revisions, omissions) was 
possible.  
Table 8.5 presents observations on students’ compliance with instructions and overall cooperation during 
the assessment administration. According to the IQOs’ observations, in almost all the sessions, students 
complied very well or well with the instruction to stop work. In addition, IQOs described the students as 
extremely orderly and cooperative for more than two thirds of the testing sessions.

Administration of the Student Questionnaires 
Table 8.6 shows selected questions from IQOs’ observations of the Student Questionnaire administration. 
IQOs reported that the Student Questionnaires were distributed according to the Student Tracking 
Forms and questionnaire labels. A deviation from the procedure occurred in one country because the 
questionnaires were distributed together with the tests at the beginning of the administration and in 
another case, students distributed the assigned booklets. 
In most cases (85%), TAs followed the Student Questionnaire administration script exactly. Additions (24 
cases), revisions (18 cases), or deletions (27 cases) to the script were reported almost equally in those instances 
where changes occurred (53 cases in total, with multiple categories of changes to one script possible).

The Student Questionnaire was administered in exactly 40 minutes (full allocated time) in 31 percent of 
the sessions. As instructed in the TA Manual, students were able to ask for extra time to complete the 
questionnaire, and this took place in approximately one quarter of sessions (27%). The allotted extra time 
varied from a few minutes to 56 additional minutes. Administration of the questionnaire took 42 minutes on 
average (ranging between 15–96 minutes).
The IQOs also provided observations on the administration of the two regional instruments for ICCS 2022 
(Table 8.7) as follows:
•	
European Student Questionnaire (18 participating countries and the 2 benchmarking participants);
•	
Latin American Student Questionnaire (2 participating countries). 
Paper-based Regional Student Questionnaires were distributed according to the Student Tracking Forms 
and questionnaire labels in 80 percent of cases. Deviations from the procedure were reported in two 
countries: in one country, both questionnaires were distributed at the beginning of the ICCS administration 
and in another, students handed out the assigned questionnaires. 
IQOs judged the Test Administrators to have followed the administration script exactly in 78 percent of the 
sessions. Changes were generally considered minor (in 20 out of 34 total cases) and were most commonly 
characterized as omissions (21 cases).

Summary Observations of the ICCS 2022 Administration 
Section C of the classroom observation record included questions about general impressions of how the 
administration was conducted, how well the TA monitored students, and any unusual circumstances that 
arose during the session. Table 8.8 reports on these.
Overall, IQOs reported that the quality of ICCS administration was very good or excellent (90%). Instruments 
of high quality were delivered to schools, only one case of defective booklets was reported, and these were 
detected and properly replaced after the administration session began. 
Table 8.8: General impressions on the quality of administration—378 ICCS administration sessions (percent of

Student refusal to take the assessment was rare and only occurred in 4 percent of observed assessment 
sessions. There were no problems observed in relation to test refusals and no late students in the majority 
(88%) of observed sessions (see Table 8.9). Out of the students who did arrive late, most were still admitted 
into the room, either before (7%) or after (4%) the beginning of the administration. There were only four 
reported cases (1%) of late students not admitted into the room. In 19 percent of the observed administration 

QUALITY ASSURANCE IN ICCS 2022
101
sessions, students left the room for an “emergency” (usually a bathroom visit) during the test administration. 
In such cases, TAs were instructed to secure the students’ computers or collect their test booklets until the 
students reentered the assessment room. In some sessions, the booklet was left closed on the student’s 
desk until the student returned to the room.
In 10 percent of the sessions there were students requiring special accommodation. Most commonly, 
students with dyslexia/reading difficulties had the questions read out to them in a separate room or used 
Optical Character Recognition (OCR) files. In addition, a few students with autism or other conditions had 
an assistant available to help them take the test. 
While in most testing sessions students complied with the instructions to store everything away, sometimes 
cell phones or tablets were kept on the tables for listening to music after test completion and until the time 
for completion of the test or questionnaire(s) expired. In some other instances, books were left on the tables 
for students to read after finishing the test. In a few classrooms students also kept their computers on the 
tables, but they were turned off during the test (with the exception of a few students with dyslexia using 
them for the OCR files). 
TAs were not walking around the room in a few of the observed sessions, but IQOs noted they were 
nonetheless able to monitor students from the front of the room. In 5 percent of the cases, IQOs reported 
evidence of students attempting to cheat. However, in some of these instances, the IQOs observed that 
students only tried to communicate between themselves until they found out that the tests were different. 
IQOs reported that TAs intervened when necessary in appropriate ways.

Interview with the School Coordinator and Test Administrator
The IQOs conducted interviews with the SCs and TAs according to the guidelines included in Section 
D of the Classroom Observation Record. The purpose of this activity was to solicit an evaluation of the 
ICCS administration from the responsible persons, to collect suggestions for improvement, and to obtain 
additional background information on survey-related activities such as:
•	
Shipment of assessment materials;
•	
Arrangements for ICCS administration;
•	
Responsiveness of the national center to queries;
•	
Organization of classes in the school (to validate within-school sampling procedure). 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
102
School Coordinators 
A large majority of SCs (82%) expressed a favorable impression of the ICCS survey, reporting that the ICCS 
2022 administration in their school went very well overall (Table 8.10). When SCs reported issues, they 
were primarily related to students’ absenteeism, lack of time to prepare for the assessment, waiting time 
for those students who finished earlier, and technical issues (difficulty setting up USB sticks, spotty internet 
connection, test login details, or the link not working). Staff members’ attitude toward the administration 
was reported as positive in 63 percent of the observed sessions. Although the majority of staff could see the 
importance of the survey, there were complaints in some schools; teachers considered the timing unfortunate 
(due to COVID-19 and other assessments happening at the same time), saw the assessment as an additional 
burden after the difficult period of the pandemic, and expressed concerns that the administration time took 
time away from lessons. 
SCs were also asked about the quality of the School Coordinator Manual. Less than six percent said that the 
manual needed improvements and provided reasons such as the length, repetition, and lack of visual appeal. 
Some SCs also asked for a digital version to be provided and mentioned that additional visuals to show the 
login process and details on handling technical difficulties would have been helpful. It should also be noted 
that not all SCs had received or read the manual by the time of the administration.

the ICCS materials to the school on the administration day. In two countries, the School Coordinator

by the respective national centers.

In more than half of the cases (60%), SCs indicated that the principal, the civic and citizenship education 
teacher, or the students’ classroom teacher gave students special instructions, motivational talks or 
incentives prior to administration. In a few countries, the students received leaflets or were shown a short 
video. In one country there was an article about ICCS published in the school newspaper. 
Because the sampling of classes required a complete list of all classes in the school at the target grade, 
IQOs were also asked to verify that the class list really included all classes. The majority of the SCs (94%) 
confirmed that the complete list of classes had been documented. There were some schools where IQOs 
reported one or multiple missing classes on the list. In one country, the SC explained that the missing class 
was in line with the national center’s instructions to exclude certain types of classes in line with the sampling 
frame. In another country, the class listing form had not been used for class selection but rather an official 
school registry provided by the Ministry of Education was used in its place. 
The presence or absence of students from the initial student listing forms sent to the national centers could 
be explained due to recent changes in the school enrolment during the school year or students with severe 
learning disabilities who were excluded from the assessment. There were no reported cases of students 
enrolled in more than one of the classes. 
As a reflection of the planning and implementation of ICCS 2022, 88 percent of interviewed S Cs said that 
they would be willing to serve in the same role in future international assessments. Several remarked that 
the experience was enjoyable and positive overall. The main reasons mentioned for the lack of willingness 
to serve again were the timing of the test (during a busy end of the school year with other assessments 
happening in the same period) and that it required a lot of extra time and effort. Finally, it is notable that the 
response rate for the Classroom Observation Records, Section D, was extremely high on all questions. The 
only exceptions recorded were three SCs who were ill or unavailable for the interviews.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
104
Test Administrators 
In the 2022 cycle, IQOs also interviewed the Test Administrators for the first time. About 27 percent of TAs 
were teachers from a different class, 8 percent teachers from the same class, 3 percent school counsellors 
and 1 percent were principals at the school or school district. The rest (48%) fell into the “other” category 
and were mostly external staff trained by the national center or an external agency, administrative assistants 
(secretaries), IT personnel, or teachers from a different school district. 
Similar to SCs, a large majority of TAs (82%) also expressed a favorable impression of the ICCS survey, 
reporting that the administration in their school went very well overall (Table 8.12). In addition, nearly 
all (98%) TAs reported they were satisfied with the set up for the administration of the assessment and 
questionnaire(s). As for the quality of the Test Administrator Manual, about 15 percent said that the manual 
needed improvements. Most commonly they mentioned that the manual was too long and repetitive 
(especially the instructions for students on how to behave and answer questions included in the script). 
TAs also suggested some structural changes such as making the necessary codes easily accessible in one 
place (on one page) or dividing the manual into sections (preparations before, on the day at the school, 
the administration itself, after the administration). They also suggested to emphasize in the script that all 
answers are anonymous, that the assessment is not graded and is only an investigation, and the implications 
of pressing the “Finish” button (no possibility to go back to review previous questions). In addition, the TAs 
would have appreciated if the manual contained more guidance on how to login and to deal with (regular) 
school breaks and FAQs from students.

As a reflection on the administration and related tasks, 94 percent of interviewed TAs said that they would 
be willing to serve again as a TA in future international assessments (Table 8.13). They often remarked that 
the manuals were easy to follow and things well organized, however they also mentioned that the role is 
time-consuming, and a different time of the school year would be better to administer the survey. Although 
some questions were left unanswered, there were no TAs refusing to take part in the interview.

QUALITY ASSURANCE IN ICCS 2022
105
8.3	Findings from the national materials review
As part of their tasks, IQOs reviewed selected sections of the national versions of the School Coordinator 
and Test Administrator Manuals, to ensure the content was comparable to the international versions. In 
general, any modifications reported by the IQOs were minor. For example, a sentence specifying that no 
exclusion code should be assigned to students who lack parental permission to participate was omitted in 
the School Coordinator Manual in multiple countries. While the text specifying different exclusion codes for 
students was often rephrased, the overall meaning was retained, and the information remained comparable 
to the source. Similarly, there were no significant deviations from the international version found in the 
national Test Administrator Manuals. In particular, IQOs reviewed the administration script provided in the 
Test Administrator Manual. In a few cases, the instruction to put away any schoolbooks or papers on the desk 
was omitted or adjusted to include other objects, such as mobile phones. Additionally, minor adjustments 
were made to other instructions in some cases to reflect a specific response mode or classroom reality. 
In addition, IQOs also reviewed the assessment instruments to ensure that the results of the international 
translation and layout verifications documented in the national adaptation forms had been properly 
addressed and integrated into the final assessment instruments. There were no significant issues reported 
in this review.
8.4	Findings from the survey activities questionnaire
As another valuable source of information on the overall quality of the ICCS administration, the survey 
activities questionnaire (SAQ) gathered information from the NRCs about whether the implementation of 
the ICCS procedures accorded with the standards outlined in the survey operations procedures manuals. 
The SAQ also collected feedback on the strengths and weaknesses of the approaches and materials used. It 
covered various topics including:
•	
Sampling;
•	
Contacting schools; 
•	
Recruiting School Coordinators;
•	
Translating and preparing the survey instruments;
•	
Administering the assessment;
•	
Implementing the national quality assurance program;
•	
Scoring open-ended response items;
•	
Entering and coding occupation data;
•	
Entering and submitting data;
•	
Other experiences. 
One major purpose of the SAQ is to gather information to improve the quality of future IEA studies. Data 
were collected online from each NRC personally, with data manager and/or other national center staff 
assisting this process where necessary. The results presented in this section reflect the quality of the ICCS 
procedures and materials in 20 of the participating countries (all of those who filled the survey out within 
the specified deadline).

Adapting and Translating the ICCS Assessment Materials
NRCs provided information about the process of adapting and translating the ICCS student cognitive test 
and questionnaires into national languages. Most NRCs identified national center staff as the individuals 
responsible for adapting and/or translating the survey instruments, as well as their review (Table 8.17). In 
about half of all participating countries, an outside specialist was involved in the translation in addition to 
the own staff.

Finalizing the ICCS Assessment Materials
Table 8.18 shows that countries completed the final step of the verification process—layout verification 
conducted by the ISC—with some difficulties, stemming from the system used for the study and unrequested 
changes introduced to the materials by the delivery system provider. 
In general, NRCs did not find it difficult to assemble the various survey instruments. Few of them detected 
printing errors (missing pages and wrong page order). In all cases these errors were minor and were resolved 
in a straightforward manner before the distribution of the materials. 
All external providers were asked to sign confidentiality agreements where applicable. Potential security 
breaches were reported in two cases, both of which involved materials getting lost before reaching the 
National Study Center after being (supposedly) shipped back from a participating school.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
110
Sampling
The summary information provided in Table 8.19 indicates that the sampling process worked well overall. 
In most countries, the NRCs reported no difficulties adapting the international sampling design to national 
specifications or compiling a sampling frame (a list of eligible schools). Among those national centers that 
experienced some level of difficulty, all felt well supported by IEA’s sampling team. The Within-School 
Sampling Software (WinW3S) provided by IEA to select classes and teachers was used by all countries 
participating in the Main Study without difficulty.
Three NRCs reported encountering special conditions or organizational constraints that required 
deviations from the standard ICCS within-school sampling design. In one country, all classes of the target 
grade at a selected school were already sampled in other large scale international studies and thus could 
not be included in the ICCS sample. Other deviations stemmed from specific stream/track or special class 
characteristics of the educational systems making it necessary to adjust the design to ensure reasonable 
class selection. In each of these cases, a sampling expert was consulted to ensure that the altered design met 
all sampling requirements. In about two thirds (13) of the countries, numbers or other codes were used on 
student and/or teacher lists instead of or in addition to names.

Administering the Main Survey
The section of the SAQ inquiring about main survey administration addressed some important aspects 
of the data collection; selected results are presented in Table 8.20. Some countries reported that at least 
some of their TAs were drawn from national center staff or contracted externally, but it was also common to 
recruit TAs from among the SCs or teachers, either of the sampled schools or from other schools. 
As with the training of SCs, most countries used more than one approach to train their TAs, relying most 
heavily on written instructions (12 countries) supplemented with online meetings (12 countries) and email/
telephone correspondence (12/11 countries, respectively). Formal face-to-face training sessions were 
conducted in only three of the participating countries. This large shift towards online trainings compared 
to the previous cycle (where 18 countries conducted formal in person sessions) is likely at least partially 
related to the COVID-19 pandemic.
Almost two thirds (9) of the schools administering paper-based instruments encountered problems with 
student participation. NRCs reported absences due to sickness, COVID-19 outbreaks, and lack of parental 
consent as the most common reasons for students not to participate. Make-up sessions were conducted 
when possible to reach high participation rates. Issues with teacher and principal participation were also 
reported in some circumstances.

Preparing and Administering Online Questionnaires
As shown in Table 8.21, more than two thirds of countries elected to administer teacher and school 
questionnaires online. Half of the respondents reported experiencing major problems with the online 
platform (Assessment Master) during the administration and five reported issues with the online data 
collection of these questionnaires in general. The most common issues mentioned were downtime of the 
system resulting in users unable to access or being logged out of the system, changes not being saved 
properly, and difficulty monitoring the collected data in the system through reports. More than half (nine) of 
these respondents also reported problems with the login procedures, especially the URLs, checksums and 
IDs being confusing, mistyped or lost by the participants, or not working properly.
NRCs also reported difficulties reaching high participation rates for online questionnaires from school staff, 
mainly teachers. The explanations provided included lack of interest, time and motivation, high workload, 
or getting discouraged due to technical issues. NRCs also mentioned that there were notable differences 
between individual schools and that SCs can have a large impact on participation rates since they serve as the 
only channel to remind and encourage the respondents to participate. Relatedly, the most common means 
to organize follow-up procedures for teachers and principals that did not complete their questionnaire by 
the given deadline were contacting the SC through an email or a phone call, reminding the school principals, 
and sometimes extending the deadline.

National Quality Assurance Program 
IEA provided materials to assist national centers in conducting their own national quality assurance 
program. The ICCS 2022 Survey Operations Procedures Unit 4 provided guidelines for selecting the 
national quality observers (NQOs), and basic information about the observers’ duties. In addition, the ICCS 
2022 National Quality Observer Manual was developed for use in training NQOs. The NQOs’ responsibilities 
were similar to those of the IQOs, and involved visiting selected schools to observe and document the data 
collection sessions and interview SCs. Seventeen NRCs confirmed that they had implemented a national 
quality assurance program, and that NQOs and their assistants had visited 281 schools in the participating 
countries (Table 8.22). In three countries, NRCs did not conduct national programs because there were 
COVID-19 restrictions in place preventing physical school visits, they considered that information collected 
through the international program was sufficient, or because the TAs employed were considered very well 
trained.
In line with the IQO reports, the NQOs confirmed the quality of the survey process was generally good. 
There were issues reported in five cases, mostly errors with the testing system and data uploads, unruly/
distracting behavior from students who finished the assessment early, or unsuitable seating arrangements. 
Additional comments highlighted that students sometimes felt the assessment was too long/too much to 
do in one day and often lost concentration, others additionally expressed the content was too difficult/hard 
to understand. In addition, there were some technical issues observed during administration, as previously 
mentioned.

Scoring Open-Ended Response Items and Coding Occupation Data
NRCs were also asked to comment on the persons responsible for scoring open-ended response items, 
the procedures used to verify scoring reliability, and the coding of occupation data (see Table 8.23). The 
participating countries used scorers with a variety of different backgrounds, including national center staff, 
teachers, university students or externals with degrees in relevant areas. Five NRCs reported experiencing 
difficulties with implementing the reliability (independent double scoring) procedure in their countries, 
often related to the scoring system or difficulty in finding consensus between scorers. These difficulties 
were generally resolved later on, sometimes with additional scoring practice. 
The majority of countries (16) used the Occupation Data Excel file for coding occupations. Some PBA 
countries also mentioned coding occupations directly in the instruments. Countries used anywhere between 
2–7 coders to code the occupation data. The coders were trained mostly through online and in-person 
sessions, where they were provided with concrete examples, and had the opportunity to ask questions. In 
some countries, coders also attended an online discussion group and/or received printed materials with 
instructions. In a few countries (two), no training was necessary as already experienced/trained coders were 
used. Four countries reported difficulties coding occupations, stemming mostly from the answers given by 
students being vague and not clearly referring to any of the ISCO-08 coding scheme definitions.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
114
Entering, Collecting, and Submitting Data
As Table 8.24 shows, when asked about the persons responsible for entering paper questionnaire data, the 
NRCs reported using their own (national center) staff most often, followed by a combination of staff from 
the national center and an external data entry company. In some cases, data entry was done by university 
students or other external associates/assistants. Most countries used the IEA DME software to enter data 
manually. Those few that did not typically used scanning procedures or relied on an external company to 
carry out the data entry to a format that would later be imported into the IEA DME. 
About two thirds (14) of the countries used the RM player for the student assessment. The most common 
form of delivery for CBA was online delivery (10), followed by USB (8), and portable computers taken 
to schools (7). Only two countries used a local server network to administer the assessment. More than 
half (10) of the countries administering CBA experienced difficulties using the RM player, although this 
is believed to only affect a small portion of the data. These difficulties included faulty USBs and technical 
issues with the online system (login issues, long loading times, buttons not working properly, too small font 
size, difficulty retrieving answers after accidental log outs). Some difficulties such as data not syncing up or 
other errors uploading the data from USB sticks or via a server method were encountered in five countries. 
Eight countries also reported difficulties monitoring the data collection, mentioning the data collection 
reports not working at the beginning of the data collection for a few weeks and/or only working using an 
Excel macro workaround.

The NRCs’ Assessment of the Quality of the ICCS Manuals and Study Resources
The survey activities questionnaire also provided an opportunity for NRCs to give feedback on the quality of 
the various ICCS manuals and study resources. The NRCs were generally very positive about the manuals 
and webinars, and nearly all of them described these resources as “very” or “somewhat” helpful for carrying 
out the survey (Table 8.25). When asked for their suggestions for improvement, some NRCs suggested that 
the information in the manuals could be more concise, extra webinar time dedicated to those new to the 
project, and that short accessible videos would be an appreciated additional resource.
In addition, most NRCs found the ICCS SharePoint website very or somewhat helpful, especially for 
exchanging data and materials and accessing meeting documents. The individual country schedules and 
country consultations were also perceived as useful for the preparations for the main survey and national 
material production.
