8.5	Summary
The ICCS quality assurance programs conducted at both the international and national levels provided 
crucial documentation of participating countries’ adherence to the standardized data collection procedures. 
The registered observations of the IQOs in the Classroom Observation Records indicate that in the vast 
majority of cases, the prescribed survey administration procedures were very closely followed. These 
included the preparatory activities, delivery of the administration script, timing of the sessions, and handling 
of the instruments.
The Survey Activities Questionnaire further documented a number of key aspects of the ICCS survey 
administration and provided information for improving the quality of future IEA surveys. Complementing 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
116
the IQO reports, the results of this questionnaire also show that NRCs generally felt well-supported by the 
study consortium and operational manuals, and that they were able to comply well with the international 
procedures related to sampling, external verification, and reliability scoring.
These results provide evidence of the high quality of the data collection sessions, despite being affected 
by various technical issues and COVID-19 related disruptions. The observed good behavior of most of 
the participating students, strong evaluations from national and international quality observers, School 
Coordinators, and Test Administrators, and positive attitudes of other school staff members toward the 
survey administration further attest to the careful planning and implementation of this cycle of ICCS.

DATA MANAGEMENT, CLEANING, AND CREATION OF THE ICCS INTERNATIONAL DATABASE
117
Chapter 9:
Data management, cleaning, and creation of the 
ICCS international database
Hannah Kowolik and Yasin Afana
9.1	Introduction
This chapter describes the procedures for verifying the International Civic and Citizenship Education 
Study (ICCS 2022) data and creating the ICCS 2022 international database (IDB) implemented by the 
International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA), the ICCS International Study 
Center (ISC) at the Australian Council for Educational Research (ACER), the associate research center at 
Lumsa University and the national centers of the participating countries.
Preparing the ICCS 2022 international database and ensuring its integrity was a challenging endeavor 
requiring extensive collaboration between IEA, the ISC, and the national centers of participating countries. 
Participating countries followed standardized survey operations procedures to prepare instruments, 
administer the civic knowledge test and questionnaires, collect national context questionnaire data, and 
prepare data files (depending on the administration mode) and documentation. Once the team at IEA 
received the ICCS 2022 data and documentation, an exhaustive process of verification and editing known 
as ‘data cleaning’ began.
Data cleaning is the process of checking data for inconsistencies and formatting the data to create a 
standardized output. The overriding concerns of the data cleaning process were to ensure the following:
•	
All information in the database conformed to the internationally defined data structure;
•	
The content of all codebooks and documentation appropriately reflected national adaptations to 
questionnaires;
•	
All variables used for international comparisons were comparable across countries (after harmonization, 
where necessary); and
•	
All institutions involved in this process applied control measures throughout it to assure the quality and 
accuracy of the ICCS data.
9.2	Data sources
In ICCS 2022, participating education systems could choose between two different administration modes 
for the students’ assessment: computer-based administration (CBA) and paper-based administration (PBA). 
Countries that opted for the CBA option and that participated in ICCS 2016 were administering student 
instruments in paper format as part of the bridging study. For the school and teacher questionnaires, 
participating countries could choose to administer these online and/or paper-based.
Data collected as part of ICCS 2022 required work by participating countries before being submitted to 
IEA for processing and cleaning. This included data collected from: (i) the ICCS Player, which delivered 
the CBA, including student test and questionnaire; (ii) online administration, including school and teacher 
questionnaires (iii) paper instruments, including all instruments. This included the scoring of constructed 
response items, as well as verification procedures. While data from the computer-based and online 
administration were automatically stored on central servers, data from the PBA had to be manually entered 
into a national database.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
118
Data required for processing and cleaning came from the following sources (depending on the administration 
mode):
•	
Data from the CBA for the student survey; and/or
•	
Data from the online data collection of school and teacher questionnaires; and/or
•	
IEA DME database including the response data for the PBA (student, school, and teacher);
•	
WinW3S database including participation, tracking, and sampling information. 
Computer-based administration (CBA) for the student survey
As documented in Chapter 7 of this report, one major change was the decision to offer countries the option 
of a computer-based delivery for the student survey. 
The CBA option allowed countries to administer the ICCS instruments in digital format and entailed the 
creation of players as applications to administer the ICCS instruments. The computer-based instruments 
could be administered in three different ways: (i) on USB sticks; (ii) online using a common internet browser; 
and (iii) via a local network server. Whereas web delivery ensured that data were immediately available on 
servers for further processing, USB delivery needed Test Administrators to upload the student response 
databases after the testing session.
To ensure confidentiality, student login information was provided to the Test Administrators with the 
student tracking forms. This login information corresponded to the ID and checksum provided by the IEA 
WinW3S, meaning that the identity validation step occurred when the respondents were logged-in to the 
survey.
Raw response data were saved on central servers hosted by RM Australia in native format (further information 
on how data were exactly stored is an intellectual property of RM Australia and cannot be described in 
more detail). Answers to constructed-response items were displayed in the AM Marker component of the 
Assessment Master™ system and assigned a numeric code by a human scorer (see details in Chapter 4 and 
Chapter 7). Answers to non-constructed-response items (e.g., multiple choice or drag and drop) were coded 
to numeric values. The data were transferred to flat files (CSV) for creating the data output suitable for 
import into the data processing system employed at IEA for all international large-scale assessments. Each 
row represented one respondent while each column represented a variable (i.e., a numeric representation 
of the answer). The produced data files matched the structure given in the international codebook. The 
data included all variables that held respondents’ direct responses or scores (human scores or computer 
scores). Some additional variables holding information either from the login (e.g., School ID) or from the 
system itself (e.g., a unique code for the session) were included. One file per instrument and country was 
created. For countries administering the instruments in different languages, the data were merged into one 
file per instrument. 
Online data collection of school and teacher questionnaires
Furthermore, ICCS offered an online collection of school and teacher questionnaire data instead of or in 
addition to using paper-based questionnaires.
To ensure confidentiality, national centers provided every respondent with a letter that contained individual 
login information along with information on how to access the online questionnaire. This login information 
corresponded to the ID and checksum provided by the IEA WinW3S, meaning that the identity validation 
step occurred when the respondents logged-in to the survey.
As respondents completed their online questionnaires, their data were automatically stored in central 
servers hosted by RM Australia and, therefore, no manual data entry was needed.
Raw response data were saved on central servers hosted by RM Australia in native format and then 
transferred to flat files (CSV) for creating the data output suitable for import into the data processing 
system employed at IEA for all international large-scale assessments (see the description of Computer-
based administration (CBA) for the student survey above).  

DATA MANAGEMENT, CLEANING, AND CREATION OF THE ICCS INTERNATIONAL DATABASE
119
Data entry and verification of paper instruments
Each national center was responsible for entering the information from any paper-based test booklets and 
questionnaires into computer data files using the IEA Data Management Expert (DME) software. The DME is 
a software system developed by IEA that facilitates data entry and incorporates validation checks to identify 
inconsistencies. As a general principle, national centers were instructed to enter data for any booklet or 
questionnaire that contained at least one valid response, discarding unused or empty instruments.
National centers entered responses from the paper instruments into data files created from an internationally 
predefined codebook. The codebook contained metadata information about the names, lengths, labels, valid 
ranges for continuous measures, counts or valid values for nominal or ordinal questions, and missing codes 
for each variable.
Before data entry commenced, national centers were required to adapt the international codebook 
structure to reflect any approved adaptations made to the national questionnaire versions (e.g., a nationally 
added response category).
To ensure consistency across participating countries, the basic rule for data entry in the DME required 
national staff to enter data “as is” without any interpretation, correction, truncation, imputation, or cleaning. 
Resolution of any inconsistencies remaining after this data entry stage was deferred until data cleaning (see 
the section later in this chapter on Cleaning the international and national databases).
The guiding principles for data entry included the following:
•	
Responses to categorical questions to be generally coded as “1” if the first option was used, “2” if the 
second option was marked, and so on.
•	
Responses to “check-all-that-apply” questions to be coded as either “1” (checked) or “9” (not checked/
omitted).
•	
Responses to numerical or scale questions (e.g., school enrolment) to be entered “as is,” that is, without 
any correction or truncation, even if the value was outside the originally expected range.
•	
Likewise, responses to filter questions and filter-dependent questions are to be entered exactly as filled 
in by the respondent, even if the information provided was logically inconsistent.
•	
Non-responses to be coded as “omitted.”
•	
Ambiguous responses, responses given outside of the expected format, or conflicting responses (e.g., 
selection of two options in a multiple-choice question), to be coded as “invalid.”
•	
Misprinted questions or items to be entered as “not administered.”
Data entered with the IEA DME were automatically validated. As each respondent ID number was entered, 
it was checked by the IEA DME software for alignment with a five-digit checksum generated by the IEA 
WinW3S. A mistype in either the ID or the checksum resulted in an error message prompting the data entry 
person to check the entered values. The data-verification module of the DME also checked for a range of 
other issues such as inconsistencies in identification codes and out-of-range or otherwise invalid codes. 
When such issues were flagged by the software, the individuals entering the data were prompted to resolve 
or confirm the inconsistencies before resuming data entry. In addition, national centers were requested 
to perform several checks that identified inconsistent records across datasets (e.g., a student record was 
available in the student questionnaire, but not in the student test). Data managers verified all findings prior 
to data submission and documented all changes or edits applied, as well as any verified findings that could 
not or did not need to be changed and therefore, could remain.
Double data entry
To check data entry reliability in participating countries, national centers were required to enter a random 
sample of 30 units of each non-blank survey instrument (test booklets and questionnaires) twice by two 
different data entry persons (punchers). IEA recommended that countries begin the double data entry 
process as early as possible during the data capture period in order to identify possible systematic incidental 
misunderstandings or mishandlings of data entry rules and to initiate appropriate remedial actions, for 
example, retraining national center staff. The IEA DME included a check to compare entries between 
different punchers and to list all discrepancies. Those entering the data were required to resolve identified 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
120
discrepancies between the first and second data entries by consulting the original instruments and applying 
the international rules in a uniform way.
While it was desirable that each and every discrepancy be resolved before submission of the complete 
dataset, the acceptable level of disagreement between the originally entered and double-entered data was 
established at one percent or less for questionnaire data and at the 0.1 percent or less level for the student 
test data. Values above this level required a complete re-entry of data. This restriction guaranteed that the 
margin of error observed for processed data remained well below the required threshold.
The level of disagreement between the originally entered and double-entered data was evaluated by IEA, and it 
was found that in general the margin of error observed for processed data was well below the required threshold.
Data verification at the national centers
Before IEA received the data for further processing, national centers carried out mandatory validation and 
verification steps on the data and undertook corrections as necessary.
After data collection, the data manager or other staff at each national center used the information from 
the survey tracking forms to verify the completeness of the data. Participation information (e.g., whether a 
student, teacher or principal participated in the assessment or was absent) was entered via the IEA WinW3S. 
The validation process was supported by an option in WinW3S to generate an inconsistency report. This 
report listed all types of discrepancies between variables recorded during the within-school sampling and 
test administration process and made it possible to cross-check these data against the actual availability of 
data entered in the IEA DME and/or the uploaded data on the central servers. 
For the paper-based administration, the national centres could export an Excel file from the IEA DME to 
verify paper data availability for each unique Student, Teacher, and School ID that was entered during manual 
data entry. However, for the computer-based administration, national centers could use a data monitoring 
tool that listed all records present on the upload server and allowed for downloading the list to be used for 
checking and updating the data availability status in WinW3S. The exported files were in turn imported into 
the IEA WinW3S and mapped against the list of Student, Teacher, and School IDs and reported participation 
statuses in the IEA WinW3S.
Data managers were requested to resolve all problems before the final data submission to IEA. If 
inconsistencies remained or the national center could not solve them, IEA asked the center to provide 
documentation on these problems. IEA used this documentation when processing the data at a later stage.
9.3	Data processing and data cleaning
Overview
As described earlier in this chapter, the data from the computer-based and online administration were 
transferred from central servers to CSV files for import into the data processing system employed at IEA. 
For the paper-based administration, the national center staff members in each participating country were 
responsible for entering their national ICCS data into the appropriate data files and submitting these files to 
IEA. In addition, each participating country had to submit the WinW3S database holding general information 
on respondents, as well as tracking and participation information.
Staff at IEA then subjected these data to a comprehensive process of checking and editing. To facilitate 
the data cleaning process, IEA asked the national centers to provide them with detailed documentation 
of their data together with their national data files. The data documentation included copies of all original 
survey tracking forms, the national versions of test booklets and questionnaires, as well as information from 
the survey activities questionnaire (see details in Chapter 7). National centers also submitted their final 
national adaptation forms (NAFs) in order to provide and confirm complete documentation on all national 
adaptations. In addition, national centers were asked to provide documentation on all changes or edits 
applied to the data prior to submission, as well as any verified findings that could remain.

DATA MANAGEMENT, CLEANING, AND CREATION OF THE ICCS INTERNATIONAL DATABASE
121
In order to ensure the integrity of the international database, a uniform data cleaning process was followed, 
involving regular consultation between IEA and the NRCs. After each country had submitted its data and 
required documentation, IEA, in collaboration with the NRCs, conducted a four-step cleaning procedure 
upon the submitted data and documentation:
1.	
Documentation and structure check;
2.	
Identification variable (ID) cleaning;
3.	
Linkage cleaning;
4.	
Context data cleaning (resolving inconsistencies in questionnaire data).
The cleaning process was an iterative process. Numerous iterations of the four-step cleaning procedure 
were completed on each national data set. This repetition ensured that all data were properly cleaned and 
that any new errors that could have been introduced during the data cleaning were rectified. The cleaning 
process was repeated as many times as necessary until all data were made consistent and comparable. Any 
inconsistencies detected during the cleaning process were resolved in collaboration with national centers, 
and all corrections made during the cleaning process were documented in a cleaning report, produced for 
each country.
After the final cleaning iteration, the databases with information about student participation and exclusion 
were passed to the IEA Sampling Unit, which used this information to calculate students’ participation rates, 
exclusion rates, and student sampling weights (see Chapter 10 for details). Afterwards, the data, including 
sampling weights, were sent to the ICCS ISC so that scaling could be performed (see Chapter 11 and 
Chapter 12 for details). The NRCs were provided with interim data products to review at different points 
in the process.
The main objective of the data cleaning process was to ensure that the data adhered to international 
formats, that school, teacher, and student information could be linked across different survey files, and that 
the data reflected the information collected within each country accurately and consistently.
The program-based data cleaning consisted of a set of activities explained in the following subsections 
(Figure 9.1). IEA carried out all of these activities in close communication with the national centers.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
122
Figure 9.1: Overview of the data cleaning process
Checking documentation, import and structure check
For each country, data cleaning began with an exploratory review of its data file structures and its data 
documentation, including a review of NAFs, student tracking forms, teacher tracking forms, and the survey 
activities questionnaire. IEA retrieved online and CBA data, including scored data from the scoring system, 
directly from the RM Assessment Master. National centers sent their WinW3S database, their DME 
database (if applicable) including scored data, and all required documentation to IEA. The fact that most 
centers sent this documentation greatly facilitated the data-cleaning process.
After the review, IEA first merged the tracking information and sampling information captured in IEA 
WinW3S database with the student-level databases containing the corresponding student instrument data 
from either paper- or computer-based assessment. During this step, IEA staff also merged the data from 
the school and teacher questionnaires for both the online and paper modes of administration. At this stage, 
data from the different sources were transformed and imported into one structured query language (SQL) 
database so that this information would be available during all further data-processing stages.
The first checks identified differences between the international and the national file structure. Some 
countries made adaptations to their questionnaires, such as adding national variables or omitting or 
modifying international variables. The extent and nature of such changes differed across countries. 
Some countries administered the questionnaires without any modifications (apart from translations and 
necessary adaptations relating to cultural or language-specific terms), whereas other countries inserted 
response categories within existing international variables or added national variables.

123
To keep track of adaptations, IEA asked the national centers to complete NAFs while they were adapting 
and translating the international version of the survey instruments. Where necessary, IEA modified the 
structure of the national data files to ensure that the resulting data remained comparable across countries. 
Details about country-specific adaptations to the international instruments can be found in Appendix B of 
the ICCS 2022 user guide for the international database (Afana et al., 2024).
IEA then discarded variables created purely for verification purposes and made provisions for adding new 
variables necessary for analysis and reporting, including reporting variables, derived variables, sampling 
weights, and scale scores.
Once IEA staff had ensured that each data file matched the international format, as specified in the 
international codebooks, they applied a series of standard data cleaning rules for further processing of the 
national data files. Processing at this stage employed software developed by IEA that could identify and 
correct inconsistencies in the data. Each potential problem flagged at this stage was identified by a unique 
problem number and then described and recorded in a database. The action taken by the cleaning program 
or by IEA staff with respect to each problem was also recorded.
IEA reported problems that could not be rectified automatically throughout the program to the responsible 
NRC so that national center staff could check the original data-collection instruments and tracking forms to 
trace the source of these errors. Wherever possible, staff at IEA suggested a remedy and asked the national 
centers to either accept it or propose an alternative. If a national center could not solve issues through 
verification of the instruments or forms, IEA applied a general cleaning rule to the files to rectify this error. 
When all automatic updates had been applied, IEA staff used SQL recoding scripts to directly apply any 
remaining corrections to the data files.
Checking identification variables
Each record in a data file needs to have a unique identification number. The existence of records with 
duplicate ID numbers in a file implies an error of some kind. Some countries administered the school and 
teacher questionnaire online in addition to the paper mode. This could yield the possibility that a respondent 
completed both the paper and the online versions of the questionnaire. Similarly, it was possible for a 
computer-based assessment login to be used (and responses uploaded) twice.
If two records in an ICCS database shared the same ID number and contained ‘exactly’ the same data, IEA 
deleted one of the records and kept the other one in the database. In the rare case that both records contained 
different data and IEA staff found it impossible to identify which record contained the more reliable or 
complete version of the data, national centers were asked to confirm which record should be kept.
Although the ID cleaning covered all data from all instruments, it focused mainly on the student data files. In 
addition to checking the unique student ID number, it was crucial to check variables pertaining to student 
participation and exclusion status, as well as students’ dates of testing in order to calculate student age at 
the time of testing. The student tracking forms provided an important tool for resolving anomalies in the 
database.
Checking linkage
As data on students, their schools, and teachers appeared in a number of different data files, a process of 
linkage cleaning was implemented to ensure that the data files would correctly link together. The linking of 
the data files followed a hierarchical system of identification codes that included school, class, teacher, and 
student components.1 These codes linked the students with their class and/or school membership, as well 
as teachers with their school.
Linkage cleaning consisted of several checks to verify that student entries matched between student test 
files, student questionnaire files, and scoring reliability files. In addition, at this stage, checks were conducted 
to ensure that teacher and student records were linked correctly with their corresponding schools. The 
1	 The ID number of a higher level is included in the ID number of a lower sampling level. The class ID includes the school ID, and 
the student ID includes the class ID (e.g., student 50120523 may be described as student 23 of class 05 in school 5012). The 
teacher ID includes the school ID (e.g., teacher 500103 may be described as teacher 3 in school 5001).

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
124
student tracking forms and teacher tracking forms were crucial in resolving any anomalies. IEA also liaised 
with NRCs about any problematic cases, and the national centers were provided with standardized reports 
listing all inconsistencies identified within the data.
Context data cleaning: Resolving inconsistencies in questionnaire data
The amount of inconsistent and implausible responses in questionnaire data files varied considerably 
among countries, however, none of the national datasets was completely free of inconsistent responses. 
IEA determined the treatment of inconsistent responses on a question-by-question basis using all available 
documentation to make an informed decision. IEA staff also checked all questionnaire data for consistency 
across the responses given.
For example, Question 21 in the school questionnaire asked for the total school enrollment (number of 
students) in all grades, while Question 22 asked for enrollment in the target grade only.
Clearly, the number given as a response to Question 22 could not possibly exceed the number provided by 
school principals in Question 21. IEA flagged inconsistencies of this kind and then asked the national centers 
to review these issues.
A filter question, which appeared in the teacher and the school questionnaires, directed respondents to 
a further question or section of the questionnaire. IEA applied the following cleaning rules to the filter 
question and the dependent questions that followed:
•	
If the answer to the filter question was “No” IEA recoded any responses to the dependent questions as 
“logically not applicable”;
•	
If the response to the filter question was omitted but at least one valid response was found in the 
dependent questions, then IEA recoded the filter question to “Yes.” This of course is only possible for 
dichotomous filter questions (e.g., with response options such as “Yes/No”).
IEA also applied what is known as split variable checks to questions where the answer was coded into several 
variables. For example, Question 3O in the student questionnaire asked students to provide information 
about all people living at home with them most or all of the time. Student responses were captured in a set 
of seven variables, each one coded as “Yes” if the corresponding “Yes” option was checked and “No” if the 
“No” option was filled in. Occasionally, students checked the “Yes” boxes but left the “No” boxes unchecked. 
Because, in these cases, it was clear that the unchecked boxes actually meant “No,” these responses were 
recoded accordingly, provided that the students had given affirmative responses in the other categories.
Two different sets of ICCS 2022 data indicated the age and gender of students. The first set was the tracking 
information provided by the School Coordinator or Test Administrator throughout the within-school 
sampling and test/questionnaire administration process. The second set comprised the actual responses 
given by students in the student questionnaires. In some cases, data across these two sets did not match 
and resolution was needed. If the information on gender or birth year and month was missing in the student 
questionnaire, but the student participated, this information, when available, was copied over from the 
tracking data to the questionnaire. If discrepancies were found between existing tracking and questionnaire 
gender and age data for students, IEA queried the case with the national center, and the national center 
investigated which source of information was correct. If unresolved, questionnaire data were trusted over 
tracking data for students.
The teacher questionnaire did not ask teachers to provide birth year and month, but rather to choose 
between six age-ranges. The year of birth, which was indicated in the tracking forms, was then recoded 
into respective age groups and cross-checked against the range indicated by the questionnaire responses. 
If gender and/or age-range information was missing from the teacher questionnaire, but the teacher 
participated, this data was copied over from the tracking information to the questionnaire. If discrepancies 
were found between existing tracking and questionnaire gender and age data, the questionnaire information 
replaced the tracking information.

DATA MANAGEMENT, CLEANING, AND CREATION OF THE ICCS INTERNATIONAL DATABASE
125
Handling of missing data
Two types of entries were possible during the ICCS 2022 data capture: valid data values and missing data 
values. During data capture, missing data was assigned a value of “omitted,” “invalid,” or “not administered.” 
IEA applied additional missing codes to the data to facilitate further analyses. This process led to five 
different types of missing data being distinguished in the international database:
•	
Omitted: the respondent had a chance to answer the question but did not do so; the corresponding 
question or item was thus left blank.
•	
Not administered: this signified that the item or question was not administered to the respondent, which 
meant that the respondent could not read and answer the question. The not administered missing code 
was used for those student test items that were not in the set of assessment blocks administered to 
a student either deliberately (due to the rotation of assessment blocks) or, in very few cases, due to 
technical failure or incorrect translations. This missing code was also used for those records that were 
included in the international database but did not contain a single response to one of the assigned 
questionnaires. This situation applied to students who participated in the student test but did not 
answer the questionnaire, or vice versa. In addition, the not administered code was used for individual 
questionnaire items that a national center decided not to include in the country-specific version of 
the questionnaire. Furthermore, if a particular question or item (or a whole page) was misprinted or 
for other reasons not available to the respondent, then the corresponding variable(s) is coded as not 
administered.
•	
Invalid (only applicable for paper-based administration): this code was used in both the questionnaire 
and the test files for responses that were not interpretable (e.g., when respondents ticked more than 
one box in a multiple-choice question).
•	
Logically not applicable: the respondent answered a filter question in a way that made the dependent 
questions following it not applicable to him or her.
•	
Not reached: this applied only to the individual items of the student test and indicated those items that 
students did not attempt due to a lack of time. ‘Not reached’ codes were derived as follows: First, the 
last answer given by a student in a session is identified. This could be either a valid or invalid response 
to an item. Following, the first omitted response after this last answer is coded as ‘omitted,’ but all 
following responses to these items in the session are then coded as ‘not reached.’ For example, the 
response pattern ‘1 9 4 2 9 9 9 9 9 9’ (where ‘9’ represents ‘omitted’) is recoded to ‘1 9 4 2 9 R R R R R’ 
(where ‘R’ represents ‘not reached’).
Data cleaning quality control
Because ICCS 2022 was a large and highly complex study with very high standards for data quality, 
maintaining these standards required an extensive set of interrelated data cleaning procedures. To ensure 
that all procedures were conducted in the correct sequence, that no special requirements were overlooked, 
and that the cleaning process was implemented independently of the persons in charge, the data quality 
control included the following steps:
•	
Thorough testing of all data cleaning programs: before applying the programs to real datasets, IEA 
applied them to simulation datasets containing all possible problems and inconsistencies.
•	
Registering all incoming data and documents in a specific database: IEA recorded the date of arrival, as 
well as specific issues requiring attention.
•	
Carrying out data cleaning according to strict rules: deviations from the cleaning sequence were not 
possible, and the scope for involuntary changes to the cleaning procedures was minimal.
•	
Documenting all systematic data recodings that applied to all countries: IEA recorded these in the ICCS 
main survey general cleaning documentation that was provided to NRCs and National Data Managers 
(NDMs).
•	
Logging every “manual” correction to a country’s data files in a recoding script: logging these changes, 
which occurred only occasionally, allowed IEA staff to undo changes or to redo the whole manual 
cleaning process at any later stage of the data cleaning process.
•	
Repeating, on completion of data cleaning for a country, all cleaning steps from the beginning: this step 
allowed IEA to detect any problems that might have been inadvertently introduced during the data 
cleaning process.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
126
•	
Working closely with national centers and at different steps of the cleaning process: IEA provided 
national centers with the processed data files and accompanying documentation and statistics so that 
center staff could thoroughly review and correct any identified inconsistencies.
IEA compared national adaptations recorded in the documentation for the national dataset against the 
structure of the submitted national data files. Whenever possible, IEA recoded national deviations to 
match the international version to ensure consistency with the international data structure. However, 
if international comparability could not be guaranteed, IEA removed the corresponding data from the 
international database. IEA staff then documented any national adaptations made to the international 
version of the ICCS 2022 questionnaires in the ICCS 2022 user guide for the international database (Afana 
et al., 2024).
9.4	Interim data products
Building the ICCS 2022 international database was an iterative process. On completion of each major 
data-processing step, IEA sent a new version of data files to the national centers so that staff could review 
their data and run their own separate checks to validate the new data-file versions. This process meant that 
national centers received several versions of their data, and their data only, before release of the draft and 
final versions of the international database. All interim data were made available in full to the ISC at ACER, 
whereas, as mentioned, each participating country received only its own data. 
Before the international databases were finalized, three major interim versions of the data files were sent 
to each country.
The first version was sent as soon as the data could be considered “clean” with regard to the four-step 
cleaning procedures described earlier in this chapter. The goal of this data verification and validation 
phase was to provide the participating countries with an opportunity to review a version of their national 
data as originally collected and/or derived after IEA implemented per-country or general edits after data 
were submitted. The intention was to identify residual errors of systematic or incidental nature before the 
national databases were finalized with regard to weighting and scaling.
A second version of the data files was sent to countries when preliminary weights and scale scores were 
available and had been merged with the data files. The weights already reflected decisions made at the 
adjudication stage (see details in Chapter 10). Corrections due to feedback from countries, and general 
corrections or updates were implemented in this version. The goal of this data verification phase was to 
provide the participating countries with an opportunity to review their national data after additional 
country-specific and general corrections, and after weighting and scaling took place, before the national 
databases were finalized, in order to create the draft international database for analysis and reporting.
A third version, the draft international database, was made available to all participating countries under an 
affidavit of non-disclosure, reflecting all residual corrections, full ID scrambling, and general confidentiality 
edits (for more information, please refer to the ICCS 2022 user guide for the international database (Afana 
et al., 2024)). This version contained only records that satisfied the sampling standards and allowed the 
NRCs to replicate the results presented in the international reports. This version could also be used to 
prepare for national reporting.
To enable the participating countries to thoroughly review the cleaning process, three further documents 
were supplied: a cleaning report, listing remaining case-level findings; a report documenting recodings for 
all country-specific data edits applied by IEA; and a general cleaning documentation, describing in detail the 
data processing done at IEA. An international codebook documenting the structure of all data files and a 
report documenting all national adaptations were also appended to each data version.

DATA MANAGEMENT, CLEANING, AND CREATION OF THE ICCS INTERNATIONAL DATABASE
127
9.5	Final product: the ICCS 2022 international database
The ICCS international database incorporated all national data files from participating countries.
The data processing and cleaning effort implemented at IEA ensured that the ICCS 2022 international 
database contained high-quality data. More specifically, the process ensured that:
•	
Information coded in each variable was internationally comparable;
•	
National adaptations were reflected appropriately in all variables;
•	
All entries in the database could be successfully linked within and across levels;
•	
Only those records adjudicated as participating remained in the international database files; and
•	
Sampling weights and scale scores were available for international comparisons.
The indirect identification of individuals was prevented by applying general confidentiality edits, such as 
scrambling of identification variables and jackknife zone information. Furthermore, some of the personal 
data variables needed during field operations and data processing only were removed, and variables that 
were identified as highly identifying were suppressed or categorized.
Two versions of the international database are available. The Public Use Files (PUF) are available to the 
general public, whereas the Restricted Use Files (RUF) are available to researchers only upon request (from 
IEA). In the Public Use File, some variables are removed or categorized to minimize the risk of disclosing 
confidential information; the Restricted Use File is an extended version for scientific use.
More information about the ICCS international database is provided in the ICCS 2022 user guide for the 
international database (Afana et al., 2024).
Reference
Afana, Y., Brese, F., Kowolik, H., Cortes, D., Schulz, W. (Eds.). (2024). ICCS 2022 user guide for the international 
database. International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA).

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT

Chapter 10:
Weighting procedures
Umut Atasever and Diego Cortes
10.1	
Overview
A major objective of the International Civic and Citizenship Education Study (ICCS) is to derive and report 
accurate, precise, and internationally comparable estimates of population parameters for each participating 
country or benchmarking entity. To derive estimates with these characteristics, it is necessary to use 
complex estimators that account for several survey-design features. This chapter focuses on the role of 
estimation weights, which are the product of two features of our survey design. The first one is related 
to varying probabilities of inclusion across students or teachers, which makes probability weighting 
necessary to obtain unbiased estimates of population parameters. The second feature is related to weight 
adjustments that are necessary to obtain point estimates when the data collection process is subject to 
unit non-response. Adjustments are implemented under a non-response model that assumes that any unit 
non-response is ignorable within an adjustment cell. Since unit non-response plays an important role in the 
estimation of population parameters through weight adjustments, this chapter also discusses participation 
rates and outlines the different conditions under which estimates are reported in ICCS 2022.
This chapter begins by defining school participation in ICCS 2022. There is a long-standing policy implemented 
in ICCS that states that schools where only a minority of students or teachers participate in the study should 
not be considered as participating in the student or teacher study. This is applied because it is hard to justify 
that non-response is ignorable within a school when the participation rate is very low. Therefore, if a school 
had a participation rate of students or teachers lower than 50 percent, then the school was considered as 
not participating in the student or teacher survey.
The next three sections of the chapter contain a description of the sets of weights that were computed 
to ensure that results based on ICCS 2022 data resembled those in the underlying target populations. 
It describes how probability weights and adjustment weights were computed for participating students, 
classes, teachers, and schools. All reported estimates for ICCS 2022 were derived using weighted estimation 
and researchers conducting secondary analysis with this data should follow this approach. 
The final section of this chapter describes the participation rates at each sampling stage, the minimum 
acceptable participation requirements (unweighted and weighted) for students and teachers, and the 
categories of sample implementation quality that each country or benchmark entity achieved. The ICCS 
2022 team regarded response rates as an important indicator of data quality. Although national centers 
made considerable efforts to ensure full participation, not all sampled units were included in the study. 
National samples were adjudicated accordingly with respect to sample participation requirements in the 
student and teacher surveys. 
Finally, as described in Chapter 6, 11 countries transitioned into a computer-based assessment (CBA) in 
2022. For these countries, an additional sample of students was surveyed in ICCS 2022 as part of the bridge 
study. Each of the processes described in the remainder of this chapter were implemented separately for 
both student surveys (core study and bridge study). This chapter does not distinguish between these two 
surveys when describing procedures in the text; however, the chapter does summarize and report statistics 
for ICCS 2022 and the ICCS 2022 bridge study separately.
10.2	
Within-school participation requirements
Student survey participation requirements
When the student response rate within a school is very low, the likelihood of biased results increases—
provided that within-school weight adjustments are applied. Accordingly, ICCS defined a required minimum 
student participation rate within each school. This rate determined whether a school was considered as a 
“participant” in ICCS 2022. 

WEIGHTING PROCEDURES
129
In most participating countries or benchmark entities, only one class per school was selected for ICCS 2022. 
In this scenario, schools had to meet the following participation requirement: 
•	
A sampled school was regarded as “participating school” if, in its sampled class, at least 50 percent of its 
eligible students had participated in the student study.
If a school did not meet this requirement, then all students within the school were regarded as non-
participating.
In a small number of countries or benchmark entities, the selected school sample contained some schools 
where more than one classroom was selected. For these schools, the participation requirement was 
modified as follows: 
•	
A sampled class was regarded as “participating class” if at least 50 percent of its eligible students 
participated.
•	
A sampled school was regarded as “participating school” if at least one sampled class was regarded as 
participating. If a school sampled two classes and one had a participation rate above 50 percent and 
the other below this threshold, then one class was considered as participating and the other as not 
participating.
Whenever there was an indication that the survey operation procedures in a school were not followed 
properly, the school was regarded as non-participant. For example, if a school had not listed all their eligible 
classes for class sample selection, the corresponding student data from that school were not included in the 
ICCS 2022 database.
Teacher survey participation requirements
Similar to the process used for the student survey, each school had to meet a minimum teacher participation 
requirement to be counted as participating:
•	
A school was regarded as a “participating school” in the teacher survey if at least 50 percent of its 
sampled teachers had participated.
If a school did not meet this requirement, it was regarded as a non-participant with respect to the teacher 
survey. 
If the survey operation procedures in a school were not followed properly, the school was regarded as not 
participating. For example, if a school had not listed all their eligible teachers for teacher sample selection, 
or if the teacher selection procedures had not been followed, the teacher data from this school were not 
included in the ICCS 2022 database.
10.3	
Weighting components for student survey
Student weights in ICCS 2022 databases are the product of several components, which could be grouped 
into two different types:
•	
Base weights reflect the inclusion probabilities of sampled units. At each stage of the sampling process, 
the base weight is the inverse of the inclusion probability of a sampled unit. 
•	
Non-response adjustments reflect the probability of participation for each sampled unit, given that 
non-participation is ignorable within each adjustment cell (i.e., stratum or school). At each stage of the 
sampling process, non-response adjustments are the inverse of the probability of participation.
School base weight (WGTFAC1)
The first stage of sampling for ICCS 2022 involved selecting the schools in each country or benchmark entity. 
The school base weight reflects the selection probabilities of this sampling step. When explicit stratification 
was used, the school samples were selected independently in each explicit stratum h, with h=1,…,H, and H is 
the total number of strata in the country or benchmark entity. If no explicit strata were formed, the entire 
country was regarded as being one explicit stratum. 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
130
In most contexts, ICCS 2022 drew a systematic sample of schools with the selection probability of school 
i proportional to its size. Usually, the measure of school size Mhi was defined by the number of students in 
the ICCS target grade. If schools were small (smaller than the average class size in the explicit stratum), the 
measure of size Mhi was defined as the average size of all small schools in that stratum. This was done to 
reduce the variability of school base weights across sampled schools.
The school base weight was defined as the inverse of the school's selection probability. For school i in 
stratum h, the school base weight, WGTFAC1hi, was given by:

method with equal selection probabilities for each class. In this sampling step, the class base weight was the 
inverse of the selection probability.

Weighting components for school survey
ICCS was designed as a student and teacher survey, but not specifically as a school survey. Any statements 
about school-level variables must be treated cautiously because they can be subject to substantial sampling 
errors. However, school weights were calculated for ICCS 2022, and included in the international database, 
to also allow weighted analyses of data from school questionnaires.1

non-participants in the survey of school principals. To account for the non-responding school principals

10.7	
ICCS standards for sampling participation
Despite the efforts of national centers to achieve high participation rates in the aftermath of school closures 
during the COVID-19 pandemic across many countries, higher levels of non-response were evident in several 
participating countries or benchmarking entities. As is customary in IEA studies, ICCS 2022 established 
guidelines for reporting data for countries with less than full participation. Three categories for sampling 
participation were defined, and these were applied separately to the student and the teacher survey. 
ICCS 2022 reporting strategy was the same as the one in previous cycles (see, for example, Weber et al., 
2018). That is, countries grouped in Category 1 met the ICCS 2022 sampling requirements. Countries in 

WEIGHTING PROCEDURES
141
Category 2 met these requirements only after the inclusion of replacement schools. Countries in Category 
3 failed to meet the ICCS 2022 sample participation requirements. During an ICCS sampling adjudication 
meeting in February 2023, the ICCS 2022 sampling referee Marc Joncas made binding decisions as to 
which country or benchmarking entity would be grouped in which category.
Student survey participation standards
Categories for sampling participation in the ICCS 2022 student survey were defined according to strict 
criteria ( Figure 10.1). 
Figure 10.1: Categories into which countries were placed with respect to student sampling participation rates
Category 1: Satisfactory sampling participation rate without the use of replacement schools. 
A country was in this category if:
•	
It had an unweighted school response rate without replacement of at least 85 percent (after 
rounding to the nearest whole percentage point) and an unweighted student response rate (after 
rounding) of at least 85 percent
or
•	
A weighted school response rate without replacement of at least 85 percent (after rounding to the 
nearest whole percentage point) and a weighted student response rate (after rounding) of at least 
85 percent
or 
•	
The product of the (unrounded) weighted school response rate without replacement and the 
(unrounded) weighted student response rate was at least 75 percent (after rounding to the nearest 
whole percentage point).
Category 2: Satisfactory sampling participation rate only when replacement schools were 
included. 
A country was in this category if:
•	
It failed to meet the requirements for Category 1 but has either an unweighted or weighted school 
response rate without replacement of at least 50 percent (after rounding to the nearest whole 
percentage point)
and had either
•	
An unweighted school response rate with replacement of at least 85 percent (after rounding to the 
nearest whole percentage point) and an unweighted student response rate (after rounding) of at 
least 85 percent
or 
•	
A weighted school response rate with replacement of at least 85 percent (after rounding to nearest 
whole percentage point) and a weighted student response rate (after rounding) of at least 85 
percent
or
•	
The product of the (unrounded) weighted school response rate with replacement and the 
(unrounded) weighted student response rate was at least 75 percent (after rounding to the nearest 
whole percentage point).
Category 3: Unacceptable sampling response rate even when replacement schools are included.
If a country did not meet the requirements for Category 1 or Category 2 but could provide documentation 
showing that they had complied with ICCS sampling procedures, it was placed in Category 3.
Teacher survey participation standards
The sampling participation categories for the teacher survey were similar to those in the student survey. 
High response rates in the teacher survey were generally harder to obtain than for the student survey. 
However, there is no statistical justification for treating teacher data differently from student data with 
respect to an assessment of possible non-response bias, especially as teachers’ motivation to participate 
in ICCS 2022 may have depended on the subjects they were teaching, or on their general attitude towards 
civic and citizenship education. Because non-response generally held a high potential for bias in both parts

Category 1: Satisfactory sampling participation rate without the use of replacement schools. 
A country was in this category if:

rounding to the nearest whole percentage point) and an unweighted teacher response rate (after 
rounding) of at least 85 percent
or
•	
A weighted school response rate without replacement of at least 85 percent (after rounding to the 
nearest whole percentage point) and a weighted teacher response rate (after rounding) of at least 
85 percent
or
•	
The product of the (unrounded) weighted school response rate without replacement and the 
(unrounded) weighted teacher response rate of at least 75 percent (after rounding to the nearest 
whole percentage point).
Category 2: Satisfactory sampling participation rate only when replacement schools were 
included. 
A country was in category 2 if:
•	
It failed to meet the requirements for Category 1 but had either an unweighted or weighted school 
response rate without replacement of at least 50 percent (after rounding to the nearest whole 
percentage point)
and had either
•	
An unweighted school response rate with replacement of at least 85 percent (after rounding to the 
nearest whole percentage point) and an unweighted teacher response rate (after rounding) of at 
least 85 percent

(unrounded) weighted teacher response rate of at least 75 percent (after rounding to the nearest 
whole percentage point).
Category 3: Unacceptable sampling response rate even when replacement schools are included.
If a country did not meet the requirements for Category 1 or Category 2 but could provide documentation 
showing that they had complied with ICCS sampling procedures, it was placed in Category 3.
Reporting data
In those instances where a participating country could not be placed in participation Category 1, the ICCS 
2022 team considered it necessary to make readers of the international reports aware of the increased 
potential for bias. 
Based on the sample participation categories, the survey results were reported in different ways: 
•	
Category 1: Countries in this category appeared in the tables and figures in international reports without 
annotation.
•	
Category 2: Countries in this category were annotated in the tables and figures in international reports.
•	
Category 3: Countries in this category appeared in a separate section of the tables. 
of the study, the participation requirements for the teacher survey were as strict as the ones for the student 
survey. Three categories for teacher sampling participation were defined (Figure 10.2). 
Figure 10.2: Categories into which countries were placed with respect to teacher sampling participation rates

In Schleswig-Holstein (Germany), the teacher participation rate was exceptionally low, which made it 
impossible to generalize from sample data to population characteristics. Therefore, the sampling team did not 
calculate weights for these teacher data, and the benchmarking participant was not included in the analyses of 
teacher data for the ICCS 2022 international report.

Chapter 11:
Scaling procedures for civic knowledge test 
items
Wolfram Schulz and Greg Macaskill
11.1 Overview
This chapter describes the procedures used to analyze and scale the International Civic and Citizenship 
Education Study (ICCS) 2022 international test items administered to measure students’ civic knowledge. 
The chapter covers these topics:
•	
The scaling model used to analyze and scale the test items;
•	
Test coverage and item dimensionality;
•	
Assessment of item fit;
•	
Assessment of scorer reliabilities for open-ended items;
•	
Differential item functioning by gender;
•	
Review of cross-national measurement equivalence;
•	
International item adjudication;
•	
International item calibration and test reliability;
•	
International ability estimates in the form of plausible values (PV); and
•	
Estimation of changes in civic content knowledge between previous cycles and 2022.
The development of the ICCS 2022 test items is described in Chapter 2 and was guided by the ICCS 2022 
Assessment Framework (see Schulz et al., 2023). 
Data used for the main survey scaling analyses presented in this chapter include those from the computer-
based assessment (CBA), used in 16 countries and the two German benchmarking entities, and paper-based 
assessment (PBA), used in six countries. The CBA included 11 clusters of items common to both delivery 
modes plus three clusters of items used in the CBA only.  The PBA included the 11 clusters of items common 
to the CBA only. 
The main survey also included a bridging study for the purpose of investigating the effect of the delivery 
mode on measuring student outcome variables. All countries that participated in ICCS 2016 (for which all 
student assessments were delivered on paper) and were participating in ICCS 2023 using the CBA mode 
participated in the bridging study. The review of delivery mode effects used CBA data collected as part of 
the main survey these countries (using only the data from the 11 clusters of items common to both modes), 
as well as data from PBA conducted as part of the bridging study (using eight of the 11 clusters of items 
common to both modes). Comparisons between main survey and bridging study data in these 11 countries 
were used to review mode effects on how students responded to test items.
11.2 Scaling Model
We used item response theory (IRT) scaling methodology to scale the test items. 
Use of the one-parameter (Rasch) model (Rasch, 1960) for dichotomous items means that the probability of 
selecting Category 1 instead of 0 is modeled as:
Where Pi (θn) is the probability for person n to score 1 on item i, θn is the ability of person n, and δi is the 
location of item i on this dimension. For each item, item responses are modeled as a function of the latent 
trait θn. 

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST ITEMS
145
More generally, if item i is a polytomous item with score categories 0, 1, 2, …, mi, we used the Partial Credit 
Model (Masters, 1982). The probability of person n scoring x on item i is then given by:
Here, Pi (θn) denotes the probability of person n scoring x on item i and θn denotes the person’s ability. The item 
parameter δi gives the location of the item on the latent continuum while τik is an additional step parameter. 
The process of producing estimates of ability, difficulty (location), and step parameters is sometimes referred 
to as item calibration. Estimation of scaling parameters is not straightforward and requires elaborate 
iterative numerical algorithms and extensive quality control by experienced data analysts. To perform item 
calibration for the ICCS 2022 test data, we used the scaling software package ACER ConQuest, Version 5 
(Adams et al., 2020).
11.3	
 Scaling outcomes
Test targeting and item dimensionality
Test targeting
When measuring cognitive abilities, it is important to use test items that cover the different levels of ability 
found in the target population. As a start point, we estimated the distribution of cognitive abilities among 
ICCS 2022 students and the location of item thresholds (with a response probability (rp) of 0.5; see Figure 
11.1). Item thresholds were equal to item difficulties for dichotomous items. For partial credit items, a 
difficulty threshold was estimated for each score, indicated by .1, .2, or .3 appended to the item number. This 
mapping comparing student abilities and item thresholds is known as a Wright Map.1
Figure 11.1: Mapping of student abilities and item thresholds
1	 This terminology was introduced by Wilson and Draney (2002) in honor of the psychometrics expert Ben Wright (University 
of Chicago).

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
146
The range of item difficulties broadly matched the abilities found in the student population. However, the 
average item difficulties were somewhat lower than the average student abilities. Overall, ICCS 2022 test 
items were slightly better targeted to students in the lower civic knowledge ranges in comparison to the 
higher civic knowledge ranges, a lot of items had difficulties between -1 and 0 logits.2 The nature of this 
targeting varied across countries according to the distribution of students’ civic knowledge within each 
country.  
Item dimensionality 
In response to the new areas of increased emphasis in the ICCS 2022 Assessment Framework (Schulz et al., 
2023) the ICCS test instrument included 49 items mapped to the Global Citizenship Education/Education 
for Sustainable Development (GCED/ESD) themes—28 aligned with ESD and 21 aligned with GCED. Using 
items that were assigned to each of GCED, ESD and “Other ICCS” domains, we explored the associations 
across these three domains applying a multi-dimensional IRT model (see, for example, von Davier & 
Carstensen, 2007) which allows us to obtain estimated correlations between latent dimensions. This model 
is akin to a confirmatory factor analysis (Bollen, 1989) but within an IRT modeling framework. 
As a criterion for judging dimensionality, we relied on reviewing correlations between the latent dimensions, 
given that statistical tests of relative model fit are cumbersome in view of the large sample sizes. However, 
it needs to be acknowledged that these correlations were calculated based on data from the pooled sample, 
and that lower correlations might be observed when using individual national samples.
When estimating the multi-dimensional IRT model with three factors reflecting item content related to 
GCED, ESD and other (non-GCED/ESD) aspects, we found latent correlations of 0.88 between GCED 
and ESD, 0.93 between GCED and other aspects, and 0.92 between ESD and other aspects. While these 
correlations are quite high, they suggest some potential difference between the sustainability dimension 
and the two other dimensions.
We further reviewed, using the same multi-dimensional IRT approach described above, two other possible 
dimensionality structures associated with the conceptual structure of the ICCS 2022 Assessment 
Framework3: 
•	
Cognitive domains (knowing vs reasoning and applying, with 94 and 42 items respectively); and
•	
Content domains, civic institutions, and systems with civic roles and identities as a single domain (due 
to low item numbers for the latter domain and the conceptual coherence between these two domains) 
versus civic principles versus civic participation. The number of items in the resultant three domains are 
43, 51, and 42 respectively.
A two-dimensional model was used to review the correlation between the two cognitive domains. The latent 
correlation (see Bollen, 1989) between the domains was 0.94, which suggests that items from each domain 
measure highly similar aspects of civic knowledge.
A three-dimensional model was used to review the correlation among the three content domains: (i) civic 
institutions and systems/civic roles and identities4; (ii) civic principles; and (iii) civic participation. The results 
also showed very high correlations between each pair of domains. The latent correlation between civic 
institutions and systems/civic roles and identities and each of civic principles and civic participation was 
0.94 and 0.92 respectively. The latent correlation between civic principles and civic participation was 0.93. 
These results are consistent with those of previous ICCS cycles where civic knowledge was only reported 
as a unidimensional construct.
Analyses of dimensionality suggested that civic knowledge could be reported as a unidimensional scale. 
However, it is expected that secondary analyses will use ICCS 2022 cognitive data to further explore civic 
2	 The logit of a probability p is defined as log(p/1-p). It maps the probability onto the full real line.
3	 Table 1.1 in the ICCS 2022 international report (Schulz et al., 2024) includes information about the number of items in the 
intersection of these domains.
4	 Content domains 1 and 4 from the ICCS assessment framework were combined in this analysis because of their conceptual 
congruence and the relatively small number of items associated with content domain 4.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST ITEMS
147
knowledge sub-domains of specific research interest, especially regarding the ESD- and GCED-related sub-
domains.
Student non-response data
There are four possible types of student non-response that could appear in the ICCS 2022 test data: 
•	
omitted items (coded as 9): items to which a student was exposed but did not enter a response;
•	
not administered items (coded as 8): items to which a student was not exposed (most commonly those 
items in clusters that were not in the test form completed by a given student because of the rotated 
cluster design), but in some cases responses were also missing due to technical issues (CBA) or printing 
errors (PBA); 
•	
invalid responses (coded as 7): responses that could not be scored because they are inconsistent with 
the interpretable response set—such as if a student were to select two responses to a multiple-choice 
item. These were relevant to the ICCS PBA only because the CBA environment controls the response 
formats available to students; and
•	
“not reached” items (coded as 6): this is a derived category of non-response based on evidence from 
strings of omitted items at the end of a test form completed by a student. If the final valid response in a 
test form completed by a student was followed by more than two omitted responses, the first omitted 
response remained coded as 9 while all remaining trailing omitted responses were deemed to have 
been not reached and were recoded accordingly.
Table 11.1 shows the average percentages of omitted responses for each item across each delivery 
mode (main survey CBA, main survey PBA, bridging study PBA) and the average percentages of invalid 
responses for items when delivered on paper (invalid responses did not occur when items were delivered 
on computer). The percentages for computer-delivered items were also calculated only for the 11 countries 
that participated in the bridging study (column 5). This makes it possible to compare them with the PBA 
bridging study averages (column 6) calculated using the paper-based bridging study data for the same set 
of countries.

MC = Multiple choice; CR = Constructed Response; DD = Drag and drop; LT=Large task
For multiple-choice (MC) items, the average percentage of omitted responses was below one percent (Table 
11.2) and varied only slightly by delivery mode. A slightly higher rate of omission was found for large task (LT) 
items and drag and drop (DD) items that had about five percent missing by omission. Constructed response (CR) 

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST ITEMS
151
items, where students were required to write or type a response, had the highest proportions of omitted 
responses across all four item types, around about nine percent on average. This was fairly consistent with 
what was found in ICCS 2016 where omissions were around nine percent on average for CR items. For this 
item format, the average proportion of omitted responses was higher for paper-based responses (11.94% 
(PBA) and 9.28% (PBA bridging study) than for computer-based responses (7.71% (CBA) and 6.57% (CBA 
bridging study)). Similar observations about differences in missing proportions between computer- and 
paper-based test data were also found in other studies (see, for example, Kroehne et al., 2019).
The percentage of invalid responses was lower for PBA bridging study data than in the PBA mode, though 
both were fairly low. The very low percentages of invalid responses to CR items were to be expected, as 
there are very few ways in which an invalid response can be generated by students for this item format

Assessment of item fit
Goodness of fit for individual items can be determined by calculating a mean square statistic (Wright & 
Masters, 1982). Reviewing this residual-based item fit provides an indication of the extent to which each 
item is consistent with the item response model. However, there are no clear rules for acceptable item fit, 
and some statisticians recommend that analysts and researchers interpret residual-based statistics with 
caution (see, for example, Rost & von Davier, 1994). We consequently assessed item fit by using a range of 
item statistic, including the item-rest correlations and the weighted mean square (infit) statistic given in the 
table below.
We determined the item-rest correlations of correct responses (or partial credit responses) and the 
weighted item fit statistics (Table 11.3). Almost all items had item-total correlations above 0.20 (items with 
lower discrimination than 0.20 are considered only weakly related to the latent variable that is measured), 
and item CI4SLM2 had a low item-rest correlation of 0.16. The constructed items CI2WFO2, CI4ZHL2_R, 
and CI4ZSL3_R had weighted infit statistics above 1.2 (indicating lower-than expected discrimination), 
however, all of them had item-rest correlation of 0.20 or more.

Table 11.3: Item facilities, total-score correlations, and weighted item fit for international sample
We also used ACER ConQuest to generate item characteristic curves (ICCs). These provide a graphical 
representation of item fit across the range of student abilities for each item, including dichotomous and 
partial credit items.
The ICC for item CI4SLM2 shows the discrimination was not satisfactory and that the proportion of 
students with correct answers did not increase as expected by the scaling model (Figure 11.2). Given this 
outcome, we decided not to retain this item for scaling.
Figure 11.2: Item characteristic curve by category for item CI4SLM2 

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST ITEMS
153
Assessment of scorer agreement
In order to score the open-ended items in the ICCS 2022 cognitive test, we followed the scoring guides 
that were refined as an outcome of experiences in the international field trial of new test items. For the 
CBA mode, the RM AM Marker software was configured so that approximately 200 responses to each 
item were scored by a second scorer. In the online scoring context, scorers were unaware of whether any 
given response had been allocated to be double scored. For the PBA mode, subsamples of approximately 
200 student responses were double scored. This required manual handling of the booklets. In this context, 
scorers were aware of whether or not a response they were scoring was allocated to be double scored but 
were not able to see any scores allocated by the other scorer. All double scoring across both delivery modes 
was therefore completed with scorers unaware of any other scores given to responses they may have 
scored. This double-scoring procedure allowed us to assess scorer agreement and the national percentages 
of scorer agreement, which ranged between 53 and 100 percent (Table 11.4). On average, scorer agreement 
for the items was between 70 and 90 percent.5 Percentages of 100 percent indicate that national centers 
may have misinterpreted the international guidelines and used reconciled scoring data for the recording of 
double-scoring results.
Table 11.4: Percentages of scorer agreement for open-ended ICCS test items
5	 70 percent was the lower bound of expected reliability for this study.

Differential item functioning by gender
IRT scaling models assume the probability to be only a function of the measured latent variable and not 
of any group membership. Therefore, we explored the quality of the items by assessing differential item 
functioning (DIF). DIF occurs when sub-groups of students with the same degree of ability have different 
probabilities of responding correctly to an item. For example, if boys have a higher probability than girls 
with the same degree of ability of correctly answering an item, the item shows gender DIF. In this study 
we reviewed DIF across groups defined by gender (female vs male) and national context (24 participating 
education systems).  
We derived estimates of gender DIF by including interaction terms in the item response model. We used 
the following facet model to investigate the occurrence of gender DIF for dichotomously scored test items:
θn denotes the ability of person n and δi the location of item i. For the purpose of measuring parameter 
equivalence across the two gender groups, we included two extra terms in the scaling model. The additional 
parameter for gender effects is denoted by λig. To obtain proper estimates, we also needed to include the 
overall gender effect (ηg) in the model. We constrained both item-by-gender interaction estimates (λig) and 
overall gender effects (ηg) to have a sum of 0. 
For constructed items with more than two categories, this was modeled as:

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST ITEMS
155
The difference in item difficulty between gender groups was estimated by multiplying the item-by-gender 
interaction estimates by two to obtain the estimated difference between the two comparison groups and 
was reviewed for each item (Table 11.5). Based on pooled data from equally weighted countries that were 
included in the final calibration we observed 18 items for which the estimated differences in ability between 
gender groups was larger than 0.3 logits, which was used as a threshold equivalent to about a third of a 
standard deviation of the measured ability. However, occurrences of more considerable gender DIF were 
roughly equally balanced between females (8) and males (10).
