Cross-national measurement equivalence
With any test used to assess student ability cross-nationally, it is important that the test items function 
similarly across those countries. Items show item-by-country interaction when students with the same ability, 
but from different countries, vary in their probability of correctly answering these questions. Items with 
considerable lack of measurement invariance across countries should undergo careful examination as 
they may indicate translation deviations or being not appropriate to national contexts. For the main survey 
analyses of ICCS 2022 test items, we compared national calibrations with international item difficulty 
parameters in order to assess the occurrence of item-by-country interaction. We then computed confidence 
intervals for each national item difficulty parameter, basing the computation on the respective standard 
errors and adjusting them for possible design effects and for multiple comparisons.
Figure 11.3: Example of item-by-country interaction graph for Item CI4SLM2
As an example, item CI4SML2 showed significant item-by-country interaction in about half of the countries 
(Figure 11.3), indicating that there was considerable lack of measurement invariance for this item, which 
was not retained for scaling. We produced similar graphs for the test-item adjudication process at the 
international and national levels; information about occurrence of measurement invariance across countries 
was used to identify items for post-verification checks after completion of the main data collection. 
Although most of the ICCS 2022 test items showed only limited item-by-country interactions, it needs to 
be acknowledged that there were some national item difficulties that deviated quite considerably from 
the international item difficulty. In previous cycles, we omitted these items from scaling for those national 
samples where larger deviations had been observed (1.3 logits or more).6 However, we conducted a review 
6	 This threshold was introduced in ICCS 2009 as being equivalent to about two standard deviations in the overall distribution of 
item parameters.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST ITEMS
159
of the extent to which such deletions have an impact on scaling outcomes that showed only negligible 
differences. Therefore, for ICCS 2022, it was decided not to exclude any items based only on item DIF. 
Indications of deviations in item difficulty were extensively used to review possible issues with translation.
We also examined item-by-country interaction by item type, in particular by reviewing this for open-ended 
items. With these items, item-by-country interaction can be evidence of differences in the relative harshness 
of markers across countries. In ICCS 2022, we did not detect any systematic differences in relative item 
difficulties for scored items and kept all scored items for scaling at the national level. 
Item adjudication
We conducted the adjudication of test items in two phases: first at the international level for the pooled ICCS 
international sample including all data from countries that had met IEA sample participation guidelines, and 
then separately for each national subsample.  
At the international level, we assessed item characteristics for the calibration sample. Here, we reviewed 
item-fit statistics, item-score correlations, item characteristic curves, general measurement equivalence 
across countries (item-by-country interaction), and gender DIF. For open-ended items, we also considered 
scorer reliability and the correct ordering of average ability estimates per category. Only one of the test 
items (CI4SLM2) had unsatisfactory scaling properties at the international level and was removed from the 
international scaling of civic knowledge.
At the national level, we reviewed test items by comparing national item-fit statistics with international 
item-fit statistics. We also flagged test items for individual countries that showed large item-by-country 
interactions (see section on cross-national measurement equivalence).
National centers were provided with item statistics (see example in Figure 11.4) and were asked to review 
flagged (“dodgy”) test items. For convenience, a summary of all the flagged test items for the country was 
also provided (Table 11.6), as well as an international summary of flagged items (not shown). Item-level 
information included cases of unusual item-total correlation (e.g., negative correlations between correct 
response and overall score) and those showing large differences between national and international item 
difficulties. It also included highlighting open-ended items where the category-total correlations were 
disordered. In some cases, national centers informed the international study center of translation problems 
that had not been detected during verification. In these cases, we categorized the items as “not administered” 
in the international database and excluded them when scaling the corresponding national data. 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
160
Figure 11.4: Example of item statistics provided to national centers

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST ITEMS
161
Table 11.6: Summary of problematic items provided to national centers
Working independently from those conducting the national item reviews, the international study center 
flagged national items that showed irregular scaling properties (item misfit or large item-by-country 
interactions) and conducted further verifications of item translation. In a number of cases, we identified 
additional national items that needed to be set to “not administered” in the international database and 
excluded from scaling of the corresponding national data based on identified translation issues. In cases 
where items were translated differently from ICCS 2016, we also excluded those national items from the 
trend estimations but retained them in the international database. Table 11.7 lists those test items that 
were identified with translation issues and their corresponding treatment.

11.4	
Final scaling and equating procedures
International item calibration and test reliability
Item parameter estimates were obtained from a joint data file that included response data from both 
assessment modes used in ICCS 2022. National samples from countries meeting IEA sample participation 
requirements were equally weighted for the calibration and all items were included (except for items that 
were deleted nationally or internationally following the adjudication process). To balance the impact of 
modes in this calibration, we also used bridging study data from 11 countries. Countries with both paper-

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
162
based and computer-based data received modified weights so that their contribution was the same as for 
countries that only had one national sample (from either paper- or computer-based assessments) in the 
calibration. The mode effect was estimated based on comparisons with common item parameters across 
PBA and CBA. See von Davier et al. (2019) for more information on mode effect models.
We omitted missing student responses that were likely to be due to problems with test length (“not reached 
items”) from the calibration of item parameters but treated them as “incorrect” when scaling the student 
responses. Using this calibration, we identified a set of item parameters that we used to scale the ICCS 2022 
test data (Table 11.8). The overall reliability of the international test, as obtained from the scaling model, was 
0.85 (ACER ConQuest estimate).

International ability estimates
In many educational assessments, the purpose of testing is to obtain accurate estimates of individual 
domain-based cognitive abilities. The accuracy of measuring the latent ability θ can be improved by using 
a larger number of test items. However, in large-scale surveys such as ICCS 2022, the purpose is to obtain 
accurate population estimates through use of instruments that also cover a wider range of possible aspects 
of cognitive abilities. 
The use of matrix-sampling design, where individual students are allocated booklets and respond to a set 
of items obtained from the main pool of items, has become standard in assessments of this type. However, 
reducing test length and administering subsets of items to individual students introduces a considerable 
degree of uncertainty at the individual level. Aggregated student abilities of this type can lead to bias in 

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST ITEMS
163
population estimates. This problem can be addressed by employing plausible value methodology that uses 
all available information from student tests and questionnaires, a process that leads to more accurate 
population estimates (Mislevy, 1991; Mislevy & Sheehan, 1987; von Davier et al., 2009).
Using item parameters anchored at their estimated values from the international calibration, we generated 
plausible values as random draws θng
that depend on response data xn (the conditional item response model), other available context data zn 
(conditioning variables) and group membership g, which in the case of ICCS 2022 is determined by the 
education system in which an individual was assessed. Here, Eg (θ|xn; zn) represents the posterior distribution, 
while Vg (θ|xn; zn) denotes the posterior variance, which provides a measure of uncertainty (see more detailed 
descriptions in Adams et al., 1997; Adams, 2002; von Davier et al., 2020). In order to obtain plausible values 
as estimates of students’ civic knowledge, we used the ACER ConQuest software (see Adams et al., 2020). 
Group membership g was accounted for by drawing plausible values separately for each national sample.
We used all available international student questionnaire variables and also those derived from regional 
instruments for conditioning. To deal with missing responses, we substituted all missing responses in a 
variable with either the mode or the mean and added, as extra variables, additional indicators for missing 
values. In Appendix D, we list all the international and regional student-level variables (along with their 
respective coding) that we used to condition the plausible values of civic knowledge (Table D.1). The 
numbers of conditioning variables varied across countries depending on the availability of information from 
the international student questionnaire (core and optional variables) or additional regional data (from the 
European or Latin American questionnaire).
Because of the large number of variables, we used the principal components of all student-level variables 
(single items or scale indices) as conditioning variables that reflected 99 percent of the variance. At the 
student level, we used only gender and its missing indicator as a direct conditioning variable. At the school 
level, we included stratum indicators and averages of classroom ability estimates (WLEs), adjusted for the 
contribution of a student’s own ability estimate. 
Review of delivery mode effects
As ICCS 2022 used both PBA and CBA, it has been necessary to estimate the effect of the delivery mode 
on student performance. This was done to support decisions about the best way to report ICCS 2022 data 
regardless of delivery mode within countries, after setting it on the ICCS historical scale established in ICCS 
2009, that was (like ICCS 2016) completed using PBA.
Eleven countries that participated in previous ICCS cycles on paper participated in ICCS 2022 using 
CBA. In 2022, CBA countries that had also participated in ICCS 2016 also collected data for an additional 
representative sample of students on paper as part of a bridging study. 
The bridging study instrument included eight of the 11 clusters common to CBA and PBA delivery across 
countries presented using a partially balanced incomplete block design (pBIB) distributed among eight 
paper-based bridging test forms. The smaller number of PBA bridging study clusters and test forms was 
used to minimize the necessary size of the bridging sample of students within countries while, maintaining 
a sufficient breadth of item coverage within the PBA-bridging instrument content to adequately reflect the 
ICCS civic knowledge construct.
The two item sets (CBA/core and PBA/bridging study) were calibrated separately, once with CBA and once 
with PBA-bridging study data. Figure 11.5 shows the correspondence of item parameters estimated based 
on CBA and PBA-bridging study data, where both sets of parameters have the same average difficulty of 
0. The scatterplot suggests that there are only minor differences in terms of item parameters estimated 
with each respective sub-sample. The two lines indicate the degree to which estimated item parameters are 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
164
within the expected range due to sample variation (adjusted for an assumed sampling design effect of 47), 
and all items appear within or (in a few cases) only slightly outside this range.
The data shown in Figure 11.5 suggested that the item difficulty parameters from the computer-based main 
survey and paper-based bridging study were well aligned and provided a sound basis for supporting the 
equating procedures required to report all ICCS 2022 data on the ‘historical’ ICCS civic knowledge scale 
established in ICCS 2009. The curved lines were computed to provide rough confidence intervals based on 
the original standard errors adjusted for approximate sampling design effects.
Figure 11.5 Scatterplot of item parameters for CBA and PBA bridging study items
We further looked at differences in outcomes across the two delivery modes. Initial analyses showed that 
there were more considerable differences between the (relatively small) sub-samples in Latvia that were 
caused by some ‘outlier’ schools in the bridging study sample. In liaison with the Latvian national team, we 
identified those schools and applied a re-weighting procedure to neutralize these effects, whereby seven 
out of 51 schools in the bridging sample received zero weights with weight adjustments for other schools 
where appropriate. The Latvian data from the bridging study and main survey were obtained from separate 
school samples.
Using a dataset including the Latvian data with modified sample weights, we then computed the national 
averages of percentages with correct responses within each sample for the 11 countries that had 
implemented the bridging study and compared them across the two delivery modes (Table 11.9). The results 
showed that, on average, there were slightly higher percentages in samples from paper-based assessment, 
the difference was on average about one percentage point. Further, we also observed some variation across 
countries, ranging from zero differences to 2-3 point-differences in Italy, Latvia, and Norway, a phenomenon 
that has also been observed in other international studies of mode effects (see, for example, von Davier 
et al., 2020). In view of the slight negative overall effect of computer mode in comparison with a paper-
based assessment, it was decided to apply a mode effect adjustment as part of the final scaling and equating 
procedures.

Equating test scores
For ICCS 2009 (Schulz & Fraillon, 2011), we transformed the civic knowledge scale to a metric with a 
mean of 500 and a standard deviation of 100 for equally weighted ICCS countries that had met sampling 
requirements (Categories 1 and 2). This linear transformation was computed by applying the formula
where θn
' were the student civic knowledge estimates (plausible values) in the international reporting 
metric, θn
09were the original estimates (in logits), θ and σθ were the corresponding ICCS 2009 mean (-0.01) 
and standard deviation (0.95) computed with weights assigning equal weight to each national sample. This 
transformation was applied to each of the five plausible values.
In 2016, the international project team conducted a concurrent calibration of ICCS 2009 and 2016 data 
on the ICCS 2016 scale using all items from both cycles. All ICCS 2009 item parameters were re-estimated 
concurrently during the ICCS 2016 calibration process and used to re-scale the ICCS 2009 data to obtain a 
linear transformation that was applied to set the ICCS 2016 estimates to the reporting metric established 
in the first cycle (Gebhardt & Schulz, 2018). Given the dual delivery mode including both paper- and 
computer-based assessment across different countries in a design where not all trend items were included 
in the bridging study, it was not possible to directly apply the same linking method as in the previous cycle. 
Before implementing our linking procedures, we reviewed the relative difficulties of the common items to 
evaluate the quality of the link. A scatter plot of the relative difficulties of the 55 common items (Figure 
11.6) revealed that most items were within or relatively close to the confidence interval that was computed 
around the assumption of measurement invariance across cycles. None of the differences between relative 
items difficulties were more than half a logit and there were no obvious outliers. Therefore, we decided 
to keep all the common items as link items after removing all national items for which we had detected 
discrepancies in translation between 2016 and 2022.

that the trend estimation was based on the same delivery mode and the parameters for the items used in 
ICCS 2022 are displayed in Table 11.10.

The scaling, equating, and mode effect procedures were implemented in three steps, each of which consisted 
of obtaining three different linear transformations (T1, T2, and T3) that were applied to civic knowledge 
estimates (plausible values, θn to transform them to estimates in a different metric (θn
') using different 
intercept (A1, A2, and A3) and slope parameters (B1, B2, and B3) applying the formula:
The procedures consisted of the following steps:
I.	
A re-scaling of ICCS 2016 data (using data from countries also participating in ICCS 2022) through the 
generation of PVs with full conditioning based on the joint calibration of paper-based test data from 
ICCS 2022 and ICCS 2016. Based on this new set of PVs and the original PVs used for reporting ICCS 
2016 results we computed the linear transformation T1 that transformed the paper-based ICCS 2022 
test data to be on the historical civic knowledge scales (A1 = 434.521, B1 = 98.138).
II.	
Paper-based ICCS 2022 data (from core and bridging study samples) were used to draw to sets of 
plausible values with full conditioning, the first set of estimates (PVPBA2016/22) was scaled based on the 
joint calibration based on ICCS 2022 and 2016 data, the second set (PVPBA2022) was scaled based on the 
ICCS 2022 calibration (using a combined database of all computer-based and paper-based ICCS 2022 
data including bridging study data). These sets of PVs from both calibrations were used to compute 
a second linear transformation that aligned the ICCS 2022/2016 paper-based calibration with the 
calibration based on all ICCS 2022 test data (A2 = 0.129, B2 = 1.004). 
III.	 Computer-based ICCS 2022 test data were scaled using the ICCS 2022 calibration through the 
computation of plausible values (PVCBA2022) with full conditioning. For the 11 countries with plausible 
values from computer-based (PVCBA2022) and paper-based assessments (PVPBA2022), we then computed 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
168
the third linear transformation (T3), which was applied for computer-based civic knowledge data to 
adjust for the observed overall mode effect (A3 = 0.033, B3 = 1.021).

P22)  from paper-based assessment based on the combined ICCS 2022 
calibration (PVPBA2022) were set on the historical civic knowledge applying the following transformation:

Uncertainty in the link
Because the transformation equating the ICCS 2022 data with the ICCS 2016 data depended on the change 
in the degree of difficulty of each of the individual link items, the sample of link items chosen influenced the 
choice of transformation. This meant that the resulting transformation would have been slightly different if 
we had chosen an alternative set of link items. Uncertainty in the transformation thus relates in part to the 
sampling of the link items, in the same way that uncertainty in values such as country averages is an outcome 
of the particular sample of students that is used. It should be noted that there are also other factors that may 
cause uncertainty in the link (such as model error), and that the chosen approach described in the following 
paragraph constitutes only an approximation.
The uncertainty resulting from link-item sampling is often referred to as linking error, and it is an error that 
analysts should consider when comparing the results arising out of different data collections (see Monseur 
& Berezner, 2007). As is the situation with the error that is introduced through the process of sampling 
students, the exact magnitude of this linking error cannot be determined. We can, however, estimate the 
likely range of magnitudes for this error and take it into account when interpreting results. As with sampling 
errors, the likely range of magnitude for the errors is represented as a component of the uncertainty.
The following approach has been used to estimate the equating error. Suppose we have a total of L score 
points in the link items in K modules. Use i to index items in a unit and j to index units so that δij
y is the 
estimated difficulty of item i in unit j for year y, and let:
The size (number of score points) of unit j is mj so that:
and
Further let:
and then the link error, considering the clustering was computed as follows:
and
It is important to note that this way of estimating the linking error is only appropriate for the Rasch model, 
which was used in this study. The procedure does not consider changes in item functions as reflected in 
parameter changes in more general models. The linking error was computed after setting item parameters 
from 2022 and 2016 on the same scale (by shifting link items so that the average of item parameters were 
0 for both item sets).

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR CIVIC KNOWLEDGE TEST ITEMS
169
The development of proficiency levels for civic knowledge
One of the objectives of ICCS was to establish a described civic knowledge scale that would become a 
reference point for ongoing international assessments in this learning area. Establishing proficiency levels 
of civic knowledge is an informative way of describing student performance across countries and also sets 
benchmarks for comparisons of survey results across study cycles. In the second ICCS cycle, we applied 
some changes to the way ICCS proficiency were described (see Schulz et al., 2018).
Students whose results are located within a particular level of proficiency are typically able to demonstrate 
certain understandings and skills that are associated with that level. These students also typically possess 
the understandings and skills defined as applying at lower proficiency levels. 
When developing proficiency levels, we applied a method that ensured that the notion of “being at a level” 
could be interpreted consistently and which recognized that the ability scale is a continuum. We therefore 
attempted to provide a common understanding about what being at a level meant and to ensure that this 
meaning was consistent across different proficiency levels. This method took the following three questions 
into account:
•	
What is the expected success of a student at a particular level on a test containing items at that level?
•	
What is the width of the levels in that scale?
•	
What is the probability that a student in the middle of a level will correctly answer an item of average 
difficulty for that level?
In ICCS 2009, we adopted the following two parameters for defining proficiency level: 
•	
The response probability (rp) for reporting item parameters: this was set at rp = 0.62; 
•	
The width of the proficiency levels: this was set at 0.8 logits.8
Using these parameters, we were able to infer the following about students’ aptitude in relation to the 
proficiency levels: 
•	
Students whose results placed them at the lowest possible point of the proficiency level were likely to 
correctly answer (on average) slightly over 50 percent of the items on a test made up of items spread 
uniformly across the level, from the easiest to the most difficult item. 
•	
Students whose results placed them at the lowest possible point of the proficiency level had a 62 
percent probability of giving the correct response to an item at the bottom end of the proficiency level. 
•	
Students whose results placed them at the top of the proficiency level had a 78 percent probability of 
correctly responding to an item at the bottom end of the proficiency level.
The approach that we chose was essentially an attempt to apply an appropriate choice of mastery by placing 
item locations at rp = 0.62 while simultaneously ensuring that the approach would be understood by the 
readers of ICCS reports.9
In ICCS 2016, we identified four proficiency levels that could be used when reporting student performances 
from the assessment, extending the original three levels that had been applied in ICCS 2009. In ICCS 2022, 
we used the same proficiency levels as in ICCS 2016, and Figure 11.7 shows the cut-points for these levels 
and the percentage of students at each proficiency level across the participating ICCS 2022 countries.
8	 If the variance of ability estimates (in logits) is 1, five levels of width 0.8 logits will cover the range -2 to 2 logits, or roughly 95 
percent of student abilities. With a variance of 1.3 as observed in ICCS 2009, six levels would cover approximately the same 
range.
 9	 A description of this is given is given in OECD (2002), PISA 2000 Technical Report, OECD.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICCS 2022 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS

Chapter 12:
Scaling procedures for ICCS 2022 questionnaire 
items
Wolfram Schulz and Tim Friedman
12.1 Introduction
This chapter describes the scaling procedures used for scaling questionnaire scales that were reported 
in the International Civic and Citizenship Education Study (ICCS) 2022 publications (Schulz et al., 2023; 
Damiani et al., 2024). Generally, it is possible to distinguish two general types of indices based on data from 
the ICCS 2016 questionnaires:
•	
Simple indices were constructed through arithmetical transformation or recoding—for example an 
index of immigration background based on information about the country of birth of students and their 
parents;
•	
Scale indices that were derived through the scaling of items—this was typically achieved by using the 
Rasch model to derive scale scores from items with two or more categories.
The first part of this chapter describes the simple indices that were derived from ICCS 2022 data and how 
they were created. The second part outlines the procedures used for the scaling of questionnaire data in 
ICCS 2022. The third part includes a description of scaled indices based on student, teacher, and school 
level questionnaire data, providing statistical information on the factor structure of related item sets, scale 
reliabilities, and parameters used for IRT (Item Response Theory) scaling. The chapter concludes with a 
section about the comparability of questionnaire data from computer-based and paper-based delivery 
modes.
Results from an analysis of cross-country validity of item dimensionality and constructs were already part 
of ICCS 2022 field trial analyses, as part of which the International Study Center at the Australian Council 
for Educational Research (ACER) conducted a review of the extent to which measurement models were 
invariant across participating countries for draft item material. The review of field trial data made use of 
exploratory and confirmatory factor analysis, and item response modeling to examine cross-national 
measurement equivalence before the final selection of main survey questionnaire items (see an overview of 
the different types of analyses in Schulz, 2009). 
12.2 Simple Indices
Student questionnaire
Student age (S_AGE) was calculated as the difference between the year and month of the testing and the 
year and month of a student’s birth. Information from the student questionnaire (Question 1) was used to 
derive age, except for students where this information was missing. In these cases, information from student 
tracking forms (see Chapter 9 for more details) provided data for the calculation of this index. The formula 
for computing S_AGE was:
where Ty and Sy are, respectively, the year of the test and the year of birth of the tested student, in four-digit 
format (e.g., “2022” or “2007”), and where Tm and Sm are respectively the month of the test and the month of 
the student’s birth. The result was rounded to two decimal places.
In Question 2, students were asked their sex, choosing between ‘Girl,’ ‘Boy’ and in some countries, a third 
option, ‘Other’ (countries had a choice whether to include this third option or not, and how it can be named). 
These were captured in the indicator variable student gender (SD_GENDER) with a value of 1 assigned to 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
174
females and value of 0 assigned to males. In cases where students had omitted data for this question, we 
used the information from the tracking form instead. For this indicator variable, student data corresponding 
to the optional third category were treated as missing information. However, the variable with the original 
is also included in the international database. 
Question 3 asked students to indicate their expected highest level of educational attainment. The 
corresponding index of students’ expected educational attainment (S_ISCED) had the following categories: 
(0)	 Completion of ISCED level 2 (lower-secondary) or lower; 
(1)	 Completion of ISCED level 3 (upper-secondary); 
(2)	 Completion of ISCED level 4 (non-tertiary post-secondary) or ISCED level 5 (short-cycle tertiary 
education); 
(3)	 Completion of ISCED level 6 (bachelor’s degree or equivalent), ISCED level 7 (master’s degree or 
equivalent) or ISCED level 8 (doctoral degree or equivalent). 
In Question 4, students were required to indicate the country of birth for themselves and for each of their 
parents. The index of immigrant family background (S_IMMIG) was created using these data, and had three 
categories:
(1)	 Native students (students who had at least one parent born in the country of assessment);
(2)	 First-generation students (students who were born in the country of assessment and whose parent(s) 
were born in another country); 
(3)	 Non-native students (students who were born outside the country of assessment and whose parent(s) 
were born in another country). 
We assigned missing values to students with missing responses for either their own place of birth, or that of 
their mother and father, or for all three questions. The analysis of immigrant background and civic knowledge 
was based on a dichotomous indicator variable that distinguished between students from immigrant families 
(Categories 2 and 3) and students from non-immigrant families (Category 1).
Question 5 of the ICCS 2022 student questionnaire asked students what language they speak at home most 
of the time, with each country providing a list of options relevant to their context. We used this information 
to derive an index on test language used at home (S_TLANG), in which responses were grouped into two 
categories:
(0)	 The language spoken at home most of the time differed from the language of assessment;
(1)	 The language spoken at home most of the time was the language of assessment.
Students were asked to provide details about the jobs of their two parents or guardians1 using open-
ended questions (Questions 6 and 8). The responses to these questions were coded by national centers 
into four-digit codes using the International Standard Classification of Occupations (ISCO-08) framework 
(International Labour Organization, 2007). These codes are contained in the indices S_P1ISCO (student’s 
parent/guardian 1) and S_P2ISCO (student’s parent/guardian 2). We further mapped these codes to the 
international socioeconomic index of occupational status (ISEI) (Ganzeboom et al., 1992). The three indices 
that we obtained from these scores were occupational status of parent/guardian 1 (S_P1ISEI occupational 
status of parent/guardian 2 (S_P2ISEI), and the highest occupational status of both parents or guardians (S_
HISEI), with the last corresponding to the higher ISEI score of either parent/guardian or to the only available 
parent’s ISEI score. For all three indices, higher scores indicate higher levels of occupational status.
Questions 7 and 9 asked about the education attainment of students’ mothers and fathers. The core 
difficulties with this variable relate to international comparability (education systems differ widely across 
countries and over time within countries) and response validity (students are often unable to accurately 
report their parents’ levels of education). In ICCS 2022 we classified levels of parental education according 
to the International Standard Classification of Education (ISCED) (UNESCO, 2006). 
1	 The two parents were named <parent/guardian 1> and <parent/guardian 2> in the English source version, and national centers 
adapted them as appropriate for their national contexts.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICCS 2022 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
175
Recoding educational qualifications into the following categories provided indices of highest parental 
educational attainment: 
(0)	 Did not complete ISCED level 2; 
(1)	 ISCED level 2 (lower-secondary);
(2)	 ISCED level 3 (upper-secondary); 
(3)	 Completion of ISCED level 4 (non-tertiary post-secondary) or ISCED level 5 (short-cycle tertiary 
education); 
(4)	 Completion of ISCED level 6 (bachelor’s degree or equivalent), ISCED level 7 (master’s degree or 
equivalent) or ISCED level 8 (doctoral degree or equivalent). 
For each student, indices with these categories are available for the highest educational attainment of parent/
guardian 1 (S_P1ISCED) and highest educational attainment of parent/guardian 2 (S_P2ISCED). The index for 
highest educational level of parental education (S_HISCED) corresponds to the higher educational attainment 
of either parent or guardian.
Question 10 of the student questionnaire asked students how many books they had in their homes. 
Responses to this question formed the basis for an index of students’ home literacy resources (S_HOMLIT) 
with the following categories: 
(0)	 None or very few (0–10 books);
(1)	 Enough to fill one shelf (11–25 books);
(2)	 Enough to fill one bookcase (26–100 books);
(3)	 Enough to fill two bookcases (101–200 books);
(4)	 Enough to fill three or more bookcases (more than 200 books).
Question 11 requested students to indicate how interested they were and how interested their parent(s) or 
guardian(s) were in political and social issues. The (recoded) indices for student interest (S_SINT), interest of 
parent/guardian 1 (S_P1INT) and interest of parent/guardian 2 (S_P2INT) consisted of the following categories: 
(0) “not interested at all,” (1) “not very interested,” (2) “quite interested,” and (3) “very interested.” An index 
of the highest level of parents' interest in political and social issues (S_HINT) was created by computing the 
maximum value of both S_P1INT and S_P2INT.
The religion of each student was captured in Question 33. This question was not mandatory internationally, 
and some countries opted not to administer it. Those countries that did participate provided a list of options 
relevant to their context. We used this information to derive an index on student’s religious affiliation (S_
RELIG) in which responses were grouped into two categories:
(0)	 The student indicated that they do not have a religion;
(1)	 The student indicated that they do have a religion.
Countries were also given the option to administer a question on the frequency that students attend 
religious services (Question 34). A simple index was formed capturing students’ attendance of religious services 
(S_RELSER) based on the following categories:
(0)	 Never;
(1)	 Less than once a year;
(2)	 At least once a year;
(3)	 At least once a month;
(4)	 At least once a week.
Teacher questionnaire
Question 2 of the teacher questionnaire asked teachers to indicate what percentage of their classroom 
teaching time was at the target grade during current school year. Responses to this question were used to 
form the index teacher's teaching load at school (T_TIME). This index was coded that a value of 0.1 reflects 
“less than 20%”, 0.3 “20–39%”, 0.5 “40–59%”, 0.7 “60–79%”, and 0.9 “80% and more.”

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
176
The individual teacher age (T_AGE) consisted of the midpoint of the age ranges given in Question 4 of the 
teacher questionnaire. We assigned “less than 25” a value of 23 and coded “60 or over” as 63.
The sex of teacher (TD_GENDER) was computed from the data captured from Question 5 of the teacher 
questionnaire. Female teachers were coded as 1, whereas male teachers were coded as 0. Teachers who 
selected the third option were set to missing for this indicator variable while the original data are also 
available in the database.
School questionnaire
Question 20 collected information about whether the school was a public school or a private school. This 
information was used to form an indicator of private school management (C_PRIVATE), where public schools 
were coded as 0 and private schools were coded as 1.
Question 21 asked principals to provide the numbers of female and male students enrolled at their school. 
To calculate an indicator of school size (C_SCSIZE), we used a simple addition of the total number of boys 
enrolled on a suitable date at the beginning of the school year (IC4G22A) and the total number of girls 
enrolled on the same date (IC4G22B).
Question 22 asked principals to provide the numbers of female and male students at the target grade 
enrolled at their school. We used the same procedure to calculate school enrollment in the target grade 
(C_GENROL) by adding the number of boys (IC4G22A) and the number of girls (IC4G22B) at each target 
grade. To calculate the percentage of target-grade students at each school (C_TGPERC), we divided the number 
of students enrolled in the target grade (C_GENROL) by the total number of enrolled students at the school (C_
SCSIZE). We then multiplied this value by 100.
Question 23 collected information about the size of the immediate community where the school was 
located. This information was used to form an indicator of school urbanity (C_URBAN), where schools located 
in communities with more than 100,000 inhabitants were coded as 1 and other schools were coded as 0.
12.3	
 Scaling procedures
Review of item statistics
Before scaling questionnaire items, we reviewed reliabilities both overall and for national samples using 
Cronbach’s alpha coefficient as an estimate of the internal consistency of each scale (Cronbach, 1951), for 
which values above 0.7 are typically regarded as satisfactory and values above 0.8 indicate high reliability 
(see for example, Nunnally & Bernstein, 1994, pp. 264–265). We investigated the spread of reliabilities 
across countries. Apart from scale reliabilities, we also looked at the percentages of missing responses 
(which tended to be very low in most cases) as well as the correlations between individual items and the 
scale scores based on all other items in a scale (adjusted item-total correlations).
Confirmatory factor analysis
Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) (Brown, 2015; Jöreskog, 1969) allows the confirmation of theoretically 
expected dimensions and, at the field-trial stage, the re-specification of the expected dimensional structure.2 
When using confirmatory factor analysis, researchers acknowledge the need to employ a theoretical model 
of item dimensionality that can be tested via the collected data. Within the more general Structural Equation 
Model (SEM) (see, for example, Kaplan, 2009) framework, latent variables are linked to observable variables 
via measurement equations. 
In view of the categorical nature of the questionnaire items used for scaling, the ICCS analysis team relied 
on robust weighted least squares estimation (WLSMV) (see Muthén et al., 1997; Flora & Curran, 2004) to 
estimate the confirmatory factor models. The software package used for estimation was MPLUS 7 (Muthén 
& Muthén, 2012). 
2	 In the initial stages of field trial analyses, we also employed exploratory factor analysis (Tucker & MacCallum, 1997; Fabrigar et 
al., 1999) to determine item dimensionality of larger item pools that had been newly developed for this cycle.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICCS 2022 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
177
A random variable yj reflects the observed categorical responses to item j, that take the form of K+1 values 
0, 1, …, K. The threshold model (Muthén, 1984) assumes underlying continues responses y* for all items, for 
which the observed categorical response variable yj is discretized through thresholds τjk (j=1, 2, …, p; k=0, 
1, …, K + 1). Assuming τj0=-∞ and τj(k+1)=∞, and yj=k if τjk<y*j<τj(k+1), the latent responses for all items y* are 
modeled as: 
where ξ denotes a vector of m common factors (latent variables), δ a p×1 vector of unique error variables, 
υ a p×1 vector of intercepts, and Λ a p×m matrix of factor loadings. The covariance matrix is fitted according 
to the theoretical factor structure.
When conducting the confirmatory factor analyses for ICCS 2022 questionnaire data, selected model-fit 
indices provided measures of the extent to which a particular model with an assumed a-priori structure 
“fitted the data.” For the ICCS 2022 analysis, the assessment of model fit was primarily conducted through 
reviews of the root-mean square error of approximation (RMSEA), the comparative fit index (CFI), and the 
non-normed fit index (NNFI), all of which are less affected than other indices by sample size and model 
complexity (see Bollen & Long, 1993). 
For the purpose of assessing model fit, RMSEA values over 0.10 were rated as poor, those between 0.08 
and 0.1 as indicating marginally satisfactory model fit while values of 0.05 and lower indicate a close model 
fit (see MacCallum et al., 1996). As additional fit indices, CFI and NNFI are bound between 0 and 1. Values 
below 0.90 indicate a non-satisfactory model fit whereas values greater than 0.95 were interpreted as 
suggesting a close model fit, with values in between regarded as marginally satisfactory (see Bentler & 
Bonett, 1980; Hu & Bentler, 1999). 
In addition to these fit indices, standardized factor loadings and the corresponding residual item variances 
provided further evidence of model fit for questionnaire data. Standardized factor loadings λj can be 
interpreted in the same way as standardized correlation coefficients between the indicator variable and the 
latent factor. The loadings also reflect the extent to which each indicator measures the underlying construct. 
Squared standardized factor loadings indicate how much variance in an indicator variable can be explained 
by the latent factor and are related to the (standardized) residual variance estimate δj (these provide an 
estimate of the unexplained proportion of variance) as:
The use of multidimensional models also allows an assessment of the estimated correlation(s) between 
latent factors which provide(s) information on the similarity of the different dimensions measured by 
related item sets. Both unidimensional and multidimensional models were applied when confirming 
expected questionnaire item dimensionality, the latter was more prominent for the analysis of items that 
measured related latent variables or that were included in the same questionnaire item batteries. This 
approach allowed an assessment of the measurement model and the associations between related latent 
factors. The scaling analyses were restricted to data from those countries that met sample participation 
requirements (see Chapter 7 for further information). National samples of students, teachers, and schools 
received weights that ensured equal representations of countries in the analyses. 
In international studies, model parameters may vary across countries, and it may not be appropriate 
to assume the same factor structure for each population. To test parameter invariance, multiple-group 
modeling, as an extension of CFA, offers an approach to test the equivalence of measurement models across 
sub-samples (Little, 1997; Byrne, 2008). 
If one considers a model where respondents belong to different groups indexed as g=1, 2, ..., G, the multiple-
group factor model becomes:
For example, a test of factorial invariance (HΛ) where factor loadings are defined as being equal, often 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
178
referred to as “metric equivalence” (Horn & McArdle, 1992), can be defined as:
It is possible to compare model-fit indices across different multiple-group models, each with an increasing 
degree of constraints, including relaxed models with no constraints through more constrained models to 
models with largely invariant model parameters. While such comparisons can include many different ways 
of constraining parameters across different groups of cases or variables, in this report, for all international 
and regional student questionnaire scales, three different multiple-group models for CFA were estimated 
with the following different levels of constraints on parameters:
A.	
Unconstrained models where all parameters are estimated as country-specific (configural invariance);
B.	
Models with constrained factor loadings across countries (metric invariance);
C.	
Models with constraints on factor loadings and intercepts (scalar invariance).
The last model is the one that indicates the fullest level of measurement comparability across participating 
countries. Hypothesis testing using tests of statistical significance is rather problematic (especially with 
data from larger samples), therefore, when reviewing results, it was deemed more appropriate to focus on 
relative changes in model fit across the three models with different levels of constraints. The multiple-group 
analyses presented in this report provide a rough indication of the extent to which measurement models 
tended to be invariant across countries. While there are no clear scientific criteria about the point at which 
lack of measurement invariance causes scale scores to become incomparable, for those scales for which a 
lack of measurement invariance was noted, comparisons across national contexts should be interpreted 
with due caution.
Item response modeling
In line with the scaling of test item data (see Chapter 11), item response modeling was used to derive scales 
from ICCS 2022 questionnaire items. The one-parameter (Rasch) model (Rasch, 1960) for dichotomous 
items models the probability of selecting an item category 1 instead of 0 as:
Here, Pi(θn) is the probability of person n scoring 1 on item i, θn is the estimated latent trait of person n, 
and δiis the estimated location of item i on this dimension. For each item, item responses are modeled as a 
function of the latent trait θn. 
In the case of items with more than two categories (as, for example, with Likert-type items), this model can 
be generalized to the (Rasch) partial credit model (Masters & Wright, 1997; Masters, 1982), which takes 
the form of:
where we define exp∑0
k=0 (θn-δi+τik)=1, Pxi(θn) denotes the probability of person n scoring x on item i, θn the 
person’s latent trait, the item parameter δi the location of the item on the latent continuum, and τik an 
additional step parameter for each step k between adjacent categories.
We used weighted mean-square statistics based on model residuals, as one indicator of assessing general fit of 
items to the scaling model (Wright & Masters, 1982). The residual-based statistics were used in conjunction 
with a wide range of further item statistics provided the basis for an assessment of IRT model fit. ICCS 2022 
used the ACER Conquest software package (Adams et al., 2020) for the analysis of item scaling properties 
and the estimation of item parameters. 
The international item parameters were derived using equally weighted national datasets: 
A.	
Calibration of item parameters for the student questionnaire: This was done based on a pooled database 

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICCS 2022 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
179
where each of the national samples from 20 countries that met sample participation requirements for 
the student survey received the same weight. 
B.	
Calibration of item parameters for the teacher questionnaire: This was done based on a pooled database 
where each of the national samples from 13 countries that met sample participation requirements for 
the teacher survey received the same weight.
C.	
Calibration of item parameters for school questionnaire: This was done based on a pooled database 
where each of the national samples from 20 countries that met sample participation requirements for 
the student survey received the same weight.
D.	 Calibration of item parameters for the European questionnaire: This was done based on a pooled 
database where each of the national samples from 17 European countries that met sample participation 
requirements for the student survey received the same weight. 
Following the estimation of international item parameters from the calibration sample, we computed 
weighted likelihood estimates of person parameters to obtain individual student scores. So-called weighted 
likelihood estimates (Warm, 1989) are computed by minimizing the equation:
for each case n, where rx is the sum score obtained from a set of j items with mi+1 categories and k steps. This 
was achieved by applying the Newton-Raphson method with fixed item parameters from the international 
item calibrations. The term Jn/2In (with In being the information function for student n and Jn being its 
derivative with respect to θ) is used as a weight function to account for the bias inherent in maximum 
likelihood estimation (Warm, 1989). ACER ConQuest software was used to calibrate the item parameters 
before deriving scale scores.
For scales that were new to ICCS 2022, the transformation of weighted likelihood estimates to an 
international metric resulted in reporting scales with an ICCS 2022 average of 50 and a standard deviation 
of 10 for equally weighted datasets from the countries that met sample participation requirements. This 
was achieved by applying the following formula:
where θ'n denotes the scores in the international metric, θn the weighted likelihood estimates in their original 
logit metric as derived from the estimation, and μθ(ICCS22) the international mean of these logit scores using 
weights that ensured equal contributions for each of the national samples. Derived using the same weight, 
σθ(ICCS22) represents the corresponding international standard deviation of the original likelihood estimates in 
their logit metric. The means and standard deviations (Table 12.1) were used to transform the original scale 
scores for the international and regional student, teacher, and school questionnaires into the international 
metric.

Linking questionnaire scale indices to previous cycles
The ICCS 2022 international, European, and Latin American student questionnaires included a number 
of questions with unchanged question and item wording, and countries participating in both cycles were 
requested to use identical translations from the source version to those in the previous cycle. For scales 
based on this type of item sets, it was possible to link the scale scores based on ICCS 2022 data to those 
from ICCS 2016 and/or 2009. Scale linking was applied to 17 scales from the international questionnaire 
and three scales from the regional European questionnaire.3 For some of the scales individual items had 
been discarded or had been augmented by new items.4 In these cases, the linking procedure was based only 
on those items which had remained unchanged. 
Prior to conducting an equating of scale scores across cycles, item parameter estimates from calibrations 
from each cycle were reviewed with regard to differences. This review showed that differences were 
generally negligible across the two cycles. In one case, an item set measuring students’ attitudes toward their 
country of residence, reviews of item parameters suggested that responses to the common (unchanged) 
items might have been influenced by the presence or absence of items related to students’ perceptions of 
certain features of their country of residence (such as the functioning of the political system). In view of 
the changes in item composition and the differences between item parameters across the two cycles we 
decided not to equate this scale with the one established in ICCS 2009.
As for scales new to ICCS 2022, item parameters were calibrated using the IRT (Rasch) partial credit 
3	 Given that only two Latin American countries implemented the ICCS 2022 main survey, four scales from the Latin American 
regional questionnaire were equated through scaling using the same parameters from the previous cycle.
4	 In all but one case, there was only one item exchanged or added. 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
182
model and we used the resulting item parameters to compute weighted likelihood estimates (as described 
previously in this chapter). To transform the scale scores to be on the same reporting metric as established 
in ICCS 2016, we used a two-step transformation process. The first step placed the ICCS 2022 logit scores 
on the ICCS 2016 logit metric (using linear constants denoted A1 and B1), while the second step (using linear 
constants denoted A2 and B2) transformed the latter to the same reporting metric as in ICCS 2016. Both 
transformations were combined into one set of final transformation constants, A and B, as follows:
B = B1 x B2
A = A2 + B2 × A1
To compute the first set of transformation parameters we used the mean/sigma method suggested by Kolen 
and Brennan (2004). For both cycles, we calculated the combined item location and step parameters (δi+τij) 
for each of the common items.5 The transformation parameters in the first step were calculated on the 
means and standard deviations of these combined parameters as:
5	 This is equivalent to using the differences between item location and set parameters (δi+τij). The number of these (combined) 
parameters for each item is equal to the number of steps between item categories.
MNICCS22 and SDICCS22 are the mean and standard deviation of the combined item location and step parameters 
from the calibration of ICCS 2022, respectively, while MNICCS16 and SDICCS16 are the corresponding (combined) 
parameters from the calibration based on ICCS 2016 data.
The second set of transformation parameters, A2 and B2 were based on the scale transformations established 
in the previous cycle for the reporting of ICCS 2022 data. For scales that were established in ICCS 2016, 
the transformation parameters A2 and B2 were calculated as:
B2=10/σθ(ICCS2016)
A2=50-(μθ(ICCS2016) ×B2)
Here, μθ(ICCS2016) is the mean and σθ(ICCS2016) is the standard deviation of logit scores for equally weighted 
countries in ICCS 2016. In case of ICCS 2022 scales that had already been equated to ICCS 2009, the 
transformation parameters A2 and B2 were the same as those used for the transformation of scale scores 
(A and B) that were applied to set the logit scores from ICCS 2016 to the reporting metric established 
in ICCS 2009, where 50 represents the average of equally weighted ICCS 2009 countries and 10 the 
corresponding standard deviation (see Table 11.2 in Schulz & Friedman, 2018).
For all linked scales, the final transformation of ICCS 2022 was computed as follows:
θn' =A + B * θn
Here, θ'n are the scores in the international metric, and θn are the original weighted likelihood estimates in 
their original logit metric. 
Given the (albeit relatively minor) changes in item parameters between the different cycles of ICCS, there is 
a degree of uncertainty associated with equating procedures applied to common sets of items (see Monseur 
& Berezner, 2007). To consider this lack of precision, we estimated the likely magnitude of error based on 
the differences between calibrated item location parameters in 2022 and 2016 using the following formula:

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICCS 2022 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
183
Here, σ2 is the variance of the differences between item location parameters across the two cycles and N 
reflects the number of link items in each scale. The linking error estimates were then transformed to the 
reporting metric (see Table 12.2) and was considered when estimating changes in scale scores between 
cycles (see Chapter 13).

Describing questionnaire scale indices
Questionnaire scales derived from weighted likelihood estimates (logits) present values on a continuum 
with an ICCS 2022 (or, where equated, 2016 or 2009) average of 50 and a standard deviation of 10 (for 
equally weighted national samples). This allows an interpretation of these scores by comparing individual 
scores or group average scores with the ICCS 2022 average, or the respective averages for ICCS 2016 
and 2009 in the case of equated student questionnaire scales. However, the individual scores do not reveal 
anything about the actual item responses and the extent to which respondents endorsed the items used to 
measure the latent variable. The scaling model used to derive individual scores allows the development of 
descriptions of these scales through a mapping of scale scores to (expected) item responses.6
It is possible to describe item characteristics by using the parameters of the partial credit model to provide 
an estimate for each category of its probability of being chosen relative to the probabilities of all higher 
categories. This process is equivalent to computing the odds of scoring higher than a particular category. 
As an example, we plotted cumulative probabilities against scale scores for a fictitious item (Figure 12.1). 
The three vertical lines denote those points on the latent continuum where it becomes more likely to score 
> 0, > 1, or > 2. These locations Γk are Thurstonian thresholds which can be obtained through an iterative 
procedure that calculates summed probabilities for each category at each (decimal) point on the latent 
variable.
6	 This approach was also used in the IEA ICCS 2009 and 2016 surveys (see Schulz & Friedman, 2011, 2018).

Summed probabilities are not identical to expected item scores and have to be understood in terms of the 
probability of scoring at least a particular category.7 Thurstonian thresholds can be used to indicate for each 
item category those points on a scale at which respondents have a 0.5 probability of scoring this category or 
higher. For example, in the case of Likert-type items with the categories strongly disagree (SD), disagree (D), 
agree (A), and strongly agree (SA), we can determine at what point of a scale a respondent has a 50 percent 
likelihood of agreeing with the item.
The item-by-score maps included in ICCS 2022 reports predict the minimum coded score (e.g., 0 = “strongly 
disagree,” 1 = “disagree,” 2 = “agree,” and 3 = “strongly agree”) a respondent would obtain on a Likert-type 
item. For example, we could predict that students with a certain scale score would have a 50 percent 
probability of agreeing (or strongly agreeing) with a particular item (see the example item-by-score map in 
Figure 12.2). For each item, it is thus possible to determine Thurstonian thresholds, namely the points at 
which a minimum item score becomes more likely than any lower score to occur and which determine the 
boundaries between item categories on the item-by-score map.
7	 Other ways of describing item characteristics based on the partial credit model are item characteristic curves, which involve 
plotting the individual category probabilities and the expected item score curves (for a detailed description, see Masters & 
Wright, 1997).

Example of how to interpret the item-by-score map
#1.	
A respondent with score 30 has more than 50% probability to strongly disagree with all three items
#2.	
A respondent with score 40 has more than 50% probability not to strongly disagree with items 1 and 2 
but to strongly disagree with item 3
#3.	
A respondent with score 50 has more than 50% probability to agree with items 1 and to disagree with 
items 2 and 3
#4.	
A respondent with score 60 has more than 50% probability to strongly agree with items 1 and to at 
least agree with items 2 and 3
#5.	
A respondent with score 70 has more than 50% probability to strongly agree with items 1, 2, and 3

This information can also be summarized by calculating the average thresholds across all items in a scale. 
For example, it is possible to do this for the second threshold of a four-point Likert-type scale, which allows 
the prediction of how likely it would be for a respondent with a certain scale score to have responses in the 
two lower or upper categories (on average across items). The ICCS 2022 team used this approach in the 
case of items measuring agreement to distinguish between scale scores for respondents who were most 
likely to agree or disagree with the “average item” used for measuring the respective latent trait.
In the reporting tables for questionnaire scales, we depicted national average scale scores as boxes that 
indicated their mean values plus/minus sampling error and that were set in graphical displays featuring two 
underlying colors. National average scores located in the darker shaded area indicated that, on average 
across items, student responses had resided in the lower item categories (“disagree or strongly disagree,” 
“not at all or not very interested,” “never or rarely”). If these scores were found in the lighter shaded area, 
however, then students’ average item responses would have been in the upper item response categories 
(“agree or strongly agree,” “quite or very interested,” “sometimes or often”).
12.4	
 Scaled indices
Student questionnaire 
National index of students’ socioeconomic background
The bivariate and multivariate analyses presented in the international report (Schulz et al., 2024) include 
data from a composite index reflecting students’ socioeconomic background, which was derived in the same 
way in the previous ICCS cycles in 2016 and 2009.8 The national index of students’ socioeconomic background 
8	 Similar approaches to deriving socio-economic background indices have been applied in the IEA International Computer and 
Information Literacy Study (ICILS) and the OECD Programme for International Student Assessment PISA (see, for example, 
Schulz & Friedman, 2020; OECD, 2023).

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
186
(S_NISB) was derived from the following three indices: highest occupational status of parents (S_HISEI), 
highest educational level of parents (S_HISCED), and the number of books at home (S_HOMLIT). To avoid 
small numbers of cases in these categories, for the S_HISCED index, we collapsed the lowest two categories 
resulting in an indicator variable with four categories: lower-secondary or below, upper-secondary, 
tertiary non-university, and university education. To achieve a more equal distribution of proportions, 
the S_HOMLIT index was also reduced from five to four categories (0 to 10 books; 11 to 25 books; 26 
to 100 books; more than 100 books) collapsing the two highest categories. Previous analyses had shown 
approximately linear associations across these categories with civic knowledge test scores and the other 
indicators of socioeconomic background.
We imputed values for students who had missing data for only one of the three indicators based on a 
regression on the other two variables that had been estimated for students with values on all three variables 
using predicted values plus a random component. This simple imputation procedure was carried out for 
each national sample separately.
After converting the resulting variables including the imputed values into z-standardized variables (with a 
mean of 0 and a standard deviation of 1 for each national dataset), principal component analysis of these 
indicator variables were conducted separately for each weighted national sample. 
The final S_NISB scores consists of factor scores for the first principal component with national averages 
of 0 and national standard deviations of 1. Table 12.3 shows the factor loadings and reliabilities for each 
national sample.

Students’ participation in out-of-school activities
Question 12 of the student questionnaire required students to indicate their level of involvement in a series 
of activities outside of school. Students reported their frequency of undertaking the following activities 
including media use and participation in discussions of political or social issues using the response categories 
“Never or hardly ever,” “Monthly (at least once a month),” “Weekly (at least once a week),” and “Daily or 
almost daily.” Using the same response options, Question 13 was designed to capture students’ engagement 
with digital media. 
Figure 12.3: Confirmatory factor analysis of items measuring students’ participation in out-of-school activities
Two scales were derived from these two item sets. Four of the items in Question 12 were used to derive 
a scale reflecting students’ discussion of political and social issues outside of school (S_POLDISC), while four 
of the items included in Question 13 were used to derive a scale of students’ engagement with digital media 
(S_ENGDM). Higher scale scores on both scales reflect more frequent engagement in each type of these 
activities.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess 
students’ participation in out-of-school activities (Figure 12.3). The model fit was marginally satisfactory 
and the two latent factors (S_POLDISC and S_ENGDM) were highly correlated (r = 0.53). When reviewing 
measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit was poor to 
marginally satisfactory across multiple-group models.

Students’ civic engagement in the community and at school
Question 14 asked students to indicate their participation (“yes, I have done this within the last twelve 
months,” “yes, I have done this but more than a year ago,” or “no, I have never done this”) in different types 
of groups or organizations in the community. Using the same response categories, Question 15 measured 
student reported participation in the six different types of civic-related school activities. 
Five of the items in Question 14 were used to derive a scale reflecting students’ participation in wider 
community groups or organizations (S_COMPART), while seven of the items included in Question 15 were 
used to derive a scale of students’ participation in civic activities at school (S_SCHPART). Higher scale scores 
on both scales reflect more frequent engagement in each type of these activities. Both scales were not 
equated to the previous cycles and treated as new scales for ICCS 2022.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess students’ 
participation in the community and at school (Figure 12.4). The model fit was satisfactory for the pooled 
item set and the two latent factors (S_COMPART and S_SCHPART) were highly correlated (r = 0.51). When 
reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit 
was less satisfactory for more constrained models, suggesting a lack of measurement invariance across 
countries.

Students’ perceptions of classroom climate and civic learning
Question 16 of the student questionnaire required students to report the frequency (“never,” “rarely,” 
“sometimes,” or “often”) of things happening in their classrooms that indicated openness to the discussion 
of political and social issues. Question 17 was designed to capture students’ reports on their learning (“to a 
large extent,” “to a moderate extent,” “to a small extent,” or “not at all”) about different civic issues at school.
Two scales were derived from these two item sets. Six of the items in Question 12 were used to derive 
a scale reflecting students’ perceptions of openness in classroom discussions (S_OPDISC), while nine of the 
items included in Question 17 were used to derive a scale of students’ civic learning (S_CIVLRN). Higher 
scale scores on both scales reflect more openness in classroom climate or a larger extent of reported civic 
learning, and both scales were equated to ICCS 2009 and ICCS 2016, respectively.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess students’ 
perceptions of classroom climate and civic learning (Figure 12.5). The model fit was satisfactory and the 
two latent factors (S_OPDISC and S_CIVLRN) were moderately correlated (r = 0.43). When reviewing 
measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit was less 
satisfactory for more constrained models, suggesting a lack of measurement invariance across countries.

Students’ perceptions of school interactions
Question 18 of the student questionnaire required students to indicate their level of agreement (“strongly 
agree,” “agree,” “disagree,” or “strongly disagree”) with statements about social interactions at their school. 
Using the same response categories, Question 19 asked students about their agreement with statements 
about student participation at their school.
Three scales were derived from these two item sets. Five of the items were used to derive a scale reflecting 
students’ perceptions of student-teacher relations at school (S_STUTREL), while three of the items were used 
to derive a scale of students’ perceptions of student interaction at school (S_INTACT). The six items included in 
Question 19 were used to derive a scale reflecting students’ beliefs about their influence on decision-making at 
school (S_INFDEC). Higher scale scores on both scales reflect higher levels of agreement. While S_STUTREL 
and S_INTACT were equated to ICCS 2009 and ICCS 2016, respectively, S_INFDEC was a new scale for 
ICCS 2022.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a three-dimensional model for these items to assess 
students’ perceptions of school interactions (Figure 12.6). The model fit was satisfactory, and all three latent 
factors were highly correlated with each other. When reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-
group models with different constraints, the model fit was still satisfactory for more constrained models, 
suggesting a relatively high level of measurement invariance across countries.

Students’ perceptions of political systems and institutions
Question 20 of the student questionnaire required students to indicate their level of agreement (“strongly 
agree,” “agree,” “disagree,” or “strongly disagree”) with positive and negative statements about the political 
system in their countries. Question 27 was designed to measure how much trust (“completely,” “quite a lot,” 
“a little,” or “not at all”) students expressed in different institutions or groups in their country. 
Three scales were derived from these two item sets. Four of the items in Question 20 were used to derive a 
scale reflecting students’ satisfaction with the political system (S_SYSSAT), while three of the items included in 
this question were used to derive a scale of students’ critical views of the political system (S_SYSCRT). Six items 
included in Question 27 provided a basis for deriving a scale of students’ trust in civic institutions (S_INTRUST). 
Higher scale scores on these scales reflect more positive or negative views, or higher levels of trust. S_
INTRUST was equated to ICCS 2009.
We used confirmatory factor analyses 
assuming a three-dimensional model 
for these items to assess students’ 
perceptions of political systems and 
institutions (Figure 12.7). The model fit 
was satisfactory for the pooled data set, 
and while S_SYSSAT and S_INTRUST 
were positively correlated (r = 0.69), 
S_SYSCRT had negative correlations of 
-0.21 with both other latent variables. 
When 
reviewing 
measurement 
invariance 
across 
multiple-group 
models with different constraints, the 
model fit remained satisfactory across 
models with slightly poorer fit for the 
most constrained model.

Students’ beliefs about threats to democracy and restrictions in national emergencies
Question 21 of the student questionnaire required students to indicate to which extent they considered 
certain situations as bad for democracy (“very bad,” “quite bad,” “somewhat bad,” or “not bad at all”). Question 
23 of the student questionnaire required students to indicate their level of agreement (“strongly agree,” 
“agree,” “disagree,” or “strongly disagree”) with actions governments may take in time of a national emergency 
(such as the COVID-19 pandemic) in their countries.
Two scales were derived from each of these two item sets. All seven items in Question 21 were used to derive 
a scale reflecting students’ beliefs about threats to democracy (S_DEMTHRT), while the nine items included in 
Question 23 were used to derive a scale of students’ endorsement of restrictions in a national emergency (S_
ENREST). Higher scale scores reflect more concern about threats for democracy (S_DEMTHRT) or higher 
levels of endorsement of restrictions in a national emergency (S_ENREST). Both scales were new to ICCS 
2022.
We used confirmatory factor analyses 
assuming a two-dimensional model 
for these items to assess students’ 
beliefs about threats to democracy and 
restrictions in national emergencies 
(Figure 12.8). The model fit was 
satisfactory for the pooled data set, 
and the two latent factors were weekly 
correlated (r = 0.14). When reviewing 
measurement 
invariance 
across 
multiple-group models with different 
constraints, the model fit was slightly 
less satisfactory for the most restricted 
(scalar) model.

Students’ perceptions of the importance of good citizenship behavior
Question 22 of the student questionnaire required students to indicate how important (“very important,” 
“quite important,” “not very important,” “not at all important”) they considered different behaviors for good 
citizenship. 
Three scales were derived from this item set. Three sets of four items each were used to derive scales 
reflecting students’ beliefs about the importance of conventional citizenship activities (S_CITCON), students’ 
beliefs about the importance of social movement related citizenship activities (S_CITSOC), and students’ beliefs 
about the importance of globally oriented citizenship activities (S_GLOBCIT). Higher scale scores on these 
scales reflect higher levels of importance assigned to these types of citizenship. S_CITCON and S_CITSOC 
were equated to ICCS 2009.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a three-dimensional model for these items to assess 
students’ perceptions of the importance of good citizenship behavior (Figure 12.9). The model fit was 
marginally satisfactory for the pooled data set, and the three latent factors were highly correlated with 
each other, with the highest correlation (r = 0.86) observed between S_CITSOC and S_GLOBCIT. When 
reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit 
remained marginally satisfactory all across models with different levels of constraints.

205
Students’ support for equal rights for immigrants and gender equality
Question 24 of the student questionnaire required students to indicate their level of agreement (“strongly 
agree,” “agree,” “disagree,” or “strongly disagree”) with statements about rights and opportunities for 
immigrants. Using the same response categories, Question 25 asked students to rate their agreement with 
four positive and three negative statements about gender rights. 
Two scales were derived from these two item sets. All five items in Question 24 were used to derive a 
scale reflecting students’ positive attitudes toward immigrants (S_IMMPOS), while six of the items included in 
Question 25 were used to derive a scale of students’ endorsement of gender equality (S_GENEQL). Higher scale 
scores on both scales reflect higher levels of endorsement of equal rights or opportunities for immigrants 
and gender equality. S_GENEQL was equated to ICCS 2009, however, due to changes in the item set and 
because the item set about immigrant rights and opportunities had only been included in the European 
questionnaire in ICCS 2016, S_IMMPOS was not equated to previous cycles.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess students’ 
support for equal rights for immigrants and gender equality (Figure 12.10) Due to the mix of positive and 
negative items, correlated error terms were included to consider the measurement effects of this item set 
format. The model fit was very satisfactory for the pooled item set, and the two latent factors were highly 
correlated (r = 0.62). When reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different 
constraints, the model fit was satisfactory for both configural and scalar models.10 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.83 for S_IMMPOS (ranging from 
0.77 to 0.93) and 0.81 for S_GENEQL (ranging from 0.73 to 0.87) (see Table 12.18). These scales were 
based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.19. 
10	 The scalar model did not converge for this item set and could not be reported.

Students’ endorsement of equal rights for all ethnic groups in society
Question 26O of the student questionnaire was an international option requiring students to indicate 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
208
their level of agreement (“strongly agree,” “agree,” “disagree,” or “strongly disagree”) with statements about 
rights and opportunities for all ethnic groups in their country. These five items were used to derive a scale 
reflecting students’ endorsement of equal rights for all ethnic groups in society (S_ETHGRP). Higher scale scores 
on both scales reflect higher levels of endorsement and the scale was equated with ICCS 2009.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items to assess students’ 
endorsement of equal rights for all ethnic groups in society (Figure 12.11). The model fit was satisfactory for 
the pooled sample. When reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different 
constraints, the model fit remained satisfactory across models with different constraints, indicating a high 
degree of measurement invariance. 
The average reliability (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples for S_ETHRGT was 0.87 for (ranging 
from 0.82 to 0.93) (see Table 12.20). The scale was derived using item parameters shown in Table 12.21.
