Students’ support for environmental protection and their environmental concerns
Question 26 of the student questionnaire asked students about their level of agreement (“strongly agree,” 
“agree,” “disagree,” or “strongly disagree”) with statements about environmental protection, while Question 
28 required students to indicate to which extent they thought of global issues as threats to the world’s 
future (“to a large extent,” “to a moderate extent,” “to a small extent,” or “not at all”).

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
210
Two scales were derived from these two item sets. All five items in Question 26 were used to derive a 
scale reflecting students’ positive attitudes toward environmental protection (S_ATTENV), while four of the 
items included in Question 28 were used to derive a scale of students’ concern about threats to the global 
environment (S_ENVCON). Higher scale scores reflect more positive attitudes for S_ATTENV, and higher 
levels of concern for S_ENVCON.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess students’ 
support for environmental protection and their environmental concerns (Figure 12.12). The model fit 
was satisfactory for the pooled sample and the two latent factors were highly correlated (r = 0.58). When 
reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit 
was slightly poorer for the most constrained (scalar) model.11
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.77 for S_ATTENV (ranging from 
0.59 to 0.85) and 0.78 for S_ENVCON (ranging from 0.69 to 0.85) (see Table 12.22). These scales were both 
new for ICCS 2022 and based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.23.

Students’ citizenship self-efficacy and willingness to participate at school
Question 29 of the student questionnaire required students to indicate how well (“very well,” “fairly well,” 
“not very well,” or “not at all”) they would do different activities related to civic engagement. Questions 
30 asked students to report how likely (“very likely,” “quite likely,” “not very likely,” or “not at all likely”) they 
expected to undertake different civic engagement activities at school. 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
212
Two scales were derived from these two item sets. All seven items in Question 29 were used to derive a 
scale reflecting students’ citizenship self-efficacy (S_CITEFF), while all four items included in Question 30 
were used to derive a scale of students’ willingness to participate in school activities (S_SCACT). Higher scale 
scores reflect higher levels of self-efficacy for S_CITEFF, and higher levels of willingness for S_SCACT. S_
CITEFF was equated to ICCS 2009, while S_SCACT was linked to ICCS 2016, when it was first introduced.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items (Figure 
12.13). The model fit was satisfactory for the pooled data set and the two latent factors were highly 
correlated (r = 0.67). When reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-group models with 
different constraints, the model fit remained marginally satisfactory across models with increasing 
constraints. 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.87 for S_CITEFF (ranging from 
0.80 to 0.92) and 0.79 for S_SCACT (ranging from 0.71 to 0.88) (see Table 12.24). These scales were based 
on the item parameters shown in Table 12.25.

Students’ expected participation in activities to express their opinions
Question 31 of the student questionnaire asked students to indicate whether they would expect taking part 
in different activities to express their opinion in the future (“I would certainly do this,” “I would probably do 
this,” “I would probably not do this,” “I would certainly not do this”). 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
214
Three scales were derived from this item set. Five of the items were used to derive a scale reflecting students’ 
expected participation in legal activities (S_LEGACT), four of the items were used to derive a scale of students’ 
expected participation in environmental protection activities (S_ENVACT), and three items were used to derive 
a scale reflecting students’ expected participation in illegal protest activities (S_ILLACT). Higher scale scores on 
all scales reflect higher levels of expectations for each type of engagement. S_LEGACT and S_ILLACT were 
equated to ICCS 2016.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a three-dimensional model for these items (Figure 12.14). 
The model fit was marginally satisfactory for the pooled sample (according to the RMSEA), and all three 
latent factors were positively correlated, with the strongest correlation observed between S_LEGACT and 
S_ENVACT (r = 0.71), while the weakest correlation was found between S_ILLACT and S_ENVACT (0.29). 
When reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different constraints, the 
model fit was equally marginally satisfactory across models with different constraints. 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.86 for S_LEGACT (ranging from 
0.80 to 0.91), 0.83 for S_ENVACT (ranging from 0.71 to 0.88), and 0.86 for S_ILLACT (ranging from 0.80 to 
0.93) (see Table 12.26). These scales were based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.27.

Students’ expected political participation
Question 32 of the student questionnaire asked students to indicate whether they would expect taking part 
in different types of political participation in the future (“I would certainly do this,” “I would probably do this,” 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
216
“I would probably not do this,” “I would certainly not do this”). 
Two scales were derived from this item set. Three of the items in Question 32 were used to derive a 
scale reflecting students’ expected electoral participation (S_ELECPART), while four further items included 
in Question 32 were used to derive a scale of students’ expected active political participation (S_POLPART). 
Higher scale scores on both scales reflect higher levels of expected participation. Both scales were equated 
to ICCS 2009.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess students’ 
expected political participation (Figure 12.15). The model fit was satisfactory for the pooled data set, and the 
two latent factors were moderately correlated (r = 0.45). When reviewing measurement invariance across 
multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit was poor for more constrained models.12
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.86 for S_ELECPART (ranging 
from 0.79 to 0.92) and 0.86 for S_POLPART (ranging from 0.74 to 0.91) (see Table 12.28). These scales 
were based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.29.

Students’ attitudes toward religious influence in society
Question 35 of the student questionnaire was optional for countries, and it asked students to indicate their 
level of agreement (“strongly agree,” “agree,” “disagree,” or “strongly disagree”) with different statements 
about religion of activities outside of school. Six out of seven items in this question were used to derive a 
scale of students’ positive attitudes toward the influence of religion in society (S_RELINF). Higher scale scores on 
both scales reflect more positive attitudes, and the scale was equated to ICCS 2009.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items (Figure 12.16). 
The model fit was satisfactory for the pooled data set, and the model included a correlated error term for 
two items with similar content. When reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-group models 
with different constraints, the model fit was only marginally satisfactory for more constrained models, 
indicated some lack of measurement invariance across countries. 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
218
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples for this scale were 0.90 (ranging from 
0.87 to 0.93) (see Table 12.30). The scale was based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.31.

European student questionnaire
Students’ European identity and perceptions of learning about Europe at school
Question 1 of the European student questionnaire required students to indicate their level of agreement 
(“strongly agree,” “agree,” “disagree,” or “strongly disagree”) with statements about their European identity. 
In Question 2 of this instrument students reported about the extent that they had learned about European 
issues or topics at school using the response categories “to a large extent,” “to a moderate extent,” “to a small 
extent,” or “not at all.” 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
220
Two scales were derived from these two item sets. Four of the items in Question 1 were used to derive 
a scale reflecting students’ sense of European identity (E_EUIDENT), while five of the items included in 
Question 2 were used to derive a scale of students’ reports on opportunities for learning about Europe at school 
(E_EURLRN). Higher scale scores on E_EUIDENT indicate higher levels of European identity, while on 
E_EULRN they reflect higher levels of having learned about European issues or topics. E_EUIDENT was 
equated to ICCS 2009, while, due to some item modifications from a similar question in the previous survey, 
E_EULRN was treated as a new scale for ICCS 2022.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess students’ 
European identity and learning about Europe (Figure 12.17). The model fit was satisfactory for the pooled 
dataset, and the two latent factors were moderately correlated (r = 0.33). When reviewing measurement 
invariance across multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit was somewhat poorer for 
the most constrained (scalar) model. 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.81 for E_EUIDENT (ranging 
from 0.72 to 0.86) and 0.82 for E_EULRN (ranging from 0.77 to 0.88) (see Table 12.32). These scales were 
based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.33.

learning about Europe at school

Students’ attitudes toward freedom of movement in Europe
Question 3 of the European student questionnaire required students to indicate their level of agreement 
(“strongly agree,” “agree,” “disagree,” or “strongly disagree”) with positively and negatively worded 
statements about freedom of movement in Europe. Two scales were derived from these two item sets. The 
three positively worded items were used to derive a scale reflecting students’ endorsement of freedom of 
movement within Europe (E_FREEMOVE), while the three negatively worded the items were used to derive 
a scale of students’ endorsement of restrictions of movement in Europe (E_RESMIG). Higher scale scores on 
E_FREEMOVE indicate higher levels of endorsement of freedom of movement, while on E_RESMIG they 
reflect higher levels of support for restriction of movement within Europe. E_FREEMOVE was equated to 
ICCS 2016, while, due to some item modifications, E_RESMIG was treated as a new scale for ICCS 2022.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
222
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess students’ 
attitudes toward freedom of movement in Europe (Figure 12.17). The RMSEA fit index indicated a non-
satisfactory model fit for the pooled dataset, while CFI and TLI indicated marginally satisfactory fit. The two 
latent factors were moderately correlated (r = 0.25), suggesting that many students expressed both positive 
attitudes as well as support for restricting freedom of movement. When reviewing measurement invariance 
across multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit was not satisfactory according to the 
RMSEA, and only marginally satisfactory according to the other indices across multiple-group models with 
varying constraints. 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.77 for E_FREEMOVE (ranging 
from 0.69 to 0.82) and 0.68 for E_RESMIG (ranging from 0.56 to 0.78) (see Table 12.34). These scales were 
based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.35.

Students’ support for European cooperation
Question 4 of the European student questionnaire required students to indicate their level of agreement 
(“strongly agree,” “agree,” “disagree,” or “strongly disagree”) with statements about different types of 
cooperation in Europe. Question 5, using the same response categories, asked specifically about agreement 
with environmental cooperation across European countries. 
Two scales were derived from these two item sets. All seven of the items in Question 4 were used to derive 
a scale reflecting students’ support for cooperation among European countries (E_CCOOP), while all five items 
included in Question 5 were used to derive a scale of students’ endorsement of environmental cooperation in 
Europe (E_COOPENV). Higher scale scores on both scales reflect more support for cooperation in Europe. 
Both scales were new for ICCS 2022.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
224
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess students’ 
support for cooperation in Europe (Figure 12.19). The model fit was satisfactory for the pooled sample, and 
the two latent factors were strongly correlated (r = 0.76). When reviewing measurement invariance across 
multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit remained satisfactory across multiple-group 
models with different constraints. 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.78 for E_CCOOP (ranging from 
0.70 to 0.84) and 0.83 for E_COOPENV (ranging from 0.76 to 0.91) (see Table 12.36). These scales were 
based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.37.

Students’ expectations for Europe’s future
Question 7 of the student questionnaire required students to indicate the extent to which they rated 
the likelihood of different possible scenarios for Europe’s future in a series of activities outside of school. 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
226
Students used the response categories “very likely,” “likely,” “unlikely,” and “very unlikely.” 
Two scales were derived from these two item sets. Six of the items were used to derive a scale reflecting 
students’ positive expectations for European future (E_EUFPOS), while seven of the items included in this 
question were used to derive a scale of students’ negative expectations for European future (E_EUFNEG). 
Higher scale scores on both scales reflect more endorsement of either positive or negative scenarios for 
Europe’s future. Both scales were new to ICCS 2022.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess students’ 
expectations for Europe’s future (Figure 12.20). The model fit was marginally satisfactory for the pooled 
sample, and the two latent factors were positively (albeit weakly) correlated (r = 0.20). When reviewing 
measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit remained 
marginally satisfactory across models. 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.70 for E_EUFPOS (ranging from 
0.61 to 0.80) and 0.68 for E_EUFNEG (ranging from 0.54 to 0.77) (see Table 12.38). These scales were 
based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.39.

Students’ expectations for their own future
Question 8 of the student questionnaire required respondents to indicate the extent to which they rated the 
likelihood of different statements about their own future, using the response categories “very likely,” “likely,” 
“unlikely,” and “very unlikely”. All of the five items were used to derive a scale reflecting students’ expectations 
for their own individual future (E_INDFUT), where higher scale scores on both scales reflect more positive 
expectations. The scale was equated to ICCS 2016 to allow comparisons of scale scores across the two last 
cycles.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT

Figure 12.21: Confirmatory factor analysis of items measuring students' expectations for their own future

expectations for their own future (Figure 12.21). The model fit was satisfactory for the pooled sample, and, 
when reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different constraints, the 
model fit remained satisfactory across models. 
The average reliability (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples for E_INDFUT was 0.81 (ranging from 
0.76 to 0.86) (see Table 12.40). The scale was based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.41.

Students’ reports on ethical consumerism and sustainable behaviors
Question 10 of the European student questionnaire asked students how often (“often,” “sometimes,” “rarely,” 
or “never) they or their parents had bought only certain products or refused to buy products for ethical 
reasons. Using the same response categories, Question 11 gathered data about students’ sustainable 
behaviors (such as reducing water use or avoiding plastic packaging). 
Two scales were derived from these two item sets. All six items in Question 10 were used to derive a scale 
reflecting students’ reports on political consumerism behaviors (E_ETHCON), while all eight of the items 
included in Question 11 were used to derive a scale of students’ reports on their sustainable behaviors (E_
SUSBEH). Higher scale scores on both scales reflect more frequent engagement in each type of activities. 
Both scales were new for ICCS 2022.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess 
students' reports on ethical consumerism and sustainable (Figure 12.22). The model fit was only marginally 
satisfactory for the pooled sample, and the two latent factors we highly and positively correlated (r = 0.58). 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
230
When reviewing measurement invariance across multiple-group models with different constraints, the 
model fit remained marginally satisfactory across models. 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.87 for E_ETHCON (ranging 
from 0.78 to 0.92) and 0.84 for E_SUSBEH (ranging from 0.79 to 0.88) (see Table 12.42). These scales were 
based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.43.

Students’ attitudes toward the EU
Question 12 of the European student questionnaire asked students to indicate their level of agreement 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
232
(“strongly agree,” “agree,” “disagree”, or “strongly disagree”) with statements about the European Union. Two 
scales were derived from this item set. Six positively worded items were used to derive a scale reflecting 
students’ positive attitudes toward European Union (E_EUATPOS), while four negatively worded items were 
used to derive a scale of students’ negative attitudes toward European Union (E_EUATNEG). Higher scale 
scores reflect more support for either positive or negative statements toward the EU.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess 
students' attitudes toward the EU (Figure 12.23). The model fit was satisfactory for the pooled sample, and 
the two latent factors were positively correlated (r = 0.51), suggesting that many respondents tended to 
express both positive and negative perceptions of the EU. When reviewing measurement invariance across 
multiple-group models with different constraints, the model fit remained satisfactory across multiple-group 
models with different constraints. 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.82 for E_EUATPOS (ranging 
from 0.71 to 0.87) and 0.68 for E_EUATNEG (ranging from 0.54 to 0.77) (see Table 12.44). These scales 
were based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.45.

Teacher questionnaire
Teachers’ perceptions of teacher participation at school
Question 6 of the teacher questionnaire required teachers to give their perspective of the number of 
teachers that participated in a list of activities at school (“all or nearly all”, “most of them”, “some of them”, or 
“none or hardly any”). One scale was derived from this item set. The six items were used to derive a scale 
reflecting teachers' perception of teacher participation at school (T_TCHPRT). Higher scale scores reflect 
higher numbers of teachers participating.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items to assess teacher’s 
perception of teacher participation at school (Figure 12.24). The model fit was satisfactory for the pooled 
sample.

The average reliability (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples was 0.81 for T_TCHPRT (ranging from 
0.69 to 0.88) (see Table 12.46). The scale was based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.47.

Teachers’ reports of student activities in the community
Question 8 of the teacher questionnaire required teachers to indicate whether they and their students 
participated (“yes” or “no”) in a list of activities in cooperation with external groups/organizations. One scale 
was derived from this item set. The ten items were used to derive a scale reflecting teachers' reports of student 
activities in the community (T_STDCOM). Higher scale scores reflect a higher number of student activities.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items to assess teachers’ 
reports of student activities in the community (Figure 12.26). The model fit was satisfactory for the pooled 
sample.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
238
The average reliability (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples was 0.79 for T_STDCOM (ranging from 
0.72 to 0.86) (see Table 12.50). The scale was based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.51.

Teachers’ perceptions of classroom climate
Question 9 of the teacher questionnaire required teachers to indicate their perception of the classroom 
climate that exists between students (“all or nearly all”, “most of them”, “some of them”, or “none or hardly 
any”). One scale was derived from this item set. The four items were used to derive a scale reflecting teachers' 
perceptions of classroom climate (T_PCCLIM). Higher scale scores reflect a higher classroom climate.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
240
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items to assess teachers’ 
perceptions of classroom climate (Figure 12.27). The RMSEA index indicated poor model fit, while both CFI 
and TLI suggested a close fit. 
The average reliability (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples was 0.88 for T_PCCLIM (ranging from 
0.84 to 0.92) (see Table 12.52). The scale was based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.53.

Question 10 of the teacher questionnaire required teachers to indicate the level of involvement (“to a large 
extent”, “to a moderate extent, “to a small extent” or “not at all”) that their students have in a list of activities.

reports on student involvement in activities (T_STDINV). Higher scale scores reflect a higher level of student 
involvement.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items to assess 
teachers’ reports on student involvement in activities (Figure 12.28). The model fit for the pooled data was 
satisfactory.

Teachers’ opinions about the influence of cultural and ethnic differences on teaching activities
Question 12 of the teacher questionnaire required teachers to indicate their opinion (“strongly agree”, 
“agree”, “disagree” or “strongly disagree”) on the influence of cultural and ethnic differences among students 
with regards to teaching and learning. Two scales were derived from this item set. Three positively worded 
items were used to derive a scale reflecting Teachers' positive opinions about the influence of cultural and ethnic 
differences on teaching activities (T_POSCDIF), while three negatively worded items were used to derive a 
scale of Teachers' negative opinions about the influence of cultural and ethnic differences on teaching activities 
(T_NEGCDIF). Higher scale scores reflect a stronger opinion (either positive or negative) with regards to 
the influence of cultural and ethnic differences on teaching activities

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
246
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess teachers’ 
opinions on influence of cultural and ethnic differences on teaching (Figure 12.30). The model fit for the 
pooled data was acceptable and the two latent factors were slightly negatively correlated (r = -0.09). 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.73 for T_POSCDIF (ranging 
from 0.61 to 0.80) and 0.77 for T_NEGCDIF (ranging from 0.67 to 0.84) (see Table 12.58). These scales 
were based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.59. 
Figure 12.30: Confirmatory factor analysis of items measuring teachers' opinions on the influence of cultural and 
ethnic diversity on teaching activities

Teachers’ opinions about the influence of socioeconomic differences on teaching activities
Question 13 of the teacher questionnaire required teachers to indicate their opinion (“Strongly agree”, “Agree”, 
“Disagree” or “Strongly disagree”) on the influence of socioeconomic differences among students with regards to 
teaching and learning. Two scales were derived from this item set. Three positively worded items were used to 
derive a scale reflecting Teachers' positive opinions about the influence of socioeconomic differences on teaching activities 
(T_POSSDIF), while three negatively worded items were used to derive a scale of Teachers' negative opinions about 
the influence of socioeconomic differences on teaching activities (T_NEGSDIF). Higher scale scores reflect a stronger 
opinion (either positive or negative) with regards to the influence of socioeconomic differences on teaching activities.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess 
teachers’ opinions on the influence of socioeconomic differences on teaching (Figure 12.31). The RMSEA 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
248
index indicated poor model fit while CFI and TLI indicated only marginally acceptable fit. The two latent 
factors were only weakly correlated (r = 0.05). 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.72 for T_POSSDIF (ranging 
from 0.49 to 0.85) and 0.79 for T_NEGSDIF (ranging from 0.70 to 0.86) (see Table 12.60). Both scales were 
derived based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.61.

Teachers’ perceptions of the importance of good citizenship
Question 17 of the teacher questionnaire required teachers to indicate their perception on the importance 
(“very important”, “quite important”, “not very important” or “not important at all”) of certain behaviors with 
regards to their students becoming good adult citizens. Three scales were derived from this item set. Four 
items were used to derive a scale reflecting Teachers' perception of the importance of conventional citizenship 
(T_CITCON), four items were used to derive a scale of Teachers' perception of the importance of social movement 
related citizenship (T_CITSOC), while four items were used to derive a scale of Teachers' perceptions of the 
importance of global citizenship (T_GLOBCIT). Higher scale scores reflect a stronger opinion with regards to the 
importance of certain behaviors leading to students becoming good adult citizens.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a three-dimensional model for these items to assess teachers’ 
perceptions on the importance of good citizenship (Figure 12.33). The model fit for the pooled data was good and 
there were moderate correlations between T_CITCON and T_CITSOC (r = 0.36) as well as between T_CITCON 
and T_GLOBCIT (r = 0.34), while we observed a strong correlation (r = 0.82) between T_CITSOC and T_GLOBCIT.

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
252
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.61 for T_CITCON (ranging from 
0.55 to 0.67), 0.74 for T_CITSOC (ranging from 0.63 to 0.83) and 0.74 for T_GLOBCIT (ranging from 0.66 
to 0.81) (see Table 12.64). These scales were based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.65. 
Figure 12.33: Confirmatory factor analysis of items measuring teachers' perceptions of the importance of good 
citizenship
Pooled sample

Teachers’ reports on civic-related activities in class
Question 20 of the teacher questionnaire required teachers of subjects related to civic and citizenship 
education to indicate how often (“never”, “sometimes”, “often” or “very often”) students take part in a list of 
civic-related activities in class. One scale was derived from this item set. Eight items were used to derive a 
scale reflecting Teachers' reports on civic-related activities in class (T_CIVCLAS). Higher scale scores reflect a 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
254
higher level of civic-related activities taking place.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items to assess teachers’ 
reports on civic-related activities in class (Figure 12.34). The model fit for the pooled data was good for the 
model based on pooled data. 
The average reliability (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples was 0.79 for T_CIVCLAS (ranging from 
0.66 to 0.86) (see Table 12.66). These scales were based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.67

with students.

Teachers’ reports on their preparedness for teaching and student's opportunities to learn CCE topics
Question 22 of the teacher questionnaire required teachers to indicate how prepared (“very well prepared,” 
“quite well prepared,” “not very well prepared,” or “not prepared at all”) they were to teach different 
topics and skills related to civic and citizenship education (CCE). Two scales were derived from this item 
set. Thirteen items were used to derive a scale reflecting Teachers’ preparedness for teaching CCE topics 
and skills (T_PRPCCE) and thirteen items were used to derive a scale reflecting Teachers’ perceptions of 
students’ opportunities to learn about civic topics and skills (T_OPPLRN). Higher scale scores reflect a higher 
level of preparedness for teaching and students’ opportunities to learn topics and skills related to civic and 
citizenship education.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a two-dimensional model for these items to assess teachers’ 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
258
reports on teaching topics related to civic and citizenship (Figure 12.36). The model fit for the pooled sample 
was good and the two latent factors were strongly correlated (r = 0.50). 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.91 for T_PRPCCE (ranging from 
0.82 to 0.95) and 0.93 for (ranging from 0.88 to 0.95) for T_OPPLRN (see Table 12.70). These scales were 
based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.71.

Teachers’ reports on their PD activities
Question 23 of the teacher questionnaire 
required teachers to indicate whether 
they attended (“yes, during pre-service 
training,” “yes, during in-service training,” 
“yes, during both pre- and in-service 
training,” or “no”) any teacher training 
courses addressing a list of topics and 
skills. Two scales were derived from this 
item set. Thirteen items were used to 
derive a scale reflecting teachers’ reports 
on their professional development (PD) 
activities (T_PDACCE) and thirteen items 
were used to derive a scale reflecting 
teachers’ PD activities for teaching methods 
(T_PDATCH). Higher scale scores reflect 
a higher level of attendance for the 
teacher training courses.
We used confirmatory factor analyses 
assuming a two-dimensional model for 
these items to assess teachers’ reports 
on their PD activities (Figure 12.37). The 
model fit for the pooled sample was good 
and the two latent factors were strongly 
correlated (r = 0.71). 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s 
alpha) across national samples were 0.92 
for T_PDACCE (ranging from 0.87 to 
0.96) and 0.83 for T_PDATCH (ranging 
from 0.70 to 0.92) (see Table 12.72). 
These scales were based on the item 
parameters shown in Table 12.73.

of items measuring teachers’ reports on 
their PD activities

School questionnaire
Schools’ principals’ perceptions of teacher participation at school
Question 2 of the school questionnaire required principals to give their perception as to the level (“all 
or nearly all,” “most of them,” “some of them,” or “none or hardly any”) of teacher participation in school 
governance. One scale was derived from this item set. Five items were used to derive a scale reflecting 
school principals' perceptions of teacher participation at school (C_TCPART). Higher scale scores reflect a 
higher level of teacher participation at school.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items to assess schools’ 
principals’ perceptions of teacher participation at school (Figure 12.38). The model fit for the pooled sample 
was satisfactory. 
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples was 0.72 for C_TCPART (ranging from 
0.49 to 0.85) (see Table 12.74). These scales were based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.75.

Schools’ principals’ perceptions of student and parent involvement at school
Question 3 of the school questionnaire required principals to give their perception as to the level (“to a large 
extent,” “to a moderate extent,” “to a small extent,” or “not at all”) of student and parent involvement at school 
with regards to a list of statements. Two scales were derived from this item set. Four items were used to 
derive a scale reflecting perceptions of student involvement at school (C_STDINV), and five items were used to 
derive a scale reflecting perceptions of parent involvement at school (C_PARINV). Higher scale scores reflect a 
higher level of involvement at school.

principals’ perceptions of student and parent involvement at school (Figure 12.39). The model fit for the 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT

pooled sample was acceptable and the two latent factors were strongly correlated (r = 0.78).

0.60 to 0.85) and 0.77 for C_PARINV (ranging from 0.57 to 0.88) (see Table 12.76). These scales were based 
on the item parameters shown in Table 12.77.

Schools’ principals’ perceptions of school activities in the community
Question 8 of the school questionnaire required principals to give their perception as to how many students 
took part (“all or nearly all,” “most of them,” “some of them,” “none or hardly any,” or “Not offered at school”) in

a list of activities in the community. One scale was derived from this item set. Ten items were used to derive a 
scale reflecting school principals perceptions of student activities in the community (C_STDCOM). Higher scale 
scores reflect a higher level of student participation in activities.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items to assess schools’ 
principals’ perceptions of school activities in the community (Figure 12.40). The model fit for the pooled 
sample was acceptable.

Schools’ principals’ reports on environmentally friendly practices at school
Question 10 of the school questionnaire required principals to give their perception as to the extent (“to a large 
extent,” “to a moderate extent,” “to a small extent,” “not at all,” or “Not applicable”) that environment-friendly 
practices are followed at school. One scale was derived from this item set. Nine items were used to derive 
a scale reflecting school principals' perceptions of environment-friendly practices being followed (C_ENPRAC). 
Higher scale scores reflect a higher level of environmentally friendly practices being followed at school.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items to assess schools’ 
principals’ reports on environment friendly practices at school (Figure 12.41). The model fit for the pooled 
sample was acceptable. 
The average reliability (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.73 for C_ENPRAC (ranging from 
0.57 to 0.85) (see Table 12.80). These scales were based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.81.

Schools’ principals’ reports on training activities related to the use of digital technologies
Question 12 of the school questionnaire required principals to state whether their school has undertaken 
training activities relating to the use of digital technologies (“yes, for teachers and students,” “yes, only for 
teachers,” “yes, only for students,” or “no, this is not offered”). One scale was derived from this item set. Six 
items were used to derive a scale reflecting school principals' reports on training activities related to the use of 
digital technologies (C_ACTDIGT). Higher scale scores reflect a higher level of training activities taking place.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a one-dimensional model for these items to assess schools’ 
principals’ reports on training activities related to the use of digital technologies (Figure 12.42). The model 
fit for the pooled sample was good. 
The average reliability (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.73 for C_ACTDIGT (ranging from 
0.55 to 0.86) (see Table 12.82). These scales were based on the item parameters shown in Table 12.83.

Schools’ principals’ perceptions of social tensions in the community
Question 14 of the school questionnaire required principals to state their perception as to whether a list of 
issues are a source of social tensions (“To a large extent,” “To a moderate extent,” “To a small extent,” or “Not at 
all”) within the community where the school is located. Three scales were derived from this item set. Three 
items were used to derive a scale reflecting principal's perceptions of ethnic tensions in the community (C_
COMETN), three items were used to derive a scale reflecting principal's perceptions of poverty-related tension 
in the community (C_COMPOV) and six items were used to derive a scale reflecting principal's perceptions of 
crime-related tension in the community (C_COMCRI). Higher scale scores reflect a higher perception as to the 
source of social tensions.
We used confirmatory factor analyses assuming a three-dimensional model for these items to assess 
schools’ principals’ perceptions of social tensions in the community (Figure 12.44). The model fit for the 
pooled sample was acceptable and the three latent factors were quite highly correlated for C_COMETN 
and C_COMPOV (r = 0.60), C_COMETN and C_COMCRI (r = 0.74), as well as C_COMPOV and C_COMCRI 
(r = 0.62).
The average reliabilities (Cronbach’s alpha) across national samples were 0.77 for C_COMETN (ranging 
from 0.49 to 0.91), 0.83 for and 0.83 for C_COMPOV (ranging from 0.62 to 0.93) and 0.85 for C_COMCRI 
(ranging from 0.71 to 0.94) (see Table 12.86). These scales were based on the item parameters shown in 
Table 12.87.
Figure 12.44: Confirmatory factor analysis of items school principals’ perceptions of social tensions in the 
community
Pooled sample
RMSEA
0.084
CFI
0.97
TLI
0.96

3.50
Latin American student questionnaire
As in previous cycles, ICCS 2022 included a regional questionnaire for participating countries from the 
Latin American region. However, while during the first study phase the study counted on the participation 
of five to six countries, only two countries (Brazil and Colombia) finally implemented the main survey using 
a reduced version of the instrument only including material from the previous cycles. Because data from 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
276
only two countries do not provide sufficient information for cross-country analyses, scaling reviews and 
re-calibrations, the international study team decided to derive only four scales that were derived using 
the same IRT item parameters and transformations as in the previous survey (ICCS 2016). This makes it 
possible to compare results from Colombia for these four scales with those from the previous cycles.
The following scales were constructed for ICCS 2022 and are included in the corresponding databases for 
Brazil and Colombia:
•	
Students' endorsement of authoritarian government practices (L_AUTGOV): This scale was derived using 
seven items included in Question 2 and scores were equated to ICCS 2009.
•	
Students' endorsement of corrupt practices in government (L_ATTCORR): This scale was derived based on 
five items included in Question 3 and scores were equated to ICCS 2009.
•	
Students' acceptance of disobeying the law (L_DISLAW): This scale was derived using ten items included 
in Question 2 and scores were equated to ICCS 2009.
•	
Students' positive attitudes toward homosexuals (L_ATTHS): This scale was derived based on five items 
included in Question 5 and scores were equated to ICCS 2016.
All these scales were constructed based on their respective ICCS 2016 item parameters (Table 12.86), and 
subsequently transformed using the linear transformations displayed in Table 12.87. IRT scores (θn) based 
on the ICCS 2016 item parameters were set on their reporting metric (θ'n) established in ICCS 2009 or 
2016 using the following equation: 
θn' =A+B×θn

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICCS 2022 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
277

As for cognitive data, the delivery of student questionnaires on paper in additional student samples (bridge

previous cycle) allowed an analysis of psychometric item characteristics across the two delivery modes

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICCS 2022 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
279
For all 30 scales we found almost perfect correlations between IRT parameters from computer- and paper-
based calibrations. These findings confirmed a very high level of measurement invariance across delivery 
modes for the parameters of the respective scaling models. This result provided a good basis for using the 
same item parameters (based on the calibration of the combined national core samples) for deriving the 
scales for both paper-based and computer-based student questionnaire data. 
We further reviewed differences in outcomes between average scales scores across bridging study and 
main survey samples in the 11 countries that had data from both delivery modes (see Tables A.1 to A.4 in 
Appendix A). While for most scales and countries we found no statistically significant differences, for some 
we recorded relatively minor differences for the following indices: 
•	
S_CITCON: Students’ beliefs of the importance of conventional citizenship activities tended to be 
somewhat higher among those responding the questionnaire on computer with an average difference 
of more than one score point, these differences were significant in nine out of 11 countries.
•	
S_GENEQL: Students’ endorsement of gender equality was somewhat higher among those completing 
the paper version with an average difference of -0.6 score points and significant differences in four 
countries (Chinese Taipei, Colombia, Latvia, and Norway).
•	
S_CITEFF: Students’ citizenship self-efficacy tended to be rated higher when responding on a computer, 
with an average difference of 1.4 score points and significant differences in seven out of 11 countries.
•	
S_LEGACT: Students’ expected participation in legal activities to express opinions tended to be higher 
when completing the questionnaire on a computer, with an average difference of one score point and 
significant differences in six out of 11 countries.
•	
S_ILLACT: Students were significantly more likely to expect participation in illegal protest when 
responding on computer in seven out of 11 countries with an average difference of 1.3 score points. 
•	
S_POLPART: Students’ expectations to engage in active political participation tended to be higher when 
provided on computer in all but two countries with an average difference of 1.3 score points.
The observed differences in student responses to the underlying questionnaire items of these scales 
suggest that respondents may have reacted differently to the delivery mode in terms of their openness to 
express opinions. Previous research has found that respondents, possibly also due to privacy concerns, may 
express beliefs more openly in computer-based environments than on paper (see, for example, Feigelson 
& Dwight, 2000; Hart & Goldstein, 1985); however, other research has also claimed that computer-based 
questionnaire may slightly increase socially desirable responses (Finegan & Allen, 1994). The findings 
from these comparative analyses should be taken into consideration when interpreting cross-nationally 
comparative results from ICCS 2022 questionnaire scales, especially for those for which we highlighted 
more systematic differences.

THE REPORTING OF ICCS 2022 RESULTS

Chapter 13:
The reporting of ICCS 2022 results
Wolfram Schulz
13.1 Overview
This chapter describes the procedures used for reporting results in the International Civic and Citizenship 
Education Study (ICCS) 2022 publications (Schulz, et al., 2023; Damiani et al., 2024). We first describe 
the replication methodology used to estimate sampling variance and illustrate how we computed the 
imputation variance of the civic knowledge scores. We then provided information about significance tests 
for differences between country and subsample means or percentages, as well as between results from 
ICCS 2022 and the two previous cycles in 2016 and 2009 for countries that participated in this and the 
preceding survey(s).
The chapter also includes descriptions of the multilevel (hierarchical) models used to estimate variation 
in students’ civic knowledge, and the multiple regression analyses developed to explain variation in 
questionnaire scale scores reflecting students’ expected electoral and active political participation. 
13.2 Estimation of sampling variance
ICCS employed two-stage cluster sampling procedures to obtain the student and teacher samples. During 
the first stage, schools were sampled from a sampling frame with a probability proportional to their size (see 
Chapter 6 for further details). During the second stage, intact classrooms were randomly sampled within 
schools. Cluster sampling techniques permit efficient and economic data collections. However, because 
these samples are not simple random samples, it is not appropriate to apply the usual formulae for obtaining 
standard errors reflecting sampling error for population estimates.
Large-scale assessments typically apply replication techniques that provide appropriate tools for the 
estimation of sampling variance on population estimates (Gonzalez & Foy, 2000; Wolter, 1985). As in 
previous cycles (see Schulz, 2011 & 2018), ICCS 2022 data were analyzed using the jackknife repeated 
replication technique (JRR) to compute standard errors for population means, percentages, regression 
coefficients, and any other population statistic. 
Generally, the JRR method for stratified samples requires pairing primary sampling units (PSUs)—in this 
survey, typically school—sinto pseudo-strata. Because assignment of schools to these “sampling zones” 
needs to be consistent with the sampling frame from which they were sampled, it is necessary to construct 
sampling zones within explicit strata. When faced with occurrences of an odd number of schools within 
an explicit stratum or the sampling frame, the ICCS 2022 sampling team randomly divided the remaining 
school into two halves, thereby forming a sampling zone of two “quasi” schools. If such a school consisted of 
2 sampled classrooms, each classroom was treated as a “quasi” school. 
Each of the countries participating in ICCS 2022 had up to 75 sampling zones. In countries with more than 
150 schools, in order to keep the total number to 75, additional zones were collapsed into the first 75 zones. 
In Cyprus, where typically two classrooms were sampled from all schools with target grade students, the 
sampling team defined classrooms as PSUs for 62 schools, while randomly allocating students from the 
remaining schools to different sampling zones. In Malta a census of all schools was conducted, with most 
schools drawn randomly for the core survey while others were allocated to a sub-sample for the bridging 
study. Here, schools were allocated in the usual way resulting in 15 sampling zones for the national core 
school sample. 
Within each of the sampling zones, one half (typically a school or classroom) was randomly assigned a value 
of 2 and the other school a value of 0. Replicated weights were computed for each of the sampling zones. 
This meant that one of the paired schools had a contribution of zero, the second a double contribution, and 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
284
all other schools remained the same. The replicate weights procedure is achieved by multiplying student or 
teacher weights with the jackknife indicators once only within each sampling zone.  
This process results in a weight being added to the data file for each jackknife replicate. Thus, within one 
sampling zone at a time, each element of one PSU receives twice its original weight, and each element of 
the other PSU receives a zero weight. This procedure can be illustrated by a simple example featuring 24 
students from six different schools (A-F) paired into three sampling zones (Table 13.1).

For each country sample, there are 75 replicate weights regardless of the number of sampling zones. In 
countries with fewer sampling zones, the remaining replicate weights are equal to the original sampling 
weight and therefore do not contribute to the sampling variance estimate.
Estimating the sampling variance for a statistic, µ, involves computing it once with the sampling weights for 
the original sample and then with each of the 75 replication weights separately. The sampling variance SVµ 
estimate is computed using the formula:
where µs is the statistic µ estimated for the population through use of the original sampling weights and µi is 
the same statistic estimated by using the weights for the ith of 75 jackknife replicates. The standard error SEµ 
for statistic µ, which reflects the uncertainty of the estimate due to sampling, is computed as:

THE REPORTING OF ICCS 2022 RESULTS
285
The computation of sampling variance using jackknife replication can be obtained for any statistic, including 
means, percentages, standard deviations, correlations, regression coefficients, and mean differences. 
Standard statistical software does not always include procedures for replication techniques. 
For the jackknife replication of ICCS 2022 data, we used tailored SPSS software macros. These results can 
be replicated by using the IEA IDB Analyzer, which is generally recommended as a tool for analyzing IEA 
data.1 Alternatively, analysts can use software packages with inbuilt procedures for replication methods 
such as SAS, STATA, MPLUS, or other more specialized software, such as for example WESVAR® (Westat, 
2007).
13.3 Estimation of imputation variance for civic knowledge scores
When estimating standard errors for test scores reflecting ICCS civic knowledge, it is important to take the 
imputation variance into account (see Chapter 10 for a description of the scaling methodology for ICCS 
2022 test items). Therefore, population statistics for ICCS 2022 civic knowledge scores should always be 
estimated using all five plausible values. While each plausible value constitutes an unbiased estimate of the 
population value, only employing all five plausible values in the database provides a way of estimating the 
uncertainty of measurement in combination with the sampling variance as well as replicating the results 
published in the ICCS 2022 reports.
If θ is the population average of civic knowledge (at the level of the combined sample, individual countries, 
or sub-groups) and μθ

all P plausible values.

13.4 Reporting of differences
In our reporting of ICCS 2022 results, we conducted a variety of significance tests2 for:
•	
Differences in population estimates between countries (pair-wise comparisons);
•	
Differences between a country and the ICCS 2022 average;
•	
Differences in population estimates between subgroups within countries; 
•	
Differences between population estimates in ICCS 2022 and 2016 (estimation of changes over time) 
for countries that participated in both surveys; and
•	
Differences between population estimates in ICCS 2022 and 2009 (estimation of changes over time) 
for countries that participated in both surveys.
In the ICCS 2022 reports, we considered differences between two score averages (or percentages) a and b 
significant (p < 0.05) when the absolute value of the test statistic t was greater than the critical value, 1.96. 
We calculated t by dividing the difference by its standard error, SEdif_ab:

multiple comparisons (see, for example, Streiner, 2015).

error between countries and the ICCS 2022 averages of the questionnaire scales throughout the ICCS 
2022 reports.

The ICCS 2022 international report also included comparisons of student survey results between ICCS 
2016 and the previous cycles in 2016 and 2009. Because the process of equating the test or questionnaire 
scales across the cycles introduced some additional error into the calculation of any test statistic, we added 
an equating error term to the formula for the standard error of the difference between country averages. The 
equating errors were estimated based on the variation of parameters for link items across the ICCS 2022 
and 2016 cycles (see Chapters 11 and 12), and it needs to be acknowledged that these are approximations 
of its true value, which may vary across different points of the corresponding scale.
When testing the difference of a statistic between ICCS 2022 and 2016, we computed the standard error 
of the difference as follows:
Here, µ can be any statistic in units on the equated ICCS scale (mean, percentile, gender difference, but 
not percentages) and SE(ICCS22) and SE(ICCS16) are the respective standard errors of this statistic from the two 
surveys. EqErr2022_2016 denotes the equating error that reflects an estimate of the uncertainty in the link 
between the adjacent surveys, which was equal to 2.6495 score points for the civic knowledge scale and 
ranged from 0.038 to 0.413 score points across the international and regional equated questionnaire scales 
(see Chapter 11 and 12 for the calculation of the respective equating errors). In Chapter 12, the equating 
errors for questionnaire scales are listed according to their respective reporting metrics (see Table 12.2).
For many ICCS 2022 countries, it was also possible to compare results with the first ICCS cycle in 2009. 
When testing the difference of a statistic between the first and third ICCS survey, we computed the standard 
error of the difference as follows:
In this equation, EqErr2022_2016 denotes the equating error estimated for the link between ICCS 2022 and 
ICCS 2016, while EqErr2016_2009 denotes the equating error for the link between ICCS 2016 and ICCS 2009, 
each reflecting the uncertainty in the link between consecutive assessments. For 2016 and 2009 this was 
equal to 3.0861 score points for the civic knowledge scale (see Gebhardt & Schulz, 2018) and ranged from 
0.019 to 0.726 score points across the international and regional equated questionnaire scales (see Schulz 
& Friedman, 2018). Table 13.3 shows the respective equating errors between ICCS 2016 and 2022 as well 
as between 2009 and 2016 for civic knowledge and (international and European) student questionnaire

a random error component with a mean of 0 and a standard deviation equal to the estimated equating error 
(2.6895). Percentages of students at or above each replicate cut-point (ρn) were computed and an equating

13.5 Hierarchical linear modeling
To review which factors were associated with variation in civic knowledge within and across schools within 
participating countries, we estimated hierarchical (or multilevel) linear regression models (Raudenbush 
& Bryk, 2002). In these models students were nested within intact classrooms that had been randomly 
selected within schools. As in the previous international report for ICCS 2016, we used predictor variables 
reflecting students’ personal and social background, the socioeconomic context of homes and schools, civic 
learning outside school, civic learning at school, and the school and community learning context.
A hierarchical regression model with i students nested in j clusters (schools, classrooms) can be estimated as
Here, Yij is the criterion variables, Xn
ij is a vector of student-level variables, with its corresponding vector of 
regression coefficients βij, and Xj
m is a school- (or classroom-) level variable with its corresponding vector of 
regression coefficients βj. U0j is the residual term at the level of the cluster (school or classroom), and εij is the 
student-level residual. Both residual terms are assumed to have a mean of 0 and variance that is normally 
distributed at each level. 
The explained variance in hierarchical linear models has to be estimated for each level separately, with 
the estimate based on a comparison of each prediction model with the baseline (“null”) model (or ANOVA 
model) without any predictor variables. Thus: 
The residual term U0j
null provides an estimate of the variance in Yij between j clusters, and εij
null is an estimate of 
the variance between i students within clusters. The intra-class correlation IC, which reflects the proportion 
of variance between clusters (in our case, schools), can be computed from these estimates as
In order to provide a comparable baseline model for the ICCS multilevel analysis, we estimated the “null” 
model. From this model we excluded students with missing data for any of the analysis variables. We 
computed the explained variance at the school level EVj as
and we computed the explained variance at the student level EVij as
Because multilevel modeling takes the hierarchical structure of the cluster sample into account, we reported 
multilevel standard errors that reflect both sampling and imputation errors. Data were weighted (with 
normalized school-level and [within-school] student-level weights) following a recommended procedure for 
the analysis of IEA data with this particular sampling design (see Rutkowski et al., 2010).  
We used the software package MPlus (Version 7; see Muthén & Muthén, 2012) to estimate all hierarchical 
models. We excluded data from Malta from the analyses due to their low number of only 29 schools. Results 
from Brazil, Denmark and the benchmarking participant Schleswig-Holstein (Germany) were reported 
separately and should be interpreted with caution. 
As is customary when applying multivariate analyses, we observed increases in the proportions of missing 
data when including more variables in the model. For the multilevel analyses of civic knowledge, 93 percent 
(weighted) of students on average across ICCS 2022 countries meeting sample participation requirements 
had valid data for all variables included in the model. In the benchmarking entity North Rhine-Westphalia 
only 84 percent of the weighted sample had valid data for inclusion in the analyses, while in Denmark, where 

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
292
the national sample did not meet IEA sample participation guidelines due to higher levels of non-response, 
this percentage was lowest with 80 percent. 
We calculated the respective unweighted and weighted percentages of students with valid data for all 
variables in the model (Table 13.5).

In most countries, one intact classroom per school was sampled, which did not allow disentangling classroom- 
and school-level variance. In Cyprus, two or more classrooms were sampled in each school; a few other 
countries had smaller numbers of schools where more than one classroom was sampled. These differences 
in sampling design need to be considered when interpreting the results of the multilevel analyses of ICCS 
2022 data, which are presented in Chapter 7 of the international report (Schulz et al., 2024).
13.6 Multiple regression modeling
As in ICCS 2016, the international report for ICCS 2022 also included results from single-level multiple 
regression models to explain variation in the questionnaire scale scores reflecting students’ expected 
electoral and active political participation, respectively (see Chapter 7 in Schulz et al., 2024). Predictor 
variables were student background variables, variables related to information sources, experience with 
civic participation, students’ perceptions of the political system and its institution, and dispositions toward 
civic engagement.
Multiple regression models (see, for example, Pedhazur, 1997) regress a criterion variable Yi on a set of 

THE REPORTING OF ICCS 2022 RESULTS
293
k predictors X1i ... Xki , with α being the intercept, εi the unexplained part of the model (residual), and k the 
number of regression coefficients β:
ICCS 2022 reported the unstandardized regression coefficients and the variance explained by the model 
to show the effects for each predictor as well as the overall explanatory power of the model. We employed 
jackknife replication using tailored SPSS macros to estimate the standard errors for the multiple regression 
model parameters (unstandardized coefficients and estimates of explained variance).
For these analyses, a listwise exclusion of missing responses was applied, that is, only students with valid 
data for all variables were included. Across the participating countries, on average 88 percent of students 
in the sample were included in the analyses of both dependent variables (Table 13.6). National (weighted) 
average percentages of students with valid data for all variables ranged from 81 percent in Bulgaria to 97 or 
98 percent (respectively) in Chinese Taipei. The lowest percentages were recorded for the benchmarking 
participant North Rhine-Westphalia (80%) and Denmark (75%), which had not met IEA sample participation 
standards. 
In order to estimate the unique contribution of each set of predictors to the variance explanation of the 
model, and the proportion of variance explained by more than one set of predictors, we computed different 
linear regression models. For each set of j with m predictor variables, we left one predictor set out of 
the model. The difference in variance explanation for the full model and the model without a certain set 
of predictors showed the unique contribution this factor made with respect to explaining variance in the 
criterion variable. We computed the variance uniquely explained for predictor variable set j (r2
u_ j) as:
Here, rn
2 is the R square for the model and r2
n-m is the R square for the regression model without the m variables 
in predictor block j. 
We then expressed the unique contribution of predictor set j to the explained variance in the predictor 
variable Yi in percentages:
The joint explained variance contribution reflects the proportion of variance explained by more than one of 
k sets of predictors. We computed the proportion of variance explained by more than one set of predictors 
JVCj as:

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT

Appendix B
Characteristics of National Samples
This appendix describes, for each education system participating in ICCS 2022, the population coverage, 
exclusion categories, stratification variables, and any deviations from the general ICCS 2022 sampling 
design.
The same sample of schools was selected for the student survey and the teacher survey. However, the school 
participation status of a school in the student and teacher survey could differ. It was particularly common 
for a school to count as participating in the student survey, but not in the teacher survey; however, the 
reverse scenario was also possible. Finally, this appendix also summarizes sampling outcomes for countries 
participating in the Bridge Study.
B.1. Bulgaria - Core and Bridge Samples
•	
Coverage is 100.
•	
School level exclusions consisted of special needs schools and very small schools.
•	
Within-sample exclusions consist of students being excluded during data collection.
•	
Explicit stratification by school type for a total of three explicit strata.
•	
Implicit stratification by location for a total of three implicit strata.
•	
Sampled one classroom per school.
•	
Small schools sampled with equal probabilities.

B.2. Brazil – Core Sample
•	
Coverage is 100.
•	
School level exclusions consisted of special needs schools, very small schools, indigenous schools, and 
inaccessible schools.
•	
Within-sample exclusions consist of students being excluded during data collection. 
•	
Explicit stratification by region and school type for a total of 10 explicit strata.
•	
Implicit stratification by SES, location 1 and location 2 for a total of 16 implicit strata.
•	
Sampled one classroom per school and two classrooms in schools with more than 100 students.
•	
Small schools sampled with equal probabilities.

B.3. Colombia - Core and Bridge Samples
•	
Coverage is 100 percent.
•	
School level exclusions consisted of special needs schools, very small schools, and foreign language 
schools.
•	
Within-sample exclusions consist of students being excluded during data collection. 
•	
Explicit stratification by school type and urbanization, and number of classes for a total of six explicit 
strata.
•	
Implicit stratification by gender and administrative unit for a total of 417 implicit strata.
•	
Sampled one classroom per school.
•	
Small schools sampled with equal probabilities.

B.4. Chinese Taipei - Core and Bridge Samples
•	
Coverage is 100 percent.
•	
School level exclusions consisted of special needs schools, very small schools, pupil referral units, and 
international schools.
•	
Within-sample exclusions consist of students being excluded during data collection.
•	
Explicit stratification by number of classes, region, and school type for a total of 10 explicit strata.
•	
Implicit stratification by urbanization for a total of four implicit strata
•	
Sampled one classroom per school.
•	
Small schools sampled with equal probabilities.

B.5. Croatia – Core Sample
•	
Coverage is 100 percent.
•	
School level exclusions consisted of special needs schools, very small schools, minority schools, and 
private schools.
•	
Within-sample exclusions consist of students being excluded during data collection
•	
Explicit stratification by region for a total of six explicit strata.
•	
Implicit stratification by county for a total of 21 implicit strata.
•	
Sampled one classroom per school and two classrooms in schools with more than 90 students.
•	
Small schools sampled with equal probabilities.

APPENDIX B

B.6. Cyprus – Core Sample
•	
Coverage is 100 percent.
•	
School level exclusions consisted of special needs schools, very small schools, schools in the Turkish 
occupied area, and language of instruction other than Greek or English.
•	
Within-sample exclusions consist of students being excluded during data collection. 
•	
Explicit stratification by type of school and curriculum, and district for a total of five explicit strata.
•	
Implicit stratification by urbanization and school type for a total of four implicit strata.
•	
Sampled one classroom per school and two classrooms in schools with more than 70 students.
•	
All schools are sampled with certainty.

•	
Coverage is 100 percent.

•	
No Explicit stratification.

•

•

•

inaccessible schools, private schools without a contract, and Mayotte schools.
•	
Within-sample exclusions consist of students being excluded during data collection. 
•	
Explicit stratification by type of school and priority level for a total of three explicit strata.
•	
No Implicit stratification.
•	
Sampled one classroom per school.
•	
Small schools sampled with equal probabilities.

APPENDIX B

B.10. Italy - Core and Bridge Samples
•	
Coverage is 100 percent.
•	
School level exclusions consisted of special needs schools, very small schools, students taught in 
Slovenian, students taught in Ladin, and private schools.
•	
Within-sample exclusions consist of students being excluded during data collection. 
•	
Explicit stratification by geographic area for a total of five explicit strata.
•	
Implicit stratification by funding for a total of two implicit strata.
•

APPENDIX D
351

ICCS 2022 TECHNICAL REPORT
352
IEA’s International Civic and Citizenship Education Study (ICCS) investigates the ways in which 
young people are prepared to undertake their roles as citizens in a range of countries in the third 
decade of the 21st century. ICCS 2022 is the third cycle of a study initiated in 2009 and collected 
data in 24 countries and benchmarking participants in Europe, Latin America, and Asia. For the 
first time, ICCS 2022 also included computer-based delivery, which was implemented in two 
thirds of the participating education systems.  
This technical report follows the publication of several international and European reports that 
presented the results of ICCS 2022. It comprises detailed information on the development of 
the instruments including their translation and translation verification, sampling design and 
implementation, the implementation of computer-based delivery system, field operations 
and quality control of data collection, data management, sampling weights and participation 
rates, and the scaling, analysis and reporting of ICCS 2022 data. The technical report enables 
researchers to evaluate published reports and articles based on data from this study and, used 
in conjunction with the ICCS 2022 User Guide for the International Database, will provide 
guidance for their own analyses.  
Over the past 50 years, IEA has conducted comparative research studies in a range of domains 
focusing on educational policies, practices, and outcomes in many countries around the world. 
The association conducted its first survey of civic education in 1971. The rich and robust 
comparative ICCS 2022 database will allow participating education systems to evaluate the 
strength of educational policies, from a comparative perspective, and to measure progress 
in achieving critical social objectives of their educational policy agenda including critical 
components of the United Nations’ 2030 agenda for sustainable development.
